--- Page 4 ---
<meta>This page follows the title page of the 1645 Amsterdam edition of Jan Amos Comenius's "Universal Wisdom Outline" (Pansophiae Diatyposis). Having established the work's title and its prestigious printer, Elzevir, this page serves as a formal address from the printer to the reader, explaining why this reprint was necessary due to high demand and the scarcity of earlier editions.</meta>

## TO THE KIND READER
### THE PRINTER <note>original: "Typographus." In the 17th century, the printer often acted as the publisher and editor, taking personal responsibility for the distribution and quality of the text.</note>
->Greetings<- <note>original: "S.," an abbreviation for "Salutem," a standard Latin greeting wishing the reader health and well-being.</note>

**Since the rarity of copies of this Outline** <note>original: "Diatyposeos," a term derived from Greek meaning a formal description or sketch.</note> **has sparked a desire in many to see it, and since there were those who—stirred even by its reputation—were demanding that a copy be made available to them; I have decided, with the Author’s** <note>original: "Auctore," referring to Jan Amos Comenius.</note> **consent, to reprint this little book for the sake of those who love these studies. My hope is that the reasoning and method of the Universal Wisdom** <note>original: "Pansophici," referring to Comenius’s "Pansophia" project to unify all human knowledge.</note> **proposal may be more correctly understood, and that the importance of this most noble subject itself might urge others to lend their own studies and efforts to this cause. Therefore, Reader, make use of these pages and support the Author’s endeavors as much as you are able.**

<note>The vocabulary listed at the bottom of the original page includes: Diatyposeos (Outline/Sketch), Typographus (Printer), Pansophici (Universal Wisdom), and Auctor (Author).</note>

<summary>A preface from the printer (Elzevir) to the reader explaining that this edition was published due to high public demand and the scarcity of previous copies of Comenius's work. It invites the reader to support Comenius's ambitious project to unify all human knowledge.</summary>
<keywords>Jan Amos Comenius, Elzevir, printing history, Pansophia, Universal Wisdom, Diatyposis, 17th-century publishing</keywords>

--- Page 5 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the printer's preface into the main text of Jan Amos Comenius's work. The author begins by using the metaphor of architecture to explain his project: the construction of a "Temple of Wisdom." He argues that just as an architect needs a blueprint to ensure a building is harmonious and cost-effective, he must provide a "Delineatio" (outline or sketch) of his universal knowledge system before attempting to build the whole structure.</meta>

our mind, because it is carried by its own
nature into the future, is delighted by the
foresight of things to come), has an
extraordinary use in preventing the

For once a likeness or idea of the future
work has been set up, it is easy to see
whether the fullness of the Whole, the

expenses can be undertaken, while from such a <term>Sketch</term> <note>original: "Sciagraphia." In early modern architecture, this term referred to a profile or sectional drawing, but Comenius uses it here as a general term for a preliminary outline.</note> the length, width, and height—

Architects usually conceive the form of

and finally, most fully, as all things will
hold together through all **Dimensions**,
inside and out.

<page-num>7</page-num>

# THE TEMPLE OF WISDOM
## THE GROUND PLAN <term>Ground Plan <note>original: "Ichnographia." From the Greek roots for "footprint" and "writing," this architectural term refers to a horizontal section or floor plan showing the proportions of a building.</note></term>

### *Expressed in*
### *aphorisms.*

->I.<-

We call the Temple of Wisdom a **BOOK**, in which all things that ever are, will be, or have been are offered for contemplation to Mankind—as a Creature made to contemplate the Works of its Creator. It is offered with that purpose, and with the means and order arranged toward that end, so that by the help of all those things, he may be led and guided to Him, from whom, through whom, and in whom all things exist: **GOD**, and to eternal **BLESSEDNESS** in Him.

II. For this, ultimately, is what it means **TO BE WISE**: *to understand and to look toward one’s final end* <note>original: "intelligere & providere novissima sua." While "novissima" can mean "the newest things," in this theological context it refers to the "Last Things"—the ultimate destiny and purpose of a human life.</note> (as **God** speaks in Deuteronomy 32:29): lest Mankind wander from its goal, Blessedness, for the enjoyment of which with its founder <note>original: "conditore." Referring to God as the builder or architect of humanity.</note> it was created:

<sig>A 4</sig>
<meta>catchword: whom</meta>

<summary>The author defines the "Temple of Wisdom" as a symbolic book of all existence, designed to lead humanity to its ultimate goal of eternal happiness with God. This section establishes the theological and philosophical foundation for the rest of the plan, identifying wisdom as the understanding of one's final purpose.</summary>
<keywords>Temple of Wisdom, Ground Plan, Aphorisms, Book of Nature, Creator, Blessedness, Wisdom, Deuteronomy, Final End, Mankind</keywords>

--- Page 10 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Ground Plan" of the Temple of Wisdom. It moves from defining the goal of wisdom (eternal blessedness) to the necessity of awakening humanity from the "stupor" of sin. It introduces the metaphor of light—specifically that knowledge should be shared and reflected among all people to better reveal the "Father of Lights."</meta>

<header>UNIVERSAL WISDOM <note>original: "PANSOPHIAE." This refers to the author’s project of "Pansophia" or All-Wisdom—a system to harmonize all human knowledge with divine truth.</note></header>

...just as that natural desire indicates to him, by which **every Human being** wants it to **go well** for himself, and indeed **without end**. For he dreads death and destruction, but desires a life as pleasant as possible for himself, and therefore flees from all adversity.

III. But because mortals have become so brutish <term>brutish <note>original: "obbrutuerunt." To become like a brute beast, losing the rational faculty that distinguishes humans from animals.</note></term> through an atrocious fall into sin <term>sin <note>original: "peccatum."</note></term>, and through a depravity continued through ages and always increasing, they understand neither the **divine plan** <term>divine plan <note>original: "divinum consilium."</note></term> for them as creatures, nor those desires of their nature—much less the Ways to a blessed end—nor do they even care to understand. They must be awakened from their stupor by all ways and means that can ever be devised.

IV. For we are all bound to be ministers of Divine goodness, and as much **light** <term>light <note>original: "lumen." Here used to mean intellectual or spiritual illumination given to an individual.</note></term> as anyone has received from God, they should desire to pour back just as much into others. Thus, with the rays multiplied and variously reflected, refracted, and tempered among one another, the **light** <term>light <note>original: "lux." Generally used here to describe the collective, objective clarity of truth.</note></term> may be increased in minds for seeing and finding the **Father of lights** <note>original: "Patrem luminum." A title for God derived from the New Testament (James 1:17).</note>, and for walking in His light.

<meta>catchword: V. God</meta>

<summary>The author argues that while all humans naturally desire eternal happiness, sin has clouded their minds; therefore, those who possess "light" or knowledge must share it to awaken others and collectively find God.</summary>
<keywords>human nature, desire for immortality, sin, depravity, divine plan, light, sharing knowledge, Father of Lights, spiritual awakening</keywords>

--- Page 11 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Diatyposis" or Ground Plan, expanding on the theological and natural reasons why knowledge must be shared. It moves from the individual's duty to the universal interconnectedness of all created things, arguing that God works through intermediate causes rather than direct intervention alone.</meta>

->## GROUND PLAN <term>Ground Plan <note>original: "DIATYPOSIS." A Greek-derived term often used in 17th-century literature to describe a structural outline, sketch, or architectural blueprint of an idea.</note></term><-

V. For although God himself sustains the **World** created by him by his own **Power**, illuminates it with his own light, and rules it by his own providence: yet because he acts ordinarily through **ordinary means**, it happens that by his arrangement some **Creatures** are formed, reformed, cherished, and directed toward their ends by others.

VI. The entire **choir of Creatures** <note>original: "Creaturarum chorus." A poetic image suggesting that all of creation works together in harmony like a musical ensemble.</note> and all the works of **Nature** bear witness to this, joined together in such a tight order that nothing among them all is or becomes by itself, for its own sake, or through itself; but each one depends on another, serves another, and is sustained by another. Therefore, indeed, he distributed various powers to various **Creatures**, so that each one (in that part where it is what it is, and acts as it acts) representing the duties of its **Creator**, might serve neighboring Creatures, and through this, the preservation of the **Universe** itself.

VII. Since, therefore, in the human race also, God has until now trained some through others for the gradual multiplication of the **light** of the knowledge of himself and of his works; but now the **Last Times** <term>Last Times <note>original: "Ultima Mundi tempora." Many 17th-century scholars believed they were living in the final age of the world, which motivated them to spread knowledge and reform society before the end.</note></term> of the world seem to be at hand, co-

<sig>A 5</sig>
<meta>catchword: gitan- [likely completing "cogitandem" or similar, referring to thinking or planning]</meta>

<summary>This page explains the interconnectedness of creation, arguing that God governs the world through intermediate means where creatures serve one another. It concludes that humans have a duty to share the "light of knowledge" with one another, especially as the author believes the world is entering its final era.</summary>
<keywords>Providence, Interconnectedness, Choir of Creatures, Natural Order, Last Times, Universal Service, Light of Knowledge</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Diatyposis" or Ground Plan. The previous page discussed how God works through "ordinary means" and how humans in these "Last Times" must help one another towards the light of knowledge. This page moves into the specific methodology for achieving that light through a new system of learning.</meta>

# OF UNIVERSAL WISDOM <note>original: "PANSOPHIÆ." From the Greek roots *pan* (all) and *sophia* (wisdom). This was the author's term for a unified system of all human knowledge integrated with divine revelation.</note>

...it must be considered how we may advance ourselves even to the final level of knowledge (at least that which can be hoped for in this earthly Life). Namely, that we might *make our knowledge* (1) *completely UNIVERSAL*, disfigured by no harmful gaps; (2) *completely TRUE*, contaminated by no mixture of the false and the vain; (3) *completely easy*, hindered by no thorns or rough paths of obscurity and ambiguity, but flowing spontaneously into our minds.

VIII. If it is granted that we obtain these three things, our human Knowledge <note>original: "Scientia." In this period, "science" referred to any structured body of certain knowledge, not just the natural sciences.</note> will be in some way perfect, as a beautifully shining image of divine Omniscience <note>This refers to the "Imago Dei" or Image of God; the author believes that by perfecting our knowledge, we restore the divine likeness lost in the Fall of Man.</note> within us. It will dispose us so that—striving along the same paths, through the same means, toward the same ends—we may unlearn our usual discords and the vanities of any side-matters <term>side-matters <note>original: "parerga." A Greek term for secondary works or distractions that lead one away from the main path of study.</note></term>, knowing how to think, act, and speak only those things which are best.

IX. We propose, therefore, that a *Book* must be composed which contains EVERYTHING necessary for a human being for this and the future life: for knowing and doing, for believing and hoping.

<summary>This page outlines the three essential qualities of perfect knowledge—universality, truth, and ease—and proposes the creation of a "Universal Book" to encompass all human necessity.</summary>
<keywords>Pansophia, Universal Knowledge, Divine Omniscience, Truth, Education Reform, The Universal Book, Imago Dei</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Diatyposis" or Ground Plan for a book of Universal Wisdom. The previous page (page 10) ended by suggesting that a single Book should be composed to contain everything necessary for human knowledge. This page explains the methodology of that book: it must map out what we know and don't know, using a structured "ladder" to lead the mind from basic facts to divine truths.</meta>

->### THE PLAN<-

...neatly: namely, an exact account <note>original: "enarrationem"</note> of those things which we already know, along with an accurate index everywhere of those things we do not know: whether because knowing them is simply not granted to us, or because they remain to be investigated. And indeed, all these things are for that end, and by that Method, so that from the first and lowest things, through the middle things, to the last and highest, the minds of men—as if by a certain skillfully made ladder <note>This "skillful ladder" refers to the *Scala Naturae* or Great Chain of Being. The author suggests that learning should be a step-by-step ascent from the physical world to the spiritual realm.</note>—may be raised up even to Him, from whom, through whom, and for whom all things exist <note>This is a paraphrasing of the biblical verse Romans 11:36, indicating that the ultimate goal of all science and study is the contemplation of God.</note>.

X. But so that the same book may teach all those things **TRULY**, and demonstrate them solidly, let it be clearly seen that these are not the mockeries of phantasms <term>phantasms <note>original: "phantasmatum." In the philosophy of this period, these were deceptive mental images or mere figments of the imagination that lacked any basis in physical reality.</note></term>, but the unshakable foundations of things themselves: so that we may know that we *know*, and not merely hold an opinion. Therefore, this Book will contain nothing that might either offend our agreement (as do things which are openly false, empty, unproven, or improbable) or merely tickle the mind and invite agreement (as do things which are merely likely), but rather it shall *compel* the mind. This is the power inherent in the invincible and always victorious Truth, provided it is skillfully brought into the light.

A 6
<meta>catchword: XI.</meta>

<summary>This page describes a systematic "ladder" of knowledge intended to lead the human mind from basic observations to divine understanding. It emphasizes that this new system of learning must be based on "unshakable foundations" and absolute truth rather than mere opinion or probability.</summary>

<keywords>ladder of knowledge, certain knowledge, phantasms, scientific method, divine truth, logic, foundations of learning</keywords>

--- Page 14 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Diatyposis" or Ground Plan for a book of Universal Wisdom. On the previous page, the author argued that this book must be built on "unshakable foundations" rather than mere opinions or mental phantasms, leading the reader from basic physical facts to divine truths.</meta>

# OF UNIVERSAL WISDOM

XI. Furthermore, it is necessary that this Book be conceived using a Method <note>In this period, "Method" referred to a systematic, pedagogical arrangement of information designed to make learning easier and more natural.</note> precisely tailored to human minds, so that whoever reads it may understand it immediately, led everywhere by a clear light. In this way, when translated even into the common languages of the Nations, it may become as common in use as God himself, the World, and our own common perception <term>common perception <note>original: "Sensus communis." This refers to the shared faculty of the mind that coordinates all five senses to create a coherent experience of reality.</note></term>. Indeed, this Book is nothing other than a true and genuine copy <note>original: "apographum"</note> of those things which the one God has revealed until now through His Works <note>The physical world of nature</note>, His Voice <note>Divine revelation or scripture</note>, and the innate Notions <note>original: "Notionibus." These are the fundamental ideas or "seeds of knowledge" thought to be imprinted on every human soul by God.</note> impressed upon every man's soul. It shall be a true and common torch and guide for us all toward the life to come, and a true standard and directory for everything we must do in this life.

XII. The *matter, form, creator, and purpose* <note>The author is using the four Aristotelian "causes" (material, formal, efficient, and final) to argue why this book must be called "Pansophia."</note> of such a book argue that it should be called **PAN-SOPHIA** <note>From the Greek roots meaning "All-Wisdom" or "Universal Wisdom."</note>.

XIII. *For its matter* <note>meaning its subject or substance</note> *will be the whole* <note>original Greek: τὸ πᾶν (*to pan*)</note>, *the Universe as it exists, which a wise man must not ignore. It will include whatever of good, beauty, or utility is found anywhere in Books; or even those things which do not yet exist in writing, and yet can be had...*

<summary>This page defines "Pansophia" as a universal book of wisdom that mirrors God's revelation in nature, scripture, and the human mind. It argues that such a book must be accessible to all people in their own languages as a practical guide for this life and the next.</summary>

<margin>A 7</margin>

...can be (including newly discovered inventions and those yet to be discovered), we wish to be gathered here: so that whenever a mention or thought of any matter arises, sufficient information regarding it may be found here.

XIV. *The Form of this Book must be universal harmony* <note>original Greek: "παναρμονία" (panharmonia)</note>, *that is, Universal Harmony*, or the consonance and consensus of all things with all things. For observations regarding things, however useful, which have been handed down here and there, are not to be heaped together here in a mass; rather, the intimate nature of the things themselves must be uncovered, through such craftsmanship that, just as in God the author of things <note>original Latin: "Deo rerum authore"</note>, and in His Works and His Words, there is no dissonance; so in our concepts, no discrepancy may be left remaining: namely, by finding the central points <note>original Latin: "mediis rerum centris"</note> of things, in which even extremes and opposites may meet and cease their conflict. By this means, it is hoped that whatever different men think and do in different ways (so long as it is done reasonably), even if they are mutually ignorant of one another, fail to understand each other, or even oppose one another, they may here come into agreement and harmony; once the streams of all thoughts and actions have been led back to their true and pu- <note>The text continues on the next page with "pure."</note>

# PANSOPHIA <note>original Latin: "PANSOPHIÆ"</note>

XVII. Regarding the composition of such a Pansophic **BOOK**, so that these thoughts may be seriously undertaken, the very order of *Divine Providence* <note>The belief that God intervenes in the world and guides human history toward a specific end.</note> now leads us there; the *Necessities of the human race* compel us; and *present opportunities* invite us. To have explained these three things will be to have laid the foundations of the Pansophic Temple.

<margin>HH</margin>
XVIII. **GOD**, because He is wise, acts in an orderly manner in all things, each in its own time; as the wise *Solomon* speaks: and because He is good, He acts well. This is the process of an ordered Good: that it grows as it goes, progressing from Good to Better, from Lesser to Greater, until it reaches that than which nothing greater or better can be given—that is, toward Perfection. All the examples of His works testify to this process of God's wisdom.

XIX. Thus, although He could have produced the World in a single moment, He nevertheless willed to employ a stretch of time, and a gradual succession of forms impressing themselves upon matter: His works

XX. He subjected the **human race** to this same condition in all things. ### OUTLINE

...about perpetual confusions, the complaints of wise men in every age are well known <margin>(a)</margin>: and no one is ignorant of them, unless they are either a stranger in the World, such as newly entered infants; or a block and a trunk <note>A Latin idiom for someone who is as senseless and unfeeling as a piece of wood.</note>, such as the dullards who pay no attention to what is happening, much less care about it. <margin>(b)</margin> However, the source of these confusions is that nearly all mortals (even those who seem wise to themselves and to the world) do everything—or certainly their most important affairs—not by counsel and reason, which they are ignorant of, but by chance <note>original: "temerè," meaning rashly or without a plan.</note>. In this sense, **Augustine** said that most men are fools (*Book 1 on Free Will, chapter 9*). And **Cicero**: *Nothing is so very common as to have no wisdom.* Also: *I believe it is more common for a mule to have given birth than for a man to have been wise.* <note>Cicero is using a proverbial impossibility; since mules are sterile, a mule giving birth is a miracle. He argues that finding a truly wise man is even more unlikely.</note> Since, therefore, **Wisdom**, the **governess of things**, abandons men, what wonder is it that everything is conducted immoderately and through turmoil? And that our affairs are not carried toward their proper ends, but rush beyond their boundaries, and throw us down into various precipices? The salvation of the human race, therefore, would be for the true **Ends** of things to be uncovered, along with the certain **Means** leading to those ends, and the legitimate **Methods** <note>original: "Modos"</note> of those Means...

# OF UNIVERSAL WISDOM

XXIX. For because there are many, and in a way infinite, things that Men have as objects of attention in this life: both outside themselves—things natural and man-made, moral and spiritual, good and bad; as well as inside themselves—inclinations, desires, and various affections; and finally, alongside themselves—the opinions, interests, efforts, and actions of other men, which are widely diverse: it cannot happen otherwise than that they are variously distracted and confused by all these things. Thus, they do not do what most needed to be done, but as anyone happens to stumble here or there, so they allow themselves to be entangled. Thus, each person may see clearly for themselves and thoroughly understand what ought to come first or last, what should be more or less attended to, loved, doubted, or acted upon.

This is not possible without a universal pre-knowledge and recognition of things. For all things that exist are so woven together by divine craftsmanship (which **Pansophia** <note>Universal Wisdom: Comenius’s project to harmonize all knowledge into one system.</note> will make clear) that each thing exists not so much for its own sake as for the sake of others. From this, it happens that all things mutually cling together and serve one another, and illuminate each other—not only those things that are similar or different, but even those that are opposites.

This is evident in the body of any living creature, where all the members—the greatest and the smallest, the highest and the lowest, the first and the last—serve one another in turn; so that, if you take one away...

->DIATYPOSIS <note>original: "Diatyposis." A Greek term meaning a sketch, outline, or vivid description. Here, it serves as a section heading for Comenius’s systematic outline of universal wisdom.</note><-

And a little later <note>Continuing the citation from Francisco Sánchez, a Portuguese skeptic philosopher who influenced the development of the scientific method.</note>: *You will recognize this by the very familiar example of a common Clock <term>Horologii</term> <note>The mechanical clock was a favorite 17th-century metaphor for the universe, illustrating a complex system where every gear depends on the others.</note>. For if you wish to know how it strikes the hours, you must inspect all the wheels from the first to the last: what moves the first, and how this moves another, and this one others, until the last, etc. The same must be thought regarding the Great World of Things <note>The Macrocosm, or the universe at large.</note>: in which you find nothing that does not move and is not moved, does not change and is not changed, does not act and is not acted upon.*

Accordingly, by a healthy plan, Pansophia <note>Universal Wisdom: Comenius's proposed encyclopedic system of all knowledge.</note> takes up all things in the universe to be considered, so that it may be most clearly evident how smaller things are subordinate to larger, and larger to greatest; prior things to posterior, and posterior to the very last; infinite things to the finite, and finite things to the One <note>Referring to God, the source of all unity in Neoplatonic and Christian thought.</note>: that is, how all Visible things <term>Visibilia</term> are subordinate to Man, temporal things to eternity <term>æternitas</term>, and created things to the Creator <term>Creator</term>. Thus, nothing of all things is left behind which is not compelled to serve our ultimate end. By this...

### OF UNIVERSAL WISDOM <note>original: "PANSOPHIAE"</note>

For surely we shall recognize by reason how beneficial the counsel of the Ecclesiastic <note>Referring to the Book of Sirach, also known as Ecclesiasticus, a collection of ethical teachings from the 2nd century BCE.</note> is: 

> *In a great matter as well as a small, ignore not even one.* (Chapter 5, verse 18)

XXX. Moreover, no less care and concern—indeed, rather more—ought to be given to **Truth** than to **Universality**. For, as Lactantius <note>Lucius Caelius Firmianus Lactantius (c. 250 – c. 325 AD), an early Christian author known as the "Christian Cicero" for his eloquent Latin.</note> says: *To know the Truth belongs to divine wisdom*; there is no use for fictions, except to deceive minds and lead men into trackless wastes. For this reason, most thoughts of mortals are smoky, slippery, crooked, vain, and useless for work, because we generally settle for things that are **merely probable** <term>verisimilibus</term> <note>Literally "truth-like." In 17th-century philosophy, this referred to ideas that seem plausible but lack the certainty of demonstrated fact.</note> instead of the truth itself. Dissolving into mere opinions, we possess little certain knowledge. From this it follows that it is better not to know than to know wrongly: for **Ignorance** is less harmful than **Error**. Just as a neutral state of health is more tolerable than a bad one; and unrefined manners are better than those polished for cunning and malice. Because, therefore, we must seek (unless we wish to be deceived and fade away) not things that merely appear true and good, but solid things, which make men truly wise, good, and blessed: oh 

<meta>catchword: how [quàm]</meta>

<summary>This page argues that while universal knowledge is important, the pursuit of absolute Truth is even more vital, as holding false opinions is more dangerous than simple ignorance. Comenius advocates for "solid" knowledge over mere probability to ensure human virtue and happiness.</summary>

# AN OUTLINE <note>original: "DIATYPOSIS"</note>

->B 2<-

how much it is to be wished that we might have certain and infallible standards for the true and the good! For although our knowledge would be more limited if we followed only what is certain, yet that very small portion of proven Truth, which provides for infallible use, will be worth more than all the widely scattered opinions and empty delights of the imagination. 

Because it is infinitely better to know one thing than to have opinions on an infinite number of things. And one small dose of medicine that relieves the sick man is better than infinite, highly sophisticated discourses about the disease, or any remedies—no matter how great the ceremony with which they are sought and administered—that do not actually shift the disease from its place. 

Rightly indeed does Augustine say: *Any truth whatsoever is better than all that can be invented by one’s own whim.* <note>original: "Melius est qualecunque verum, quàm omne id quod pro arbitrio fingi potest."</note> (From the book *On True Religion* <note>original: "Lib. de Ver. Relig."</note>, chapter 5.) Since, therefore, the wiser among men recognize, admit, and even grieve and deplore the mixture of errors with Truth in Philosophy, Theology, and everywhere else: Universal Wisdom <term>Pansophia</term> <note>Comenius’s term for a unified system of all human knowledge, intended to be both universal and certain.</note>, which seeks to gather only what is undeniably True, and to [possess it] demonstratively <term>apodicticè</term> <note>From the Greek "apodeiktikos," referring to knowledge that is proven beyond doubt, similar to a mathematical proof.</note> for secure possession—

<meta>catchword: —demonstratively [cticè]</meta>

<summary>The author argues that a small amount of certain, useful truth is far superior to a vast amount of uncertain opinion, using a medical metaphor to emphasize that effective remedies are better than theoretical talk. He introduces the concept of Pansophia as the method for filtering truth from error across all disciplines.</summary>

### OF UNIVERSAL WISDOM <note>original: "PANSOPHIÆ"</note>

->28<-

...ly <note>Continuing the word "apodictically" from the previous page.</note> to demonstrate it in a certain way, it seeks—it seeks, indeed, a thing that will be extremely beneficial to the human race. For what reason, I ask you, would we wish to be continually mocked by uncertainties, however plausibly they may be arranged? **Dogmas chosen at whim** <note>original: "Dogmata ad placitum"</note> are like the **banquets of Heliogabalus** <note>Heliogabalus (Elagabalus) was a Roman Emperor notorious for his decadence. He was said to have served his guests realistic-looking food made of wood, wax, or stone to mock them.</note>, who delighted his guests with painted feasts and then sent them away hungry.

That banquet is best where the guests are fed on healthy and nourishing foods, however few they may be; not where the palate is irritated and ensnared by various and contradictory things. In the former, moderate pleasure is followed by good **concoction** <term>concoctio</term> <note>A historical medical term for the first stage of digestion, where food was thought to be "cooked" by the body's heat.</note>, good sleep, good digestion, a healthy complexion, and a good state of body and mind. In the latter, there is indigestion, heavy sleep, delirious dreams, vomiting, or stomach pains, diseases, and death.

And just as a portrait of a person is not good because it is beautifully painted, but because it exactly reproduces a living face, whatever that face may be; likewise, a **Geographical Map** <term>Tabula Geographica</term> is not good because it is adorned with colors and pictures (such things move children), but because it shows the true and genuine distan... <note>catchword: "...tias" (completing the word "distantias" or "distances")</note>

<summary>Comenius argues that knowledge based on personal opinion is as useless as a fake feast, whereas "Pansophia" provides the essential, nourishing truth required for human well-being. He emphasizes that the value of knowledge lies in its accuracy (like a map or portrait) rather than its superficial beauty.</summary>

### OUTLINE <note>original: "DIATYPOSIS"</note>

->29<-

...distances <note>Completing the word "distances" from the previous page.</note> and displays them, it is praised. In exactly the same way, a Book will be the best when it represents everything just as things are in themselves, without inventing or painting anything according to whim. That **Universal Wisdom** <note>original: Pansophia</note> should be such a thing—a perpetual map of **Truth** <note>original: Veritas</note>—is precisely what we are seeking.

XXXI. But truly, *the light of Method will also be necessary*. It is only through this that we can hope that everything truly handed down may be read with pleasure and understood with ease. Thus, this Book may become a healthy attraction for talented minds, a successfully erected ladder for climbing more easily to the heights of knowledge, and finally, an effective remedy for removing the greater part of the world’s dissensions. It must be shown that these three things can be expected from the **Pansophic Method** <note>Comenius refers to his systematic approach to teaching and organizing knowledge, which he believed would make learning natural and universal.</note>, so that it is clear we are striving for something useful to the human race.

XXXII. It is futile to hope that human affairs, having collapsed under the weight of universal errors, can be restored except by the universal effort of all. For since all people are members of the **Worldly Republic** <term>Mundana Respublica</term> <note>The "Worldly Republic" is Comenius's term for the global human community; he believed that because all people share the same world and the same reason, they must work together for reform.</note>, the state of human society cannot be changed for the better until...

### OF UNIVERSAL WISDOM <note>original: PANSOPHIAE</note>

...that all people do nothing except by reason, whatever they do, those who are subjects as well as those who are in charge. <note>Completing the sentence from the previous page: "that all people [should act]..."</note> However, they will not be able to begin until they have been taught to know the reasons: how and why it is necessary for each thing to be done. After all these things have been outlined according to the norms of **Truth** <note>original: Veritas</note> itself, so that everyone might desire to know them, there will be a need for certain allurements: namely, a gentle **Method** <note>original: Methodus</note> that everywhere "mixes the useful with the sweet" <note>An allusion to the Roman poet Horace’s *Ars Poetica*: "qui miscuit utile dulci," suggesting that education should be both practical and enjoyable.</note> and exposes everything to clear light. Through this, this **Amphitheater of the Universe** <term>Universitatis Amphitheatrum</term> <note>Comenius uses the "Amphitheater" metaphor to describe the world as a structured place where God's creation is displayed for human study.</note> will appear to no one as a Labyrinth or a thicket of thorns, but to everyone as a garden of delights. But **Universal Wisdom** <note>original: Pansophia</note> proposes this for itself: to unfold the entire universe of things before the eyes of all, in such a way that it is a joy to contemplate each thing in itself, and a necessary expansion of one's desire to move from one thing to another.

XXXIII. This same skillful Method promises to ensure that whoever wishes to read these things may at the same time be able to understand them. This is surely a thing to be desired above all else. For it is common knowledge that the ordinary paths of the **Sciences** <note>original: Scientiarum</note> are overgrown with thorns and rugged obstacles...

<summary>Comenius argues that universal reform requires everyone to understand the rational "why" behind their actions, facilitated by a teaching method that is as pleasant as a garden. He introduces Pansophia as a tool to reveal the entire universe clearly and Joyfully to all people.</summary>

# THE SKETCH <note>original: DIATYPOSIS. A Greek term used to describe a vivid outline or a preliminary draft of a system.</note>

...as the complaints testify, as well as the examples of the very few who, only with great difficulty, finally manage to struggle out of their confusion. For most teachers deliver their subjects in such a way that it is not so much the loftiness or the complexity of the things themselves, but rather the perplexed and obscure **method of teaching** <note>original: tradendi modus</note> that entangles the minds of students. Therefore, since **Universal Wisdom** <note>original: Pansophia</note> seeks shortcuts and skillful strategies—and by God’s help finds them—by which everything may be tempered in a certain proportion to the capacity of average minds, why should it not be seen as offering something of great public benefit? 

For it unfolds everything according to the natural **order of things** <note>original: rerum ordine</note>, so that each thing stands in its proper place, following from what was stated before and giving birth to what follows. In this way, it is impossible for preceding things not to bring light to those that follow, or for following things not to add strength to those that preceded them. If this system is woven together with equal diligence and success from the beginning to the end, it will be impossible for the human mind not to penetrate everything equally. Indeed, there is no tower so high, nor any rock so inaccessible, that anyone equipped with feet cannot reach its summit, provided you move a fixed ladder toward it or carve out the necessary steps.

<header>OF UNIVERSAL WISDOM <note>original: PANSOPHIAE</note></header>

...since without such a guide, anyone who tried would find precipices sooner than they would reach the heights they ignorantly grasp for. In the same way, anyone who approaches the **investigation of things** <note>original: Res speculandas</note> with no order, or with a distorted one, easily suffers from dizziness and falls back down; or they stumble into confused, backward, and monstrous opinions. However, he who proceeds in the proper order and step-by-step can easily elevate himself to the very highest peaks.

### XXXIV.

Finally, it is common knowledge that disagreements, lawsuits, and quarrels reign everywhere and throw human affairs into turmoil. Those who are wiser are also aware that these contradictions and conflicts arise from diverse and contrary pursuits. This happens because we do not learn to think the same way, act the same way, or speak the same way; instead, we whisper different things to different people, or we are each swept here and there by our own individual impulses. 

If, therefore, it could be brought about that all men—just as they were created by the **One God** <note>original: Uno Deo</note> in **His image** <note>original: imaginem ejus</note>, destined for the single goal of a blessed eternity with God, placed into the same school of this present life, and equipped with (or at least capable of being equipped with) the same necessary supports—should thus, in common, be led by 

<meta>catchword: com-</meta>

<summary>Comenius argues that a lack of orderly method leads to intellectual confusion and that the universal discord in human society stems from a lack of shared, systematic education. He proposes that since all humans share the same divine origin and destiny, they should be taught through a common, orderly system.</summary>

# THE OUTLINE <note>original: DIATYPOSIS</note>

B 5

...be led by a common principle, through common means, toward common ends, why should we not hope for more serious study regarding serious matters, and less noise regarding trivialities? And through that, why should we not hope for more concord and fewer disagreements? Since <term>Universal Wisdom</term> <note>original: Pansophia</note> desires, seeks, and attempts this, it surely desires, seeks, and attempts something that will be of common benefit to the human race.

### XXXV.

But let us look at the particulars, and briefly touch upon what hope <term>Universal Wisdom</term> <note>original: Pansophia</note> brings for improving the condition of both the *Common People* and the *Learned*; and for the *Political* and the *Ecclesiastical* <note>referring to civil government and the leadership of the church</note> spheres, and finally, for all the *nations of the whole world*.

### XXXVI.

Does anyone not see with what things the *common crowd of men* occupies itself? They occupy themselves with frivolous and vain things, or certainly external things, which extend only to this fleeting life. They do not care for more solid goods—those that are internal and eternal—because they do not know them. They do not know them because they have not recognized them; therefore, they delight in their own trifles and trash. For what else should he do, who has not learned the internal cultivation of the **Soul**, nor tasted the sweeter fruits of **Virtues**, [but...]

# UNIVERSAL WISDOM <note>original: PANSOPHIÆ</note>

### XXXVII.

...tasted, he has not inspected the more precious treasures of Wisdom, nor has he been admitted in any way to the secrets of Heaven and Earth. What remains for him but to toil at turning the soil, gathering crops, scraping together petty coins, and prying into what is happening in his neighbors' houses? For it has not been granted to **Human Nature** to be entirely idle or to lie dormant; it desires to be occupied, or it occupies itself. He who does not do one thing must necessarily do another; and he who does not direct the natural impulse toward better things will surely slip into worse. It would therefore be a beneficial undertaking if even the **Common People** <note>original: Vulgus</note> were finally invited into the universal amphitheater of God’s wisdom. There, with the veil pulled away from **Things** <note>original: Rebus; referring to objective reality as opposed to mere words</note> in the sight of all, the divine treasures could be shown to them as they truly are, to be valued according to their true worth. Once they have caught the scent of these better and truer goods, they may learn to value the fleeting allurements of the world less, and to place the mere shadows of things behind the **Realities** themselves. Finally, even those of the lowest station, condemned to a life of toil and hardship, may grow accustomed to softening their miserable lot with the hope of better things.

# OUTLINE <note>original: DIATYPOSIS; a Greek term meaning a sketch or descriptive draft</note>

->B 6<-

### XXXVII.
As far as the status of the **Learned** <note>original: Eruditorum</note> is concerned, **Universal Wisdom** <note>original: Pansophia</note>, if rightly established, will be able to benefit them in various ways.

### XXXVIII.
First, it is certain that among those who either take pride in the name of "Learning" <note>original: Eruditionis</note> or are admired for it by others, very few rely on their own foundation or on the foundation of the things themselves. Instead, they follow the opinions of one another; since these opinions depart in various ways from each other and from **Reality** <note>original: Rebus</note>, it follows that there is no end to the hesitations, contradictions, and conflicts in both the **Church** and the school. By these disputes, the minds of the learned and the consciences of the unlearned are endlessly tormented. 

If an adequate remedy is sought for this evil, it can be none other than this: that everyone be taught to follow not the leaden rule <note>A "leaden rule" (Lesbia regula) refers to a flexible, unreliable standard that bends to fit the user’s desires</note> of this or that Teacher, but the adamantine <note>meaning unbreakable or diamond-hard</note> rule of God and of **Things** themselves. Through this, they may be taught not merely to hold opinions, but to truly **know**. This will be achieved not by chasing after the external appearance of things, nor by being satisfied with it, but by searching out the very inward, solid, and immovable **Truth** <note>original: Veritatem</note> of things through reliable methods, and by placing that truth—once unearthed and solidly demonstrated—out in the open. This is exactly what **Universal Wisdom** seeks to do.

<summary>Comenius argues that current scholarship is plagued by conflicting human opinions and proposes "Universal Wisdom" as a way to ground all learning in the objective, "adamantine" truth of God and nature. He suggests this will end the endless disputes in schools and churches by moving from mere opinion to certain knowledge.</summary>

# UNIVERSAL WISDOM <note>original: PANSOPHIÆ</note>

XXXIX. Then, if we consider those devoted to the **Arts and Sciences**, some rightly observe that *that variety of methods* (by which every Science, Art, and Language is taught differently than any other—indeed, even the same subject is taught one way by this Author and another way by that one) is not only a hindrance to their minds, but a cross and a torment. 

For it happens that while all proceed with difficulty, most of them—having developed a sense of nausea and being deterred by the so many new crosses always appearing—leap away from the course of studies toward other ways of life. Or, exhausted by such a rough path, they lose their eagerness, leaving many things either untouched or only skimming the surface. 

Rare indeed are those Herculean intellects <note>A reference to the mythical hero Hercules, suggesting minds capable of overcoming immense labor and "monsters" of difficulty</note> for whom new courage returns to the heart and new vigor arises whenever a new monster appears or a new struggle begins! And for this reason, those who are solidly **Learned** <note>original: Eruditi</note> are so rare. 

Who, therefore, would not think it useful and necessary for this obstacle to be removed? Indeed, the scholars of Basel <note>original: Basileenses</note> rightly stated (in their *Diatribe* <note>original: Diatriba; here meaning a formal scholarly discussion or treatise</note> on the concise method of learning the **Hebrew** and **Chaldean** <note>In the 17th century, "Chaldean" was the common term for the Aramaic language</note> languages, in the year...

XLI. Again, those who navigate through the vast seas of *Authors* have need—just like those tossing in the wasteland of the Ocean—of some such magnetic directory, by which they can better protect themselves from errors, quicksands, and reefs. For just as before the invention and use of the **Magnetic needle** <note>original: *Magneticæ acûs*; referring to the mariner's compass, which Comenius views as a revolutionary tool for guidance</note>, those sailing through the sea could not know where they were being carried and where the prow should be directed except when the sky was clear or they were in sight of the shore; when the sky was cloudy and they were driven into the deep, guidance was lacking. In the same way, those who enter the sea of **Authors**, if they are equipped with no guidelines (except for the common sun of common notions <note>original: *communium notitiarum*; a philosophical term for innate truths or "common sense" that all humans possess</note>—which is nevertheless easily clouded—and the shores of preconceived doctrines), they cannot help but be uncertain everywhere and anxious about the outcome. Therefore, because **Universal Wisdom** <term>Pansophia; Comenius’s project to organize all human knowledge into a single, logical system</term> strives to establish such a universal directory (so that whoever has it at hand need not worry about any depths, nights, or mists, being certain of the poles of the universe and secure of the harbor toward which he tends)—namely, a universal...

XLII. If we look at *Philosophers* separately (who, being intent on the speculation of all things and the causes by which they are contained, ought to be the Masters of everything that is done reasonably anywhere), we will find their condition, above all, to be in need of improvement. For whatever philosophy the Schools <term>Scholae; the traditional academic institutions of the 17th century, often criticized for their rigid adherence to ancient authorities</term> possess so far and cling to tooth and nail, it has been drawn from the Greeks and Arabs (Gentiles and Muslims <note>original: *Mahumetanis*</note>); and therefore, even if it has been freed from the superstitions once accepted, it remains very imperfect. There are three certain proofs of this. First, because those thinkers, not being instructed by divine Revelation (by which they might have corrected the errors of the Senses and of Reason), could not help but be blind or stumble in almost every principal matter. For they were ignorant of the beginning of the World, and they were ignorant of its end; they knew neither the constitution of human Nature, nor its corruption, nor its restoration and the manner of that restoration. ### PANSOPHIA <note>Universal Wisdom</note>

...could they deliver concerning Nature, or concerning Morals? Secondly, that **Greek Philosophy**, at the time it was being established (namely, in the time of **Socrates, Plato, and Aristotle**), was a new thing in the world, lacking sufficient practical experience; it seized upon whatever was immediately obvious, taking one thing from this person and another from that. Consequently, everything was diverted into professional displays and disputes, in which they entangled one another—and continue to entangle their followers to this day—leading human intellects away from **Reality** <note>original: *Rebus*; literally "the things themselves," a key concept in Comenius’s philosophy emphasizing direct observation over abstract theory</note> toward themselves and their own mental concepts. They ensnare minds as if in shackles, so that they are unable to contemplate Reality freely.

Finally, the matter itself teaches us that this pagan philosophy <note>original: *gentilitiae*; referring to the non-Christian "gentile" origins of the classical tradition</note> lacked, and still lacks, true and living roots. Had it possessed them, it would have flourished and grown into a progressively larger tree, producing ever more abundant fruit. We see that this is how things ought to be by the example of the **Mechanical Arts** <term>Artes Mechanicae; the practical crafts and technologies—such as printing, seafaring, or clockmaking—which Comenius admired for their constant improvement through trial and error</term>. These arts, as if endowed with a vital spirit, grow and are perfected daily. Indeed, if their original **Inventors** were to come back to life, they would find it necessary to become students themselves, so crude were their first attempts compared to the heights to which the efforts of their successors...

<summary>Comenius critiques classical Greek philosophy as a collection of stagnant, self-referential disputes that lack "living roots." He contrasts this with the "Mechanical Arts," which show true progress and vitality through constant refinement and practical application.</summary>

<keywords>Pansophia, Greek Philosophy, Socrates, Plato, Aristotle, Mechanical Arts, scientific progress, scholasticism, empiricism</keywords>

-> ### D I A T Y P O S I S <note>original: *Diatyposis*; a Greek term meaning a "sketch," "outline," or "vivid description," here referring to the structural plan of Comenius's universal knowledge system.</note> <-

...advanced by skill toward perfection.

The opposite, however, is what we see in the **Philosophical Sciences**: they flourished most under their first Authors and thereafter languished, to such an extent that modern thinkers consider it enough if they follow in those footsteps even from a distance. This is openly admitted by those who elevate the Author of this philosophy, ***Aristotle***, with such great praise that they defer to him the dictatorship, the Empire, and the tribunal of wisdom, never daring to recede from his footsteps. Hence it happens that for so many centuries, the sciences remain fixed and unmoved in the tracks of the ancients, nor do they take on increases worthy of the human race; like statues, they are adored, but not moved forward.

For if anything new has been discovered in the meantime (such as the *Science of Magnetism, Algebra, Logarithms, Sine Tables* <note>original: *Canones sinuum*; refers to the trigonometric tables essential for navigation and astronomy in the 17th century</note>, *Horology, the Art of printing books*, etc.), it was certainly not discovered under the guidance of that ancient philosophy. Instead, it was found either by chance, or because more excellent minds, by penetrating further <note>original: *plus ultrà*; a reference to the motto "further beyond," signaling the breaking of ancient boundaries</note>, dared to attempt something unique. Would that they could therefore be persuaded—those who give themselves over to the contemplation of things—that to the principles...

<summary>Comenius argues that while technology and practical arts progress through innovation, academic philosophy has stagnated by treating Aristotle as an absolute authority. # OF UNIVERSAL WISDOM <note>original: *PANSOPHIAE*</note>

...only if they are properly disposed <note>Continuing from the previous page: "Provided that they [the seekers of knowledge] are of a mind properly disposed..."</note> to search for the hidden Truth of things within the **Things themselves**, and not in **Books** or the **concepts of others**. Oh, how much hope reveals itself that more abundant treasures of God’s Wisdom <note>original: *Sapientia*</note> will forever be unearthed—treasures which still lie hidden, and will remain hidden, if men persist in being satisfied with what has already been discovered and remain tethered only to the thoughts of others. For what new thing could he possibly find, I ask you, who keeps himself only on those paths where infinite numbers of people are constantly passing? What gold can he dig out who enslaves himself to a vein already exhausted by others down to the last crumbs? Or who re-melts <note>original: *recoquit*; literally "re-cooks," a metaphor for refining metal ore</note> metallic earth that has been smelted a thousand times over, still hoping to extract something from it?

It is therefore in the interest of Philosophers <term>Philosophorum|At this time, "philosopher" referred to any seeker of knowledge or scientist, not just those studying ethics or logic.</term> that, leaving those exhausted mines behind, they open up new approaches for themselves to investigate the treasures of **Nature** and **Art** <note>original: *Natura & Ars*; in the 17th century, "Art" referred to human skill, technology, and the "Mechanical Arts" discussed on the previous page.</note>. Since Universal Wisdom <note>original: *Pansophia*</note> both teaches this and, with God’s help, largely achieves it, no good or wise man—who has at heart the improvement of human affairs—should allow himself to feel envy, much less to hinder it.

**XLIII.** Those among the learned who devote themselves to the *investigation* of things, and [derive] dogmas from...

<summary>Comenius uses a mining metaphor to urge scholars to seek truth in nature and direct observation rather than recycling the exhausted ideas of ancient books. He argues that this "Universal Wisdom" (Pansophia) is essential for human progress.</summary>

<keywords>Pansophia, empiricism, scientific method, God's Wisdom, Nature vs. Books, intellectual progress, mining metaphor</keywords>

# A SKETCH <note>original: *DIATYPOSIS*; an outline or structural plan</note>

...[by ex]amining <note>The Latin text begins mid-sentence with *amining*, likely continuing from *examinando* on the previous page.</note> they establish ***disputations*** <term>disputationes|Formal academic debates used in medieval and early modern universities as a method of teaching and testing knowledge.</term>, the matter speaks for itself that they need better defenses. For what worthy result is achieved by such great efforts as those with which **polemical books** <term>Libri polemici|Books written specifically to argue against a particular theological or philosophical opponent.</term> have been written up to now? They dispute for the sake of disputing: not ending quarrels, but establishing and increasing them. 

For those customary disputations are mere circles, a work without an exit: as the persistence of opinions and **sects** <note>original: *sectarum*; referring to competing religious or philosophical groups</note> colliding in our century demonstrates. All are attacked, none are conquered: each side marvels at the stubbornness of the other, yet no one yields. 

What is the cause? First, **controversies** are stirred up without necessity, by a mere itch of the mind and by the lust for innovation or for contradicting others—which distracts, entangles, and complicates the minds on both sides. Then, controversies once stirred are exaggerated beyond measure; every slight disagreement is considered a **heresy**: which offends, alienates, and irritates the soul. Finally, the very method of **untying knots** <note>original: *enodandi*; a metaphor for solving complex intellectual problems</note> which we use until now is ineffective. Namely, we use it to draw out our own conclusions and to overthrow opposing ones, 

<meta>catchword: prin- (likely beginning 'principia' or 'principles' on the next page)</meta>

<summary>Comenius criticizes the academic and religious culture of his time, arguing that formal debates (disputations) and polemical writings fail to find truth and instead create endless, circular conflict. He identifies the root causes of this failure as intellectual vanity, the tendency to label every disagreement as heresy, and flawed logical methods.</summary>

### PANSOPHIA

...with [principles] <note>The Latin text begins mid-sentence with *principiis*, following "ineffectiv-" from the previous page.</note> (Canons and Rules, **Distinctions** <term>Distinctiones|A standard technique in Scholastic logic where a philosopher clarifies a term by dividing its meanings to avoid contradiction.</term> and **Limitations** <term>Limitationes|The practice of defining the specific boundaries or conditions under which a rule applies.</term>) snatched from wherever, or even recently invented by ourselves to serve as hypotheses and to support our own cause; or certainly principles not yet granted by the opposing party. When an **opponent** <note>original: *adversarius*</note> sees himself attacked by such means, he either laughs or feels contempt. 

Next, from these—as well as from true principles granted by the opponent—**Conclusions** are mostly drawn not by the art of **Logic**, but by the art of **Rhetoric** <note>original: *Rhetoricâ arte*; Comenius argues that debaters use the art of persuasion rather than strict, undeniable reasoning.</note>: that is, not by a **solid demonstration** <term>demonstratio solida|A logical proof that is so rigorous that the mind is forced to accept it, similar to a mathematical proof.</term> by which assent is wrung from the listener whether they are willing or unwilling (as happens among **Mathematicians**), but by certain persuasions which merely solicit assent without necessarily securing it. 

Finally, the end of a **Disputation** <note>original: *Disputationis*</note> usually reflects its beginning, resulting in prejudice and emotion: while those who disagree are condemned, and hatred for them is instilled in others. This gives the opponent an opportunity to shield himself with similar armor, and to defend himself against emotion with emotion, and against hatred with stubbornness. 

However, **Pansophia** <term>Pansophia|Universal Wisdom; Comenius’s lifelong project to organize all human knowledge into a single, unified system that reflects the divine order.</term> promises a remedy for these evils: since it teaches that only solid things should be handled in a solid way...

<summary>Comenius argues that contemporary academic and theological debates fail because they rely on invented rules and rhetorical tricks rather than mathematical-style proof. He introduces "Pansophia" as the solution to this cycle of intellectual stubbornness and mutual hatred.</summary>

<keywords>logic, rhetoric, mathematical demonstration, academic disputation, prejudice, Pansophia, methodology, Scholasticism</keywords>

# OUTLINE. 45

...succeed <note>The Latin text begins with the fragment *-cet*, likely completing *proficiet* (it will be of benefit) or *efficiet* (it will bring about), continuing from the previous page.</note>: while it burdens no one with prejudice, it rather invites everyone on equal terms. By establishing principles that are **truly universal**, **truly true**, and **truly clear**—principles that everyone admits voluntarily and with pleasure—it leads minds from those foundations step-by-step, without any gaps, and within constant boundaries. It leads them slowly to the very peaks of **Truth** <note>original: *Veritatis*</note>: not as reluctant followers, but as spontaneous and willing participants. 

And so, while it does not embitter minds but rather softens them, it shows the way to bring even the opinions of those who contradict one another into **agreement** <note>original: *consensum*</note> (provided they contain something true and rational). It ensures that all people, being led from the same foundational principles, through the same means, to the same conclusions, assent to the final points just as much as the first—not because of some persuasion based on mere probability, but because of the very clarity of unshaken truth.

# OF UNIVERSAL WISDOM

When an opponent sees himself attacked by such means, he either laughs or treats them with contempt.

Furthermore, from these principles—the fabricated ones as well as those that are true and conceded by the opponent—conclusions are usually drawn not through the art of **Logic**, but through **Rhetoric**. That is to say, they are not reached by solid demonstration (the kind by which agreement is forced from even the unwilling, as happens among **Mathematicians**), but by certain persuasions that indeed invite agreement, yet do not necessarily compel it.

This gives the opponent a handle to shield himself with a similar shield and weapons, and to defend himself against emotion with emotion, and against hatred with stubbornness.

# OF UNIVERSAL WISDOM

...and healthy persuasions, which grow along with them, to be imbued: this can hardly be obtained by any better or more certain means than if there is at hand a book common to all **Schools**, proposing all beneficial things by one continuous **method**: by whose direction nothing anywhere of those things which pertain to the useful cultivation of minds might be passed over, omitted, or neglected, but everything might everywhere be acted upon and stand firm. # AN OUTLINE
<note>original: *Diatyposis*</note>

nothing is truer than this statement <note>original: *dicto verius nihil est*</note>, if true **Philosophy**—which is the true contemplation of all things—is rightly understood. For since **order** is the foundation and the bond of the welfare of the **State** <note>original: *Reipublicae*</note> (just as it is of all things), and governors must stay vigilant to maintain this order among all people and all matters: it is evident that the better someone understands the rationale of order, the better they can preside over the achievement of that order. 

Moreover, he understands the order of things best who understands it universally, as it is the very soul of all things, and knows how to distinguish **fundamentals** from **accessories** <note>fundamental principles versus secondary details</note> (of which the former are necessarily the same everywhere, while the latter vary). Now, **Universal Wisdom** <note>original: *Pansophia*</note> tends precisely toward this goal: to reveal **Order**, the foundation and bond of all things. Therefore, it will be able to produce good governors of **Republics**. 

But it also matters greatly that those who are subjects obey not by compulsion, but spontaneously, out of a love for justice. Why should this same **Universal Wisdom** not be able to provide this, if the common people <note>original: *plebs*</note> themselves are also admitted to it? Thus, they may learn and understand that the public safety of all, and the private safety of each, consists in

XLVI. **If you look at the Church**, **Universal Wisdom** <term>Pansophia; the concept that all knowledge is a single, unified whole</term> will also be able to bring—with God’s help—no small momentum to its safety and its growth. # THE OUTLINE
<note>original: *Diatyposis*</note>

### ->DIATYPOSIS.<-

...against Christ and his saints who were raging, Christ opposed another means, by which even the hardness of many was conquered: namely, the constancy of the Martyrs, whose blood was the seed of the Church <note>original: *sanguis semen fuit Ecclesiæ*; a famous phrase by the early Christian writer Tertullian</note>. For the more Christians were slaughtered, the more were born in their place: to make manifest the victory of the Cross—that just as Christ did, so Christians would conquer by dying.

Therefore the World succumbed and, defeated, gave way. Soon after, a third means of spreading the Church arrived, when Christ indeed gave to it "nursing Kings and nursing Queens" <note>original: *Reges nutritios, & Reginas nutrices*; a biblical allusion to Isaiah 49:23, referring to Christian monarchs who protected the faith</note>, by whose example and faithful care entire peoples were converted.

By the benefit of this light and of **Printing** <term>Typography: The technology of the printing press, which Comenius viewed as a divine gift to end the "Dark Ages" and spread the Bible</term>, the Holy Books and the writings of the ancients were brought into the public eye. By their help, the **truth** has been beautifully purged of the errors and superstitions that had crept in.

# OF UNIVERSAL WISDOM

truth. Because, however, even here, both the deceivers—who stumble even in the open light—struggle against it, and those who follow the light prefer to light individual torches for themselves (and by doing so, they obstruct the light of others and even their own) rather than enjoying the common, full light; and finally, because nations still remain to be converted (for *the Gospel of the Kingdom shall be preached in all the world before the end comes*, Matthew 24:14) and the remnants of the **descendants of Abraham** <note>original: *Abrahamidum*; referring to the Jewish people whom Comenius, like many of his contemporaries, believed would be converted in the "last days"</note> are to be stirred up to *seek Jehovah their God and David their King in the last days* (Hosea 3:5)—why should it not be believed that the Lord of all things has reserved some universal means for such a universal task? 

By this means, certainly, a bright light may shine upon those sitting in darkness, and the "nerves" <note>meaning the strength or power</note> of those rebelling against the light may be cut. And may **Satan** be bound, so that he cannot deceive the nations, etc. And such a means is either this which **Pansophia** <term>Pansophia: From the Greek for "All-Wisdom." It was Comenius's lifelong project to organize all human knowledge—secular and divine—into a single, harmonious system that would lead to world peace and universal enlightenment.</term> shows, or certainly something very close to it: by which it may become most evident to every human mind that the only one *Christian Religio-* <note>The text cuts off here at the catchword "gio-", continuing the word "Religion" on the next page.</note>

### XLVIII.

> *It would also be of great interest to the nations and peoples scattered across the different hemispheres and climates of the world—who are separated from each other by the barriers of linguistic idioms—to have some common standard of things among themselves. Regulated by this, they might understand each other mutually, even those who do not understand each other’s speech. For what else would that be, if not what God promises through the Prophet: that he will change the speech <note>original: *labium*, literally "lip"</note> of the peoples, so that it may be pure, and that they all may call upon the name of the Lord, serving him with one shoulder <note>original: *humero uno*; a biblical idiom meaning to serve with one accord or unanimously</note>, (Zephaniah 3:9).*

And this would be almost like restoring one single language <note>literally "lip"</note> through which everyone could understand each other; if not in the actual sounds of the words, then certainly in the meanings behind the words. Furthermore, if this book <note>referring to his project of **Pansophia**, or Universal Wisdom</note> were brought to its completion and translated from the Latin language in which it was first conceived—using the same number of paragraphs and, as far as possible, the same words—into the native languages of all nations, it would be a remarkable aid even for the most "barbarous" <note>in this context, the author refers to non-European or non-Christian nations whom he hoped to reach through education</note> peoples, for...

### XLIX.

But now we must demonstrate, as we promised, how the **Pansophic project** is grounded in the very **necessities of our present age**. Specifically, it serves to resist, as far as possible, those "monsters" that are growing excessively powerful in these times: **Presumption**, **Curiosity**, **Samaritanism** <note>original: *Samaritanismo*; likely referring to religious schisms or the mixing of different faiths, as the Samaritans were viewed as a heterodox sect</note>, **Atheism**, and finally **Fury** <note>or "Madness"</note>, which daily arm themselves more and more for the destruction of the human race.

### L.

For first, there has prevailed among far too many people an excessive **opinion of this "learned age,"** as if the study of letters and wisdom had already reached their **peak** <note>original: *ἀκμὴν* (akmēn), a Greek term for the highest point or zenith</note> in our own era. Intoxicated by this opinion, they consider even the most meager, or certainly superficial, learn- <note>The text cuts off here, to be continued on the next page; the catchword "nis" suggests the word is *eruditionis* (of learning)</note>

<summary>This page argues that a universal book of wisdom (Pansophia) would act as a global "Mercury" to overcome the language barriers of Babel. It also frames the project as a necessary defense against the intellectual and moral "monsters" of the 17th century, such as atheism and sectarianism.</summary>

<keywords>Latin as universal language, Tower of Babel, Pansophia, 17th-century crisis, religious schism, intellectual pride, communication</keywords>

-># OUTLINE<-
<note>original: *Diatyposis*; a Greek term used to describe a systematic draft or a preliminary plan of a work</note>

-ing's slaves, they are so pleased with themselves that they almost believe they are **Solomons** <note>referring to the biblical King Solomon, the archetype of supreme wisdom</note>. They do not seek a truer education because they dream that they already possess it, and that there is nothing further left for them to strive toward. Hence it is that in a "learned age," there is actually the greatest scarcity of the truly learned, while common studies have lapsed into a kind of empty chatter and **sophistry** <note>original: *Sophisticationem*; refers here to deceptive or fallacious reasoning rather than modern "refinement"</note>. Therefore, it would be beneficial for these "know-it-alls" to be led out into the **Ocean of Universal Wisdom**. There, by revealing the shores of **unknown lands** and marking them out, they might be shown the vast deserts of human ignorance. Thus, by understanding how much more there is that we do not know compared to those things we do know, they may learn not to raise their crests in pride, but to lower them.

### LI.

But if men can be liberated from this **Presumption** and brought back to a due **modesty** and an ardent study of true wisdom, it will simultaneously be necessary to organize the matter a bit differently than has been done up to now. Namely, we must ensure that whoever is truly hungry for it may be able to proceed toward **true learning** by more certain and more concise paths. For those paths which until now... <note>original catchword: *nus*, completing *eatenus*, meaning "until now" or "hitherto"</note>

### LII.

...[which we] have had up to now are winding, uncertain, and even dangerous. Specifically, because in this "learned" century (as it likes to be called), ***Books***, ***Schools***, ***Methods***, and various ***Opinions*** on various matters have multiplied to the point of astonishment, learners are utterly confused. It is to be feared that we may all be overwhelmed, so that eventually we either read nothing or believe nothing, unless some barrier is placed against an age so lush both in the conceiving of opinions and the multiplying of books. Learners must be liberated from the weariness of reading so much, from the tumult of conflicting opinions, and from the forks—or rather, the "many-paths" <note>original: *multivius*; a play on *bivium* (a place where two ways meet), suggesting that error now leads in countless directions</note>—of error encountered everywhere. 

That this can be achieved in no other way than through a **Pansophic** <note>original: *Pansophica*; referring to Comenius's project of "Universal Wisdom" which sought to organize all human knowledge into a single, cohesive system</note> approach, anyone who has truly grasped its true purpose will acknowledge and admit. Namely, that all things should be derived from a foundation that cannot deceive—that is, from **the things themselves**, from **divine testimonies** about things, and from the **notions** <note>original: *Notitiarum*; refers to innate ideas or fundamental concepts imprinted on the human soul</note> inscribed upon our **mind**. In these three, as if in its triple palace, eternal truth resides. They must be led by such a method that all things may flow into their minds easily and through a clear light.

<summary>Comenius warns that the explosion of books and conflicting opinions in the 17th century is overwhelming students, arguing that only a "Pansophic" system based on nature, scripture, and innate reason can provide a clear path to truth.</summary>
<keywords>Pansophia, information overload, educational reform, epistemology, triple palace of truth, 17th-century schools</keywords>

### LII.

This same [approach] can simultaneously serve as a remedy for ***human curiosity***, through which some burn uncontrollably with a disordered desire for knowledge concerning less necessary and forbidden things. It does this by designating the legitimate ends of human industry, and by discovering the goals and boundaries of the power granted to the human intellect over the world. Finally, it brings into clear light all the modes of possibility and impossibility, as far as they can be known; so that those who are occupied with unseemly, useless, or impossible things may be called back from a vain and irrational impulse into the paths of reason, and others may be taught to avoid similar opportunities for wandering astray <note>original: *exorbitandi*; literally "going out of the orbit" or "leaving the track"</note>.

### LIII.

***Dissensions and conflicts, indeed even sects, have also grown too strong in Philosophy and Theology***, in which the learned and the unlearned alike are entangled. They have reached a point where they either do not understand one another or, even if they could understand, they neglect to do so because of partisan zeal <note>original: *partium studio*; referring to a biased devotion to a specific school of thought or religious faction</note>. This situation is the origin of various confusions. For while we do not understand the words of others, it happens that we do not understand the things themselves, except confusedly and wrongly; and while some [impute] absurdities to others...

<summary>This page argues that a universal system of knowledge (Pansophy) acts as a remedy for both idle curiosity and the sectarian conflicts plaguing philosophy and theology. By defining the limits of what can be known and clarifying terminology, Comenius hopes to return scholars to the path of reason and mutual understanding.</summary>

<keywords>human curiosity, intellectual boundaries, sectarianism, philosophy, theology, reason, Pansophy, Comenius</keywords>
