[absurdities that we] portray [in others], we ourselves are seen or discovered to cherish far greater ones; nor is there anyone who might effectively settle these disputes, as long as we do not build for the common good. Instead, having collapsed into factions, each person is busy only with fortifying his own positions and striving to undermine the positions of others by any tunnels <note>original: *cuniculis*; a military metaphor referring to digging tunnels under defensive walls to make them collapse</note> possible. By this very process, sects are not diminished, but fixed in place; disagreements are not removed, but multiplied; hatreds are not soothed, but sharpened, without end. 

O, would that God might therefore have mercy on us and gather us back from this dispersion, and restore to us—as He promised through the Prophets and as I have already touched upon—*one heart, and one way, and a chosen lip; so that we may all think the same things, speak the same things, and do the same things, and serve the Lord with one arm* <note>original: *cor unum, & viam unam, labiumque electum... brachio uno*; a synthesis of biblical promises for unity from Jeremiah 32:39 and Zephaniah 3:9</note>. 

If this is to be hoped for in the way it is desired, it can be obtained by hardly any other way than the ***Pansophic*** way. Namely, if the minds of all men were led out into the open field of **Things themselves**, and there, having set aside prejudices, they might freely observe not opinions about things, but the **Things themselves** <note>original: *Res ipsas*; a key concept in this era emphasizing direct observation of nature and reality over the study of old textbooks</note>: with no other leader than the **Senses**, and no other...

<term>Pansophia</term> (Universal Wisdom): A 17th-century ideal of an integrated system of all knowledge—scientific, philosophical, and theological—intended to bring about world peace and religious unity.

<term>Secta</term> (Sect/Faction): In this context, it refers not just to religious groups but to any school of thought that separates itself from the whole to follow a specific leader or dogma.

<summary>This page describes how intellectual and religious factions destroy unity by undermining one another and proposes "Pansophy" as a divine remedy. It argues that by focusing on "the things themselves" through direct observation rather than biased opinions, humanity can achieve a "single heart and way."</summary>

by the light of sound reason, with no other than God as witness and judge. For then, because **Things** are the same for all, and the same **Senses** belong to all, and the same **Reason** judges similarly about similar things, and finally the same **God** witnesses the same things about the same subjects: why should we despair that there will likewise be the same perception, agreement, and **consensus** among all regarding these same things? 

And when we have agreed upon the **Things**, **Words** will no longer be able to distract us so much; since no longer Words, nor our concepts, but the **Things themselves** (which are the same for all) will be the basis of our thoughts and our **Discourse**. And it can be provided, with the help of **GOD**, that **Things**, the **understanding of Things**, and **Speech** (the interpreter of understanding), might run in parallel throughout the whole **Universe**. 

And then disagreements, and the occasions for disagreement, will be happily removed, with no one intent on anything other than the truth of the things themselves. He is a savage enemy who, when he could achieve victory peacefully, prefers a bloody one. We are therefore savage if, when the way of peace and concord shows itself, we prefer endless wars.

We therefore—if the glory of God touches us, if the security of our faith and hope is at heart, if compassion for our perishing neighbors affects us—it is right for us to think again and again about remedies. # OUTLINE

...it is hoped that it can be accomplished, so that Atheists, forced to hear the testimonies of all Created Things <note>original: "Creaturarum"</note> concerning the Creator, are finally compelled to add their own testimony: or, entangled and constrained, they are forced to deny their own existence rather than God's. They would be reduced, that is, to the absurdities of the <term>Sceptics</term> <note>Followers of a philosophical school that doubted the possibility of any certain knowledge, even of one's own senses.</note>, claiming that there is no sensory perception of things, no physical Objects, no World, and no Men to even argue these points. LV. For we see throughout the entire World the mournful and deadly flames of discord and war spreading, devastating kingdoms and peoples with such persistence that everyone seems to have conspired for their mutual ruin, not intending to stop until they and the World itself are destroyed. ...perish, as much as some *universal restoration of souls*: toward this, likewise, a universal Peace and concord [is needed], spread as it were over the whole human race. But by Peace and concord I understand not so much that external kind between Princes <note>original: "Principum"; referring to sovereign rulers or monarchs.</note> and peoples (which is a slippery thing and changeable for slight reasons), but rather the internal peace of Souls, by reason of the Sentiments and Opinions occupying those souls. From this, if it could be obtained, the human race rightfully has much to promise itself. For opinions about things to be pursued or avoided, as they occupy minds, so do they stir the passions <note>original: "affectus"; in this period, these are the "affections" or emotions that drive human behavior.</note>, whether turbulent or calm; and, if those opinions are the same, they produce the same pursuits, desires, and efforts, toward good or evil, according to what the opinions themselves are. This is what wise antiquity beautifully expressed in that fictional *story of Orpheus's theater* <note>A reference to the myth of Orpheus, whose music was so harmonious it tamed wild beasts. -> # OF UNIVERSAL WISDOM <-

and the South against the North: and indeed, with sacred and profane causes—of both Territory and Religion—clashing together, there is a need for an extraordinary universal remedy for such an extraordinary evil. This remedy is, namely: *The bringing back of minds into a certain Universal concord. Either <term>Universal Wisdom</term> <note>original: "Pansophia"</note>, through its desirable <term>Universal Harmony</term> <note>original: "Panharmonia"</note>, will be fit to achieve this, or there is scarcely any other means under heaven.*

LVI. That we dare to conceive of such great things in our prayers, to presume them in hope, to discuss them with voice and pen, and finally to attempt them by setting our hand to the work, is made possible by *the opportunities which the most wise providence of our God provides, preparing the way for some most beautiful and greatest work.*

LVII. The first of these opportunities is the recently and so beautifully discovered *interchange of Eras and Regions*. To be sure, human affairs—both in the mechanical and liberal arts—were previously cultivated and grew only in fragments; and this occurred dispersedly, across different nations and centuries, often unknown to one another. Now we have reached a point where, by the benefit of *Printing* <note>original: "Typographia"</note>, all things

-> # AN OUTLINE <-
<note>original: "DIATYPOSIS"; a Greek term meaning a sketch, description, or plan of a work.</note>

all the monuments of antiquity, unearthed from the darkness, have been shared with the minds of our age; and through the discovered method of **Navigation**, the commerce of all the nations of the **World** has been happily begun. From this, through the emulation of minds stirred up by both these means <note>Referring to the printing press mentioned on the previous page and the navigation mentioned here.</note>, a great effort has begun to track down new inventions over the past few decades. Yet, even this has been achieved almost more through the private industry of specific individuals than for the common good.

What, then, prevents us from trying to see if somehow the observations and things to be observed, the inventions and things to be invented, of the entire **World** might finally become common to all?

Truly, it is so that everyone—those already admitted and those yet to be admitted into the <term>amphitheater of God’s wisdom</term> <note>original: "amphitheatrum sapientiae Dei"</note>—might be taught to observe what is happening, and to encourage one another with mutual enthusiasm toward observing more and greater things (for the **wisdom** of God will never cease in presenting its "plays" upon His world).

This must be attempted with the blessing of God, the guardian of our affairs. For it concerns His glory that the "plays" which His **wisdom** presents in His **Theater** <note>original: "Theatro"; a common Baroque metaphor for the world as a stage for divine action.</note>, as...

LVIII. A further opportunity for attempting **Universal Wisdom** now is provided by the **successful achievements of great minds in refining various specific fields until now, and the pile of materials already painstakingly developed in parts, which are now suitable for conceiving such a great structure.** For what have craftsmen not attempted until now? To what perfection have they not brought their arts? What have the observers of nature left unexamined? For what thing have they not labored to track down the innermost reasons? Indeed, the miraculous refinement of *Arithmetic, Geometry, Optics, Music, Astronomy, and Chemistry* demonstrates the heights to which human knowledge has climbed. Likewise *Logic* <note>original: "Dialectica"; the art of reasoning and logical dispute.</note> has been brought to the utmost precision <note>original: "ἀκρίβεια" (akribeia); Greek for exactness or meticulous accuracy.</note> by the talents of certain modern thinkers, even if it has not yet been put to public use. Furthermore, there is a fervent study of the *Sacred Scriptures* <note>original: "Divinas literas"</note>, and a previously successful effort to unearth even hidden mysteries and the secret meanings of prophecies. In this area, by the kindness of God, more progress has been made—and is being made daily—

<meta>This page continues the argument from page 69. Having established that the current era possesses the "materials" for a Temple of Wisdom (sensory data, reasoning, and Scripture), Comenius now points to the modern trend of creating encyclopedic works as evidence of a divine instinct toward universal organization.</meta>

# UNIVERSAL WISDOM
<meta>catchword: mè</meta>

Those desiring to improve the age act through a certain hidden and good instinct to ensure that they reduce the sciences and arts—however widely scattered they may be—into concise summaries <term>summaries; original: "compendia"; shorter, systematic versions of longer works intended to make study more efficient.</term>. They strive to free these subjects from rough patches and thorns <note>original: "salebris & spinis"; a common metaphor for the confusing, overly complex, and unrefined style of traditional scholastic learning.</note>, and by adding a skillful method, they recommend them to as many people as possible, thereby making men more learned through such conciseness. For we interpret the various efforts of many different people in our own time to tend toward this goal: those who have composed and shared with the world *Pandects, and Systems, and Bodies, and Summaries, and Encyclopedias, and Universal Armories, etc.* <note>These terms refer to the 17th-century explosion of scholarly collections: *Pandects* (complete digests), *Systems* (ordered arrangements), *Bodies* (comprehensive sets of laws or doctrines), and *Universal Armories* (referring to Johann Heinrich Alsted's *Panstratia Catholica*).</note> regarding Theology, Philosophy, Law, and Medicine; likewise *Theaters* of human Wisdom, *Transformations of the Sciences, Great Restorations* <note>A reference to Francis Bacon’s *Instauratio Magna* (The Great Restoration), a monumental project to overhaul human knowledge through induction.</note>, *Christian Omnisciences*, and similar works. If the effort to bring many things into one, to bring order to the scattered, and to bring light to the obscure does not displease good minds, then why should our own effort displease? We advocate for establishing one single thing out of all these—something even more general, more common, and better suited to universal ends than even those previous works. Certainly, just as in the nature of things, individuals [form] the species, species [form] the genus, and genera [form] the most general genus, next—

<summary>Comenius argues that the current trend of scholars creating "encyclopedias" and "summaries" is a divine sign that the time is right for a single, truly universal system of knowledge (Pansophia).</summary>
<keywords>Pansophia, compendium, methodology, intellectual history, Francis Bacon, encyclopedism, organization of knowledge</keywords>

...press; so it is in the **Concepts of the Intellect** <term>Concepts of the Intellect; original: "Conceptibus Intellectûs"; the mental frameworks or categories through which we perceive and organize information.</term> that just as particulars are contracted into summaries, and summaries into the summary of summaries, the progression itself requires it.

LX. Finally, the *hidden instincts of our heart*, and stimuli coming from elsewhere, as well as both the *desires* and the *exhortations* of so many godly people (once the reasons for our proposal became somewhat known) cannot but serve as proof. By this reasoning, the most wise counsel of the **Divine Power** <note>original: "Numen"; a term referring to the divine will or the deity itself, often used to emphasize God’s providence in human affairs.</note> is laboring toward something, provided we offer ourselves as obedient instruments to Him. For since **God and Nature** do nothing in vain <note>A fundamental principle of 17th-century philosophy, often attributed to Aristotle, suggesting that every natural instinct and divine revelation has a specific purpose.</note>, how could it be in vain that He implants these desires for such a great thing? That He allows us to see the means of its possibility? And finally, that He reveals the methods for those means more clearly every day? Thus, it seems nothing remains but to pursue the work vigorously until its successful completion. For surely when God offers something, He wants us to embrace it; when He leads the way, He wants us to follow; when He shows a pattern beforehand, He wants us to express it by imitation and to perfect it through the work itself. *Let them make me a Sanctuary (He said to Moses) so that I may dwell...* <note>original: "Facient mihi Sanctuarium (dixit ad Mosen) ut habitem"; a reference to Exodus 25:8, where God instructs the Israelites to build the Tabernacle. LXI. Nevertheless, we must respond to the objections of certain people who do not correctly perceive the goals and methods of the **Pansophic proposal** <term>Pansophic proposal; original: "propositi Pansophici"; the plan for a universal system of knowledge that harmonizes all truth.</term>, lest they be able to cast a shroud of mist <note>original: "offundere... nebulam"; a metaphor for creating confusion or obscuring the truth.</note> over the matter.

LXII. Someone might say, *These great undertakings involve much insolence and savor of rashness.* 
**Response:** To contemplate, desire, and labor for the **improvement of human affairs** <term>improvement of human affairs; original: "Rerum humanarum emendationem"; the central goal of Comenius’s "Great Restoration" of human society and knowledge.</term> is not so insolent, since from the very onset of our confusion, God—and following God’s example, all good and wise men—have been accustomed to doing that very thing, as necessity required, as opportunities invited, or as the matter itself permitted. How then can that which is done with such great authorities as examples be accused of rashness? Moreover, if it is not rash to behold the works of God; to observe the traces of the **Wisdom of God** <note>original: "sapientiæ Dei"; referring to the concept of the "Book of Nature" where God’s intelligence is visible in creation.</note> which disposes all things sweetly toward their ends; and finally, to arrange all of one’s own affairs according to the will of God (for God commands all those things, reproaching those who act otherwise for their stupor):

**LXV.** They say: *Nevertheless, the title of* **Universal Wisdom** <note>original: "πανσοφίας" (pansophias)</note> *is presumptuous; it is not suitable for human knowledge, however far it may extend; it should be left to God alone.*

<meta>This page continues the defense of the term "Pansophia" (Universal Wisdom). The previous page ended with the argument that human wisdom reflects the image of God; this page justifies the prefix "Universal" by comparing it to how Pythagoras coined the word "Philosophy."</meta>

<header>
### UNIVERSAL WISDOM
</header>

<page-num>78</page-num>

...calls Him **wise** <note>original: "sapientem." See 1 Timothy 1:17 and Ecclesiastes 1:7. Comenius argues that while these texts attribute ultimate wisdom to God, they do not forbid humans from seeking a "universal" understanding.</note> why should the addition of the mark of **Universality** cause so much offense? Indeed, our reasons for proposing this term are no less significant than those of **Pythagoras** when he changed the word *wisdom* into *philosophy*. <note>original: "σοφίας" (sophias) into "φιλοσοφίαν" (philosophian). Tradition holds that Pythagoras was the first to call himself a "philosopher" (lover of wisdom) rather than a "sophos" (wise man), out of humility.</note>

It is said that he had two things in mind: first, God, to whom he wished to leave that glorious title alone, so that He alone might be called **wise**. <note>original: "σοφὸς" (sophos)</note> Second, he had in mind the Sophists and triflers who, through the abuse of the word, counted themselves among the Wise. He, therefore, wishing to call men back from the mere *reputation* of wisdom to the *study* of wisdom, added the mark of "study," creating the term **philosopher**. <note>original: "φιλό-σο-φος" (philo-sophos), meaning "one who loves or pursues wisdom."</note>

As for us, the reasons why we think another mark, that of **Universality** (namely that prefix **"all-"** or **"universal"**), <note>original: "πᾶν" (pan), the Greek root meaning "all," used here to explain the "Pan-" in "Pansophia."</note> should be placed before the word are different, but based on similar causes: the **neglect of wisdom**, and the **abuse** of the words *philosophy*, *theosophy*, <note>original: "θεοσοφίας" (theosophias), meaning divine wisdom or the study of God.</note> and others commonly accepted.

First, I say, because most mortals are so sluggish that, while they over-confidently leave the title and reality of wisdom to God, they themselves become like beasts. They conduct all their affairs rashly, foolishly, and stolidly. And even those who wish to be wise seek knowledge only in fragments, concerning certain specific objects, rather than the whole of wis-

<meta>catchword: dom</meta>

<summary>Comenius justifies the term "Pansophia" by comparing it to Pythagoras's invention of the word "philosophy," arguing that a "universal" approach is needed to combat human intellectual sluggishness and fragmented learning.</summary>

<keywords>Pansophia, Universal Wisdom, Pythagoras, philosophy, humility, linguistics, fragmentation of knowledge, image of God</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the argument from page 78 regarding the necessity of a "Universal" prefix for wisdom. The text concludes the word "philosophy" or "wisdom" from the previous page's catchword and moves into a critique of those who seek knowledge without God.</meta>

<header>
### THE OUTLINE
</header>

<page-num>79</page-num>

...of wisdom, being ignorant and sluggish regarding its embrace; or they seek to be wise without God—certainly neither aiming toward God, nor conforming themselves and their affairs to His standards. Therefore, they must be called back to the laws and standards of the All-Wise God (without whom, just as there is no essence, there is no intellect and no Wisdom, and nothing is true or coherent). This is so that all people, being skilled in knowing all things—and especially divine things—may truly be made like unto God, as far as is possible. 

Therefore, if a method of Science has been found by which **ALL** people are taught to be wise concerning **ALL THINGS**, in an **ALL-ENCOMPASSING** manner <note>original: "pantes, panta, pantōs" (πάντες, πάντα, πάντως). This is Comenius's famous triad of "Universal" education: everyone, everything, every way.</note>, why should it not deserve to be called a kind of human **universal wisdom**? <note>original: "πανσοφία" (pansophia)</note> 

This is done in imitation of words already accepted long ago, in which a more complete preparation for some matter is expressed by adding that prefix "all-": <note>original: "πᾶν" (pan)</note> as can be seen in **full armor** <note>original: "πανοπλία" (panoplia)</note>, **total mobilization** <note>original: "πανστρατία" (panstratia)</note>, **universal cure** <note>original: "πανακεία" (panakeia)</note>, **complete collections** <note>original: "πάνδεκται" (pandektai, or Pandects)</note>, and the **temple of all gods** <note>original: "πάνθειον" (pantheion)</note>, etc. 

Added to this is the abuse of the name **philosophy** as opposed to **theosophy** <note>literally "divine wisdom"</note> or **theology**, and vice versa. People commonly distinguish between these as if neither had anything to do with the other, which is a manifest injury to the Truth. Where, therefore, these things are joined together, teach-

<meta>catchword: tur- (leading into "docentur" or "teaching")</meta>

<header>

<page-num>80</page-num>

...and that men may be admitted (as Augustine says) *both to philosophize in sacred matters, and to be consecrated in Philosophy* <note>original Latin: "et in sacris philosophari, et in Philosophia consecrari." This reflects the goal of merging secular study with religious devotion.</note>, the name is rightly and deservedly changed, so that the subject does not lack its own suitable label. Finally, however, if the title of *Pansophia* <note>Universal Wisdom</note> should entirely displease the Church, let it be changed to something else, or even its opposite, so that instead of *Omniscience* <note>All-knowledge</note>, we might say *Nulliscience* <note>No-knowledge</note>. For it is necessary to arrive there eventually, if we have established true Pansophia (which brings even contraries back into a state of unity), so that the more a person sees, the more defects they notice, both their own and those of others, as that wisest witness testifies in Ecclesiastes 1:15. <note>Ecclesiastes 1:15 states: "What is crooked cannot be made straight, and what is lacking cannot be counted." Comenius uses this to argue that true universal knowledge leads to the humility of realizing how much we lack.</note>

### LXVI.

They also object: *By calling Pansophia a kind of ladder to God, do we transform the work of converting people—which belongs to God alone—into a mere art?* The answer is: as if it were truly absurd either to join external aids to the internal work of the Holy Spirit, or to use a certain prudence and skill in administering them; whereas, rather, he who denies this is the one being absurd. *For God ordinarily acts through means* <note>original: "Deus enim ordinariè per media agit." A standard theological principle that God uses physical tools and human agents to achieve His spiritual ends.</note>: among which means are both people themselves (whom God, as...

<meta>The catchword "admi-" suggests the next page begins with "administris" (as ministers/servants) or "adminiculis" (as aids/instruments).</meta>

<summary>Comenius defends the term "Pansophia," arguing that true universal knowledge leads to humility ("Nulliscience") and functions as a legitimate pedagogical "ladder" or tool used by the Holy Spirit for human conversion.</summary>
<keywords>Pansophia, Augustine, humility, Omniscience, Nulliscience, Ecclesiastes, Holy Spirit, divine means, education as art</keywords>

<header>
# THE OUTLINE
</header>
<note>original Latin: "Diatyposis," referring to a structural plan or a schematic description of a project.</note>

<page-num>81</page-num>

...[God] *has assistants, or fellow-workers (1 Corinthians 3:9)*; then there is human industry, which the art of method directs. From this, when God promises a beautiful reformation of the Church, He promises Pastors who will feed the people with knowledge and understanding *(Jeremiah 3:15)*, and Teachers *with a learned tongue, who know how to speak a word in its proper season (Isaiah 50:4)*. Furthermore, the *practice* <note>original Latin: "praxis," meaning the application of theory or a practical way of acting.</note> of the Prophets, the Apostles, and Christ Himself shows that they were accustomed to approach the people they wished to awaken, move, convert, or convince, not without certain strategies. Thus, it is made clear that this work consists not of diligence and labor alone, but at the same time of a certain prudence and skill. Therefore, if we take all those things, collected into one and adapted for ready use through the benefit of Method, and make them subservient to God; what are we doing but that which is in itself pious and just?

### LXVII.

They say: *There are already enough books, and what is being accomplished? The world remains as it is, and the world will remain.* **Response:** Let [our work] teach us never to despair, never to stop hoping for better things, and never to cease providing opportunities and aids...

...[let] us [be encouraged by] the patience and kindness of God, always renewing itself, and in various ways—each more brilliant than the last—revealing itself. Thus, He promises in the end times an increase of Knowledge <note>original: "Scientiæ multiplicationem," quoting Daniel 12:4</note>, and light at the very evening of the World *(Daniel 12:4; Zechariah 14:7)*. Therefore, let us seek this advancement, as much as God wills to achieve through us, by reforming not only books, but human beings themselves, for the skillful use of books. This is what <term>Pansophia|Universal Wisdom; a systematic approach to knowledge intended to integrate all truth into a single, harmonious framework</term> will do, teaching not only all other things, but also the universal application of itself.

### LXVIII.

*But how can the same book serve different people? Since different things help different individuals, and some things are suitable for the learned, others for the unlearned; some for believers, others for unbelievers.* **Answer:** <note>original: "Resp." abbreviation for "Responsio"</note> <note>original: "Chymici," referring here to alchemists or early modern chemical philosophers</note> Chemists praise their **fifth essence** <term>fifth essence: Also known as the "quintessence," a hypothetical pure substance in alchemy believed to be a universal medicine that restores balance to the body</term> because it is of such exquisite balance that, when applied to everyone, it provides to each individual exactly what is needed: cooling for those who are hot, heat for those who are cold; moisture for the dry, and dryness for those who are moist. Therefore, it becomes a ready remedy for every disease. Something similar is rightly prepared <note>original: "adornato," continued on the next page</note> regarding this Book...

<meta>catchword: [prepared]</meta>

<summary>Comenius argues that "Pansophia" serves as a universal remedy for ignorance, comparing its versatility to an alchemical quintessence that provides exactly what each unique individual requires.</summary>
<keywords>Pansophia, increase of knowledge, end times, reformation of man, quintessence, universal remedy, alchemy, educational theory</keywords>

...it will be permitted to proclaim [established], that (as it will be a certain **fifth essence** <term>fifth essence: In alchemy, the "quintessence" was a substance of perfect balance that could heal any ailment; Comenius uses it as a metaphor for a book that provides exactly what every reader needs</term> of **Books**) it will benefit everyone, with its own fruit returning to each individual. For because of the fitting blend of simplicity with sublimity, and truth with precision <note>original Greek: ἀκριβείᾳ</note>, it will come to pass that it recommends itself to the simple by the simplicity of its Method, while feeding the learned with the sublimity of its Subject Matter. By clearly demonstrating that nothing is more rational than true Faith, it will provide delight to the faithful, while disposing unbelievers toward faith—or at least ensuring they are without excuse.

### LXIX.

However, someone may object here: *Those who, in matters of Faith, appeal to Reason are heretics. Therefore, Universal Wisdom, attempting the same thing, is deservedly suspect.*

**Answer:** <note>original: "Resp." abbreviation for "Responsio"</note> Were **Justin Martyr, Athenagoras, Lactantius, Luis Vives, Mornay, Grotius**, and others who fought for the Faith against unbelievers using reasons, therefore heretics? <note>These authors are famous Christian "apologists" who used philosophical logic to defend Christian doctrines to skeptics</note> For different people can do the same thing in such a way that it is not the same thing, provided they do it with a different end and in a different manner—which is the case here. The heretic applies Reason to divine testimonies as a *judge*; the seeker of Universal Wisdom <note>original: "Pansophus"</note> applies it as a wi-

<sig>D 6</sig>
<meta>catchword: stem. [completing the word "testem" or "witness"]</meta>

<summary>Comenius defends his project against charges of heresy, arguing that using reason to support faith is a venerable tradition used by great Christian thinkers, provided reason serves as a witness rather than a judge of scripture.</summary>

<keywords>Pansophia, Faith and Reason, Justin Martyr, Hugo Grotius, Luis Vives, alchemy metaphor, quintessence, apologists, heretics</keywords>

--- Page 88 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from page 83. The final word on the previous page was "div-," which here concludes as "-inity," referring to how heretics misapply reason to the nature of God.</meta>

<header>
# UNIVERSAL WISDOM
</header>

<page-num>84</page-num>

-inity. The Heretic orders Reason to go before and mark out the paths through which he may drag divine Revelation; the Pansophist <term>Pansophist: A practitioner of "Universal Wisdom" (Pansophia), a system that seeks to harmonize all human knowledge with divine truth</term> wants God to go before, and Reason to follow. Therefore, the Heretic uses Reason to overturn the dogmas of Faith, while the Pansophist uses it to establish them. The former pits the Creature against the Creator; the latter subordinates the one to the other. The former teaches Reason to speak against Faith; the latter makes Reason speak on behalf of Faith. This is done so that those who do not accept God’s testimonies (*Atheists and unbelievers*), or who corrupt them with their own twisted reasonings (*Heretics, Sophists <note>Sophists: Those who use clever but fallacious and deceptive arguments</note>, and Smatterers* <term>Smatterers: Translating the Latin *scioli*, referring to people who possess only a superficial or "smattering" of knowledge but speak with unearned authority</term>), may be constrained by the force of their own reason. Thus, they may be led to the point where they are compelled to stand for God and His truth even against themselves—that is, to yield to the Word of God rather than resist it. In short, *our God is worthy to have the mouths and hearts of all men bear witness to His own mouth, and to be compelled to so bear witness. This is what Universal Wisdom seeks.* <note>original: "cujus ori atteſtentur... omnium hominum ora". Comenius uses a play on words suggesting human speech (mouths) should echo God’s own declarations (mouth).</note>

### LXX.

*Do you then hope (they ask) that there will be no more contradictions? For heresies will always exist.* **Answer:** *I believe that the wicked will always be rebels against the light as long as the World shall last (Job 24:13). Yet shall we for that reason neglect to light lamps, because* <meta>catchword: [to the] blind</meta>

[Are lamps] useless to the **completely blind** because they cannot see? Or should we not purify the flames [of lamps] by trimming the wicks <note>original: "flammas emungendo". Literally "wiping the nose" of the lamp, a common Latin idiom for trimming a charred wick to make the light brighter.</note>, just because some **worker of darkness** <term>Worker of darkness: Translating the Latin *tenebrio*, referring to a person who operates in the dark; a swindler or someone who avoids the light of truth.</term> rejoices more in the gloom? Yet, should we for that reason neglect to remove them whenever **God** provides the opportunity? It is the duty of the children of light to attack the kingdom of darkness wherever possible: and to bring lit lamps into the **House of the Church** upon lampstands, so that they may shine for all who are in the house, leaving the final outcome to God. Therefore, it is right to suppress whatever darkness we can overcome by the power of light; even though the prince of darkness will never fail to find opportunities to cast shadows even over a new light. For **Christ** Himself, the Light of the World, will turn even that into an opportunity to reveal light once again; for it is His custom to transform all the efforts of His enemies—even their very furies—into an occasion for making His own glory manifest (**Psalm 16:10**) <note>While the text cites Psalm 16:10, the sentiment about human wrath being turned to God's praise aligns more closely with Psalm 76:10 in modern numbering: "Surely the wrath of man shall praise thee."</note>. LXXI. Finally, there are those who object to our insignificance: *How small are you, who dare to move such great things?* Answer: We must not look at how small we are who advise these things, but how great He is in whose name we advise them; and the things which we advise; and those for whose sake we advise them. He in whose name we dare to exhort mortals to seek a **universal remedy** <term>Universal remedy: In the context of Pansophia, this refers to a comprehensive system of knowledge and education intended to heal the social, religious, and intellectual divisions of the world.</term> for their confusions is **God**, the Lord of all, who is *prepared to confirm His mercy over all and to establish His Truth forever*; and for whom it is entirely possible even to open the mouth of a **Donkey** <note>original: "Asinae". A reference to the biblical story of Balaam’s donkey (Numbers 22:28), where God gives speech to a humble animal to deliver a divine message.</note>; or to rouse the stones to cry out, if others keep silent <note>A reference to Luke 19:40, where Jesus says the stones would cry out if His disciples were silenced.</note>. It is His custom <term>Sollemne: Literally "solemn" or "established," here meaning God's habitual or characteristic way of acting.</term> to use contemptible means, so that the glory may be His, and not belong to those who forget they are merely an instrument in the hand of God—even if they might seem to be something significant when considered on their own. The matter itself, which we wish to see promoted, is the **glory of God**, to be made manifest through everyone and everything as much as possible here under heaven: so that from the rising of the sun even unto its setting, from this moment now...

...forever, <note>original: "usque in seculum". He is the one who produced all from one blood, redeemed all with one blood, and invites all to the same **communion** <term>Communio: A shared participation or fellowship, here referring to the collective spiritual life of humanity.</term> of the future life, not wishing anyone to perish, but for all to come to **repentance** <term>Resipiscentia: Literally a "coming to one's senses" or "becoming wise again"; the Latin term used for spiritual repentance.</term>.

Therefore, he who despises these things does not despise us, but God—indeed, God's majesty and the whole human race; whose glory and salvation he either scorns or neglects (as much as lies within him), or even hinders others who are attempting it. As for us, our own insignificance will be unable to hinder things that are so desirable in themselves; since even a **fool** <note>original: "stultus". Comenius uses this self-deprecating language to argue that even if he is viewed as simple or unqualified, the merit of the "universal remedy" remains valid.</note> is sufficient to provide the occasion for a good thing. If we are such fools, then you who are wise, exert your strengths, so that whatever you perceive to be lacking in such great desires may be fulfilled.

### UNIVERSAL WISDOM

*Send out, O Lord,* **YOUR LIGHT**, *and* **YOUR TRUTH**! *let them lead us and bring us back to* **YOUR HOLY MOUNTAIN**, *into Your dwelling places.* (Psalm 43:3). <note>original: "Emitte Domine LVCEM Tuam, & VERITATEM Tuam..."</note> From these very words, we repeat over and over the three essential requirements of Universal Wisdom <term>Pansophia: A concept in the 17th century referring to a "universal wisdom" that harmonizes all knowledge—scientific, philosophical, and theological—into a single system.</term>. Namely, that there be observed:

1. The **LIGHT** of *Method*, by which the minds of all people may be led through all things without stumbling, without obscurity, and without any rough paths <note>original: "salebris". This refers to rugged or uneven ground, used here as a metaphor for difficult or confusing intellectual paths.</note>.
2. The **TRUTH** of *Doctrine*, so that we may take care that nothing vain or empty mixes itself in, neither in the whole work nor in any part of it.
3. The *subordination of all things to the Ultimate End*, which is the dwelling together with **GOD** on His holy **MOUNTAIN**.

### J. A. L. TO THE READER

->Greetings.<-

<image-desc>Decorative drop cap 'H' with foliage and floral motifs inside a square frame.</image-desc>Most kind Reader, when I read these things in *Sacred History* <note>original: "Historia sacra"; here referring to the biblical books of Chronicles.</note>: David gave to Solomon the description of the Portico, and of the Temple, and of the Storerooms, and of the Upper Room, and of the Inner Chambers in the sanctuary, and of the House of Atonement, as well as all the Courts and Side-rooms he had planned all around, for the Treasuries of the House of the Lord, and for all the Vessels of service: All these things (he said) came to me in writing by the hand of the Lord; so that I might understand all the works of the MODEL <note>original: "EXEMPLARIS". In the context of 1 Chronicles 28, this refers to the divinely inspired "pattern" or "plan" for the Temple.</note> (**1 Chronicles 28:11, 12, 13, & 19.**) from this I observe those seven points—

<margin>1. I observe.</margin> First, *that Consultations about Things must absolutely be set before the Things themselves.*

<margin>2.</margin> Second, *And that these should not only be extemporaneous,* <note>original: "extemporales"; meaning unplanned or improvised.</note>

->DELINEATION OF THE PANSOPHIC TEMPLE<-
<note>original: "TEMPLI PANS. DELIN." Short for *Templi Pansophici Delineatio*. "Pansophy" refers to a system of universal knowledge, a concept popularized by 17th-century thinkers who sought to harmonize all learning with Christian divinity.</note>

### I.

With the same words by which the most wise **Solomon** concludes his work written on the **Vanities of the World**, it pleases us to begin the delineation of this Work, which we hope will be an **antidote** to Mundane Vanities: *Of making many books there is no end, and much reading is a weariness of the flesh. The conclusion of the matter is: Fear God, and keep his commandments: for **THIS IS THE WHOLE OF MAN**. For God shall bring every work into judgment, with every secret thing, whether it be good, or whether it be evil.* (*Ecclesiastes 12: verses 14, 15, 16*) <note>In modern Bibles, these are numbered as Ecclesiastes 12:12-14. The author is quoting the Vulgate Latin version.</note>

This book would not show this or that thing piecemeal, but would show the **WHOLE OF MAN**, and teach him to live through this transitory life in such a way that he can bring with him the testimonies of a life well-lived to that awesome judgment.

### III.

We desire, I say, that a book be constructed which alone may stand for all others—a most true <term>repertory</term> <note>original: "repertorium." ### IV.

If we approach our sacred philosophy with this goal, it will happen at the same time that all lower things—through which we raise ourselves as if by an upright ladder to that Sublime One who dwells in his eternity—will not indeed hold us within themselves; yet they will delight, feed, and in their own way satisfy us more and better than they do those whom they hold captive. Namely, if the breadth of our kingdom—of **the universe** <note>original: "τοῦ παντός" (tou pantos). A Greek phrase meaning "the whole" or "the all," referring to the entirety of creation.</note>—is presented in its entirety to our minds through a skillful **Brevity**; and if minds are led into the possession of this kingdom through a skillful **Ease** that unlocks the reasons for all things; and finally, if through a skillful **Soliditity** of treatment, it is achieved that the minds of men are drawn back from the infinite, winding, and wandering mazes of opinions and preoccupations to the simple and eternal principles of **the One, the True, and the Good** <note>original: "Unius, Veri, Boni." ### VII.
Therefore, in imitation of this <note>Referring to the divine symmetry of the Temple of Ezekiel mentioned on the previous page.</note>, we establish above all else four hinges of the **Temple of Wisdom** upon which all its parts must depend: **FULLNESS**, **TRUTH**, **EASE**, and **METHOD**, all executed with precision.

### VIII.
These four qualities are indeed seen as cardinal within the structure of human knowledge by certain critics of philosophy. For those who list the advantages of **Peripatetic Philosophy** <note>The philosophy of Aristotle and his followers, who were known as "Peripatetics" (from the Greek for "walking about") because of their habit of walking while lecturing.</note> boast of these four points:

1. That no philosopher possesses a greater abundance of material and subject matter than **Aristotle** <note>The Greek philosopher (384–322 BC) whose works dominated Western science and logic for centuries.</note> does in his books.
2. That he investigated the reasons and causes of things.
3. That he employed a style of speech that was elegant, well-rounded, clear, and effective.
4. That finally, he observed **Method** <note>A systematic or logical arrangement of topics.</note> more rigorously than his predecessors.

(So **Alsted** <note>Johann Heinrich Alsted (1588–1638), a famous German scholar and author of a massive *Encyclopedia* that attempted to categorize all human learning.</note> states in his *Encyclopedia*, book 4, chapter 8.) If these qualities are pleasing when found in Aristotle or any other good author—and they cannot fail to please clear minds—then they must be observed all the more...

### IX.
Therefore, the Fullness of Things and Subject Matter *shall be the first hinge of Universal Wisdom* <note>Pansophia: a system of universal knowledge that seeks to harmonize all scientific, philosophical, and theological truth.</note>, upon which everything else first depends, both as a whole and in every part. As a whole, so that the entire totality of Things <note>original: "universitas rerum." In this context, it refers to the sum total of all that exists, rather than an educational institution.</note> is considered here: namely, the World with all its fullness. And not only as it is now, but also as it was before it existed, and as it will be after it shall cease to be; together with its wonderful and adorable Presider, **God**. 

For Universal Wisdom must provide what **Seneca** <note>Lucius Annaeus Seneca (c. 4 BC – 65 AD), a Roman Stoic philosopher who wrote extensively on ethics and the natural world.</note> desired in vain for Philosophy: *that just as the face of the whole World comes into view, so she should present herself to us, a spectacle most similar to the World itself*. (Letter 90:) For as he adds: *But for us, whose sight fails at what is near, individual things can more easily be shown, since we are NOT YET CAPABLE OF THE UNIVERSE <note>original: "VNIVERSI NONDVM CAPACIBVS"</note>*: this is testimony that this man of keen insight saw with his Universal Intellect—

--- Page 107 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the Roman philosopher Seneca, who believed that while the human mind should ideally grasp the entire universe, it was not yet capable of doing so in his time. Comenius is now explaining why he believes his own era is finally ready for this "Universal Wisdom."</meta>

<header>SKETCH</header>

...that the universe must be mastered, but that it was not yet possible. He does not touch upon the causes of this impossibility, perhaps because he did not understand them; but to us, they are manifest. Namely, there was a defect of principles <note>original: "Principiorum... defectus." In 17th-century philosophy, "principles" are the fundamental, self-evident truths or starting points upon which all other knowledge is built.</note>: especially of divine Revelation, by whose help those things which are beyond the senses—both those that existed before human memory and those that will be after us—could have become known. Then also, there was a penury <note>scarcity or lack</note> of sensory observations from the ages that had passed before. 

But since God has supplied that defect of principles for us through His Word; and since observations continued through so many centuries have increased the treasures of experience, why should there not now be a hope for a capacity to master the entire Universe? Therefore, let us no longer contemplate it only by parts, but in its whole circuit, as all things flow out from eternity and flow back into eternity, by ways and reasons accommodated to the laws of eternity itself. Let us disclose this to the World, if we can, so that men may begin to be wise not piecemeal <note>original: "frustulatim," meaning in scraps or fragments.</note>, but from the whole: filling the entire area of the mind with the complete solidity of teachings.

### X. 
This Fullness of Things in Universal Wisdom <note>original: "Pansophia." This refers to Comenius's project of a "Temple of Wisdom" that organizes all human knowledge into a single, harmonious system.</note>...

<meta>This page continues Comenius's argument that "Universal Wisdom" (Pansophia) is now possible because human history has accumulated enough observations and divine revelation to complete the "map" of knowledge. He is transitioning into the structural requirements of this system.</meta>

->## TEMPLE OF UNIVERSAL WISDOM<-

[Panso]phia <note>original: "Pansophia." This refers to Comenius's lifelong project of "Universal Wisdom," a system intended to organize all human knowledge into a single, harmonious whole that reflects the order of God's creation.</note> will not be firmly established until it is clear that for each and every thing—whether they already exist in the treasures of human and divine wisdom or still lie hidden—a certain assigned seat has been established, or at least a convenient place has been left for it. Likewise, it must be evident that whatever can be usefully and necessarily said about every **Thing** is either stated here, or at least that the springs have been opened and the channels properly arranged so that it can be drawn out from there. Namely, so that whatever any person says, writes, thinks, strives for, or does, they are doing a part of those things for which the whole will be found here.

### XI.
There was no hope that this could be obtained by any other means, except by fixing our **ultimate end** <note>In 17th-century philosophy, the "end" or "telos" is the final purpose for which a thing exists. For Comenius, the ultimate purpose of human life is union with God.</note> (for the sake of which we exist, along with the **World**, and all those things that surround us here—whether they come to us to be observed, acted upon, used, or enjoyed; namely, our **blessedness in God**) as the base of the entire Work. In this way, all the rays of our **knowledge** from every direction may be directed toward this as if to a center; and all these things that precede it in the flow of time may be referred to that final eternal goal, as means are to their end,

### XII.
We have placed **TRUTH** as the second hinge <note>original: "cardinem." In 17th-century philosophical language, a "hinge" is a fundamental principle upon which an entire system turns.</note> of this work, which all things should likewise look toward. For we have consecrated a most sacred altar to this heavenly nymph <note>Comenius uses the "heavenly nymph" as a poetic personification of Truth.</note> in the Temple of Pansophia, to which no access is open for any fabricated or wicked dogma. Therefore, so that nothing vain, false, or deceptive might force itself in here, we employ as guardians of undefiled faith: *The things themselves, and the Testimonies of the divine mouth concerning those things*; along with each person's own Sense and a **Reason** <note>original: "Ratione." We do this so that no one here need fear being deceived in any matter. ### XIV.
Those obscure and tangled terms of **second notions** <note>original: "secundarum... notionum." In scholastic logic, 'second notions' are abstract concepts that refer to other concepts (like "genus," "species," or "predicate") rather than to real-world objects. Comenius viewed this jargon as a barrier to true understanding.</note>, as they call them—with which almost all books of common Philosophy and Theology do not merely teem, but are stiff and bristling—we avoid entirely, as if they were reefs in the sea. For what need is there for me to speak in such a way that no one, or at most only a few, can understand, if I am able to speak so that everyone understands? And why should I not be able? I will give an example.

Someone wishing to describe the duty of Logic accurately writes as follows:
*The subject of **Logic** <note>original: "Logicæ." The art of reasoning.</note> is of instruction, of treatment, and of use. The subject of instruction is **Natural Ability** <note>original: "Ingenium." This refers to a person's innate mental capacity or talent.</note>; primarily indeed **Reason** <note>original: "Ratio."</note>, but secondarily partly **Memory** <note>original: "Memoria."</note>, and partly **Speech** <note>original: "oratio."</note>. The subject of treatment is the manner of arguing well. It is otherwise called the Subject of the art, as well as the doctrinal and the internal. The subject of use is being and non-being, which is otherwise called the Subject of the craftsman and the external, etc.*

Who, I ask you, understands these things, unless they have become accustomed to these thorns through several years of use? If I, on the other hand, say: *Logic instructs and di-* <note>The text cuts off here, continuing the word "directs" on the following page.</note>

| Key Term | Meaning in this Context |
| :--- | :--- |
| **Logic** | The formal study of reasoning and argument. |
| **Philosophy** | The study of fundamental nature, knowledge, and reality. |
| **Theology** | The study of the nature of God and religious belief. |
| **Natural Ability** | One's innate mental power or wit (*Ingenium*). |
| **Reason** | The power of the mind to think, understand, and form judgments. |
| **Memory** | The faculty by which the mind stores and remembers information. |
| **Speech** | The expression of thoughts through words (*oratio*). |
| **Subject** | The underlying matter or topic being discussed. |

<summary>Comenius argues against the use of "second notions" and dense technical jargon in philosophy and theology, advocating instead for plain language that anyone can understand. He provides a convoluted definition of Logic as a negative example of the "thorny" language he seeks to avoid.</summary>

In Comenius’s philosophy, this refers to the shared human capacity for basic reasoning and perception that connects all people.</note>, his own **Conscience**, and **Things** <note>original: "Rebus." **XIV.** Those obscure and tangled terms of **second notions** <note>original: "secundarum... notionum." In traditional logic, "first notions" are concepts of real objects, while "second notions" are abstract concepts about concepts, such as "genus" or "species," which Comenius found needlessly confusing for general education.</note> (with which almost all books of common Philosophy and Theology are not so much overflowing as they are stiff and bristling) we avoid entirely, like rocks at sea. For why is it necessary for me to speak in such a way that no one understands, or at most only a few, if I can speak so that everyone understands? And why shouldn't I be able to? I will give an example.

Someone wishing to describe the duty of **Logic** <note>original: "Logicæ."</note> accurately writes thus: *The subject of Logic is that of information, of treatment, and of use. The subject of information is the **Intellect** <note>original: "Ingenium."</note>; primarily indeed **Reason** <note>original: "Ratio,"</note>, but secondarily partly **Memory** <note>original: "Memoria,"</note> and partly **Speech** <note>original: "oratio."</note>. The subject of treatment is the method of debating well. It is called elsewhere the Subject of the art, and likewise of doctrine and the internal. The subject of use is being and non-being, which is otherwise called the Subject of the craftsman and the external, etc.*

This reflects Comenius’s democratic ideal of education, where knowledge should not be a secret language for elites.</note> may understand me. Therefore, I have said more, because I have spoken to more people; he has said less, because he spoke to fewer, albeit with a greater winding of words and under the **veils of enigmas** <note>original: "ænigmatum involucris." And why should I not prefer to speak in this way, by which I can hope that I am not just speaking into the winds? To whatever people God is angry, He is accustomed to speak to them in a foreign tongue (Isaiah 28:11). And when He wishes to confound the builders of **Babylon** <note>Referring to the Tower of Babel (Genesis 11), used here as a symbol of intellectual confusion and the fragmentation of knowledge.</note>, He confuses their languages (Genesis 11); but when He wishes to build **Sion** <note>Used here to represent the ideal state of the Church or a unified community of knowledge, referencing the miracle of Pentecost.</note>, He restores the easy use of language, so that those who previously could not understand one another might do so (Acts 2). 

Therefore, **Paul** <note>The Apostle Paul, specifically citing his argument against "speaking in tongues" without interpretation in the church.</note>, refuting the praise sought by some for being able to speak in a way unknown to the uneducated, says: *You indeed do those things rightly; but the other is not built up. I prefer to speak five words in the Church from understanding, so that I may instruct others with my voice, than ten thousand words in a tongue* (1 Corinthians 14:17, 19). If this must be imitated anywhere, it is certainly here, where in the face of the whole Church—indeed...

# THE OUTLINE

...of the human race, for the common edification of all, matters must be explained in words.

XV. I do not yet wish to depart from this turning point: because there is hope for great progress if that rubbish <note>original: "scruta." Literally meaning rags or second-hand junk, the author uses this to describe outdated academic jargon.</note> of obscure terms—worn out until now and deservedly discarded through useless application—is cleared away. Wise men certainly congratulated **Brahe** <note>Tycho Brahe (1546–1601), a Danish astronomer who provided the essential data for the transition from the old Earth-centered model of the universe to the sun-centered model.</note> because he had removed from **Astronomy** the <term>Real Orbs</term> <note>Solid crystalline spheres previously thought to carry the planets through space.</note>, <term>Eccentrics</term>, <term>Epicycles</term>, <term>Deferents</term>, <term>Equants</term> <note>These terms—Eccentrics, Epicycles, Deferents, and Equants—refer to the complex geometric devices used in the old Ptolemaic system to explain the varying speeds and backwards "retrograde" motions of planets.</note>, and all that other useless furniture <note>original: "supellectilem." A metaphor for the heavy, unnecessary intellectual baggage of old theories.</note>, teaching the celestial motions through simpler hypotheses. 

Why then do we not attempt the same throughout everything that can be known? We should remove so many superfluous, obscure, rough, and even monstrous terms: through which those approaching the green fields of the sciences must walk as if through thorns; and pricked by these, many either shudder and withdraw themselves, or walk through them only as if wounded, with their mental concepts utterly torn apart. But indeed, if we are able to do without such things, and to explain matters that are plain in themselves with plain words, intelligible to everyone, what need is there for such trivialities? As **Seneca** <note>Lucius Annaeus Seneca (c. 4 BC – 65 AD), a Roman philosopher who emphasized that philosophy should be practical and clear rather than a game of words.</note> rightly says:

<meta>This page (110) continues the philosophical argument for clarity in education, following the discussion of Tycho Brahe’s astronomical reforms. It moves from specific scientific terms to a broader plea for "Pansophic" (all-encompassing) clarity that even a child can understand.</meta>

### OF THE PANSOPHIC TEMPLE

*Just as it is a luxury to long for delicate things, so it is madness to avoid common things that are not costly. Philosophy demands frugality, not punishment.* <note>original: "Ep. 5." This refers to Seneca the Younger's Fifth Moral Letter to Lucilius, where he argues that a philosopher's life should be simple but not intentionally miserable.</note> Either, therefore, let us act in such a way that **all knowable things** <note>original: "scibilia." This refers to the entirety of human knowledge intended for the Pansophic system.</note> are handed down in such a manner that they may be grasped by every human intellect promptly, spontaneously, and without an interpreter: or we are doing nothing. 

Because we have taken pity on the youth, for whom so many **crosses** <note>original: "cruces." A common metaphor in this period for mental torture or the "agony" of studying poorly explained subjects.</note> are fixed everywhere without necessity, we are attempting to remove them—or, if usage demands that certain terms be retained, to soften them with a convenient placement and a good definition. How correctly we do this, the learned will be able to judge—if only they can do so without prejudice. 

If, however, we do not obtain their **approval** <note>original: "calculos." Literally "pebbles," referring to the ancient Greek and Roman practice of using white or black stones to cast a vote or render a judgment.</note> from them, we shall appeal to **the little ones** <note>original: "τοὺς νηπίους." A Greek term often used in the New Testament to refer to infants or those with child-like faith.</note>, the children and the unlearned; if they grasp our ideas better, Truth itself will rest in their judgments. For it is nothing new for children to applaud the teacher of eternal Truth, while the elders persist in their customary sluggishness. <note>This is a likely allusion to the Gospel accounts of children praising Jesus in the Temple while the authorities remained skeptical.</note>

*And this will be the third principal point that the observers will notice: whether these things of ours are so clear that they may be readily understood by anyone?*

The author positions the success of his Pansophic method on its clarity and its ability to be grasped without the need for elite interpreters.</summary>

# SKETCH.

Regarding this matter (as I have already said), there is no one who will not be a fit judge; indeed, perhaps the more unrefined and simple a person is, the more fit they will be. <note>The author continues the theme from the previous page: if a system of knowledge is truly "universal," it should be intuitively obvious even to those without formal education.</note>

XVI. The fourth **hinge** <note>original: "cardo." A metaphor for a central or pivotal point upon which a whole system turns.</note> of our endeavor, which will grant a marvelous strength to the entire edifice of **Universal Wisdom** <term>Pansophia: A 17th-century philosophical ideal—championed by thinkers like Comenius—of organizing all human knowledge into a single, unified, and coherent system.</term>, is the **harmonious fitness** <term>concinnitas: A term borrowed from classical architecture and rhetoric, meaning a beautiful, logical, and proportionate arrangement of parts into a whole.</term> of our Method. We desire this harmony to be so great that those who journey through these subjects will feel the concepts of their own minds to be as interconnected as they see the actual Things themselves to be in the Universe. It has long been a wish that such a result could finally be achieved through the benefit of a Method, so that we do not always teach in such a way that *students remain students and teachers remain teachers* <note>original: "discipuli maneant discipuli, doctores verò doctores." This cites Julius Caesar Scaliger, a prominent Renaissance scholar. The critique is directed at educational systems that keep students in a state of perpetual learning without ever reaching mastery or certain knowledge.</note>, or so that we do not forever prolong the disputes and disagreements that have crept into learning without ever reaching an end. We must now set forth what we are attempting here, and with what elegance we intend to connect Things to Things, and weave them into human minds. This will be the fourth feature that observers will principally notice.

XVII. *For us, there are **three highest and essential virtues of the Pansophic Method:***

->## OF THE PANSOPHIC TEMPLE<-

1. Perpetual **COHERENCE** of all things.
2. Perpetual **GRADATION**.
3. Perpetual **UNIFORMITY**.
Through the benefit of these, we hope to achieve that all things here may be taught and learned easily, and with the highest clarity and certainty of Truth, almost as if through Mathematics. <note>In the 17th century, mathematics was considered the "gold standard" of certainty. The author argues that if we organize knowledge correctly, learning about the soul or nature should be as clear and undeniable as 2+2=4.</note>

XVIII. Perpetual **COHERENCE** consists in this: that everything, from the greatest to the smallest, from the first even to the last, will be linked together <note>original: "concatenata." Literally "chained together." This reflects the concept of the "Great Chain of Being," where every element of creation is connected to the next.</note>, and some things will depend upon others in such a way that nothing among all the things that exist anywhere can either escape our notice, or appear to be seen in any place other than its own proper spot. 

Until now, because Things and Words lay scattered about, neither truly arranged into their proper classes nor bound together by perpetual links, it could not happen otherwise than that observations about Things and Words were found and gathered into a jumble <note>original: "farraginem." A Latin term for a "mash" or "medley" of mixed grains for animal feed; here it describes a messy, unorganized collection of facts.</note> only in a scattered and nearly accidental manner. From these observations, Rules were constructed through various Disciplines <term>Disciplinarum: The specific branches of knowledge or academic subjects (like grammar, physics, or logic). The author argues these are currently too fragmented and their number too ill-defined.</term> (the number of which was neither certain, nor...

The author seeks a perfect 1:1 correspondence where every word exactly matches a real object or concept, eliminating linguistic confusion.</note> has been discovered and established through the intervening bonds of legitimate **Concepts**—provided that the Concepts, having been accurately and precisely <note>original: "examussim." XIX. Indeed, this is what is sought: that just as all things that exist, and can be thought and spoken of, are a single **World** connecting itself on all sides in its own fullness; so too, those things which are thought and said about that whole universality of Things may be nothing other than a single **system of our science**, undivided within itself. It must connect itself on all sides by its parts in such a way that it is necessary neither to omit anything nor to repeat anything twice, and that each individual thing may be seen standing fittingly in no place other than its own. Thus at last it will happen that this work becomes most like the **World** itself, from which nothing can be withdrawn without ruin (or certainly a shameful gap), nor can anything be added or moved elsewhere without a notable monstrosity <note>original: "monstrositate." In 17th-century thought, a "monster" was something that violated the natural order or proportion. Here, adding unnecessary information to the system would be as unnatural as a biological deformity.</note>. gap), it has been believed possible for the World itself; that same thing might with better right be said of the Pansophic work, so that it may be clear.

XX. A linkage of Things and Concepts of this kind can be achieved if one traverses the Universality of Things through **perpetual Analysis**, namely from the first and highest **concept of Being** <note>original: "Entis conceptus." This refers to the most basic starting point of philosophy: the simple fact that something exists.</note> through all the differences of Things, down to the final and indivisible <note>original: "insectibiles," literally "uncuttable," used here to mean the smallest possible classification that cannot be divided further.</note> tips of those differences, as far as they can be noted: by speaking about each individual thing in its proper place, whatever needs to be said in that same spot.

XXI. But in truth, there is a need for an **entirely new Analysis of Things**: because those analyses which we have had until now are not sufficient for this purpose—especially those famous ten **Aristotelian Categories** <note>original: "Praedicamenta." These are Aristotle's ten classes of being (such as Substance, Quantity, Quality, etc.) which were the standard way of organizing knowledge in the 17th century. The author argues they are too broad to be useful for a "Pansophic" or all-encompassing system.</note>, to which it is commonly believed all things can be reduced as the highest genera of Things. However, neither Aristotle nor anyone else has shown how those genera could be led down into the most specific of species. As a result, those who contemplate things have wandered astray in the universality of Things and Concepts, as if in an infinite **Chaos** <note>original: "Chao." Referring to the Greek concept of the formless void that existed before the universe was ordered.</note>; and those who [offered things...]

<summary>The author argues for a "perpetual analysis" of all existence, starting from the basic concept of Being down to the smallest detail. He critiques Aristotle's traditional "Categories" as insufficient for organizing universal knowledge, claiming they lead to intellectual confusion rather than order.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the critique of the Aristotelian Categories from page 114. The previous page ended by noting that scholars often wandered when trying to apply these old classifications to specific things; this page explains why those efforts felt forced and why a new system is necessary.</meta>

### DELINEATIO. 115

when they attempted to reduce subjects to the **Categories** <note>original: "Prædicamenta." These are the ten classes of being defined by Aristotle (such as Substance, Quality, and Quantity). The author argues they are an artificial way to organize knowledge.</note> (an exercise we were commanded to perform constantly in the Schools), the attempt either failed or succeeded only with hesitation and force. This serves as proof that the **veins of Things** <note>original: "venas Rerum." A metaphor suggesting the natural structural lines of reality, much like the grain in wood or veins in stone, which a researcher should follow rather than cutting across them.</note> have not been correctly identified. This is further shown by the fact that no reason is given, nor is it self-evident, why so many classes of things should be established—neither more nor fewer—nor why they should follow one another in that specific order. Yet, if we had learned to correctly identify the joints of these articulations, the **Universality of Things** would naturally resolve itself into its constituent parts. It would then become obvious that Things proceed in that specific order and number, and no other.

XXII. Therefore, we must attempt the task anew. This is despite the fact that **Juan Luis Vives** <note>Juan Luis Vives (1493–1540) was a prominent Spanish humanist and educator who was often skeptical of the human capacity to fully understand the complexities of the natural world.</note> despairs of any perfect achievement in the study of nature, writing: *"To describe the orders of forms, to summon them for an accounting into classes, as it were, and to organize them in our own contemplation just as they are organized in Nature—this is something only He who created them can do. For the properties, powers, and excellences of Things are unknown to us; unless..."*

<meta>catchword: for-</meta>

<summary>The author argues that traditional Aristotelian categories are forced and unnatural, proposing instead a "Pansophic" system that follows the natural "veins" of reality. He acknowledges the skepticism of Juan Luis Vives, who believed only God could truly map the order of nature.</summary>

<keywords>Aristotelian Categories, Juan Luis Vives, taxonomy, natural order, Universal Knowledge, Scholasticism, philosophy of nature</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the limitations of human classification. While the previous page quoted Juan Luis Vives expressing doubt that humans can ever perfectly map out the natural "forms" or "orders" of the world, this page offers a more optimistic counter-argument, proposing that even an imperfect system is better than a forced one, and laying out the requirements for a truly "Universal Science."</meta>

### THE PANSOPHIC TEMPLE. 116

<margin>perhaps in general. (Book 1, on Primary Philosophy <note>original: "Prima Philosophia." This refers to Metaphysics, the branch of philosophy that deals with the first principles of things, including abstract concepts such as being, knowing, substance, and cause.</note>)</margin>
Suppose, then, that we do not attain a full and exact order of things everywhere: even so, what prevents it from being established somewhat better? For we are confident that, even if our diligence falls short in the arrangement of **Substances** <note>original: "Substantiarum." In traditional philosophy, a substance is a thing that exists in itself (like a tree or a person), rather than a property of something else.</note> through their own forms, we shall nevertheless exhibit a new skill in **Accidents** <note>original: "Accidentibus." These are qualities or properties—like color, size, or position—that can only exist within a substance. For example, "redness" is an accident of an apple.</note> and other kinds of Beings. Thus, if a perfect **analysis** <note>original: "analyſis." Here meaning the systematic breakdown of reality into its component parts.</note> of Things cannot be had, one very close to perfection may be obtained.

XXIII. We establish these conditions for a **Perfect Analysis of Things**: *First, that it be entirely universal and exhaustive, so that nothing can ever be encountered anywhere that does not find its proper seat here among its neighbors. Second, that it be not violent or forced, but rather that it arranges things limb by limb, according to how they naturally resolve themselves (with neither the senses nor the intellect ever objecting). Third, that the reason why there are so many classes of things—and why neither more nor fewer can be imagined—be either explained everywhere or be self-evident.* This, at last, will bring forth both the beauty and the certainty of **Universal Science** <note>original: "scientiæ Universalis." The core goal of Pansophy: a unified, all-encompassing system of human knowledge.</note>.

<meta>catchword: XXIV.</meta>

<summary>The author argues that while a perfect categorization of all existence may be difficult, a near-perfect system is possible if it is exhaustive, natural, and logically self-evident. This "Universal Science" aims to organize knowledge without forcing things into artificial categories.</summary>

<keywords>Pansophia, Universal Science, Substance and Accident, logic, classification, Primary Philosophy, Metaphysics, order of nature</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the systematic breakdown of "Pansophia" (Universal Wisdom). ### ->OUTLINE.<-

<meta>catchword: tis,</meta>

XXIV. Furthermore, just as the World, although one and undivided in itself, is nevertheless clearly distinguished by its parts for the sake of order: so it will be necessary for *Universal Wisdom* <note>original: "Pansophiam." This refers to the author's project of a single, unified system of all human knowledge.</note>, although it is one continuous system, to be either divided or distinguished into certain parts, *Books and Chapters*. This, however, is not to be done according to whim, by some artificial order, but by a sequence which the very intention of our ultimate goal (namely, our blessedness in God, through all things which are anywhere either to be advanced or brought to light) shall prescribe. If we follow this guidance, there will emerge for us a *Work in three parts*, which is nonetheless to be resolved into *seven sections*, or *Books*.

XXV. For before all else, it is necessary to provide a certain preparation of minds for that which is undertaken to be done: which they commonly call *preliminary theory* <note>original: "προθεωρίαν" (protheorian). In a scholarly context, this is the introductory stage of a study where the foundations and goals are set.</note>, or in Latin, *Preliminary Knowledge* <note>original: "Præcognita." Literally "things known beforehand," these are the basic principles one must accept before moving into a deeper study.</note>. Then will follow the *body of Universal Wisdom* itself, presenting a *theory* <note>original: "θεωρίαν" (theorian). Here used in the classical sense of "contemplation" or "viewing" the truth of the world.</note> or contemplation of all things that exist anywhere. Finally, a *supplemental theory* <note>original: "ἐπιθεωρία" (epitheoria). This refers to a concluding reflection or practical application that follows the main study.</note> will conclude, showing the true *Use* of all things that are truly known.

XXVI. In the Preliminary Theory, or Preliminary Knowledge,

### TEMPLE OF UNIVERSAL WISDOM
<note>original: "TEMPLI PANSOPHICI"</note>

...ge <note>Continuing from the previous page's "Preliminary Knowledge" (Præcognita).</note>, it is necessary, because in every work being produced, it is necessary for the material to be predisposed, so that it becomes fit for the form to be received: why, then, should men not also be made teachable before they are taught? Truly, unless we wish to waste our labor and pour the precious liquor of wisdom into leaky jars <note>original: "pertusis infundere doliis." This is a classical idiom—likely referencing the myth of the Danaids—used to describe futile effort where resources are wasted on something that cannot retain them.</note>, it is necessary to begin from preparations, not from the work itself. Namely, so that men might awaken from the lethargy common to mortals, and begin to be touched by a concern for some truer and better goods than those which they have in common with the beasts. This **propedeutic** <note>original: "προπαιδεία" (propaideia). This refers to introductory or preparatory instruction that paves the way for higher learning.</note> of Universal Wisdom <term>Pansophia: the concept of a "universal wisdom" that unites all branches of knowledge</term> will labor earnestly to effect this.

XXVII. *The **Theory** <note>original: "Theoria." In Comenius’s time, this meant the systematic contemplation or comprehensive view of a subject.</note> itself will truly treat Things themselves, in that order in which they were produced, and proceed, and finally end. This consideration will give five Essential parts of Universal Wisdom. Namely, because **God (1)** before he founded the World, had laid up in his divine mind the **Ideas** <note>original: "Ideas." Here used in the Platonic and Augustinian sense: the eternal, divine blueprints or archetypes of all things.</note> of Things (that is, the reasons and forms according to which Things were to be formed, and have been formed). And then **(2)** according to those ideas he constructed the machine of the **World**, and to it gave the power, which Na—* <note>original catchword: "turam," completing the word "Naturam" (Nature) on the next page.</note>

XXVIII. To the **Theory of Things** completed in this way, an **After-Contemplation** <note>original: "ἐπιθεωρία" (epitheōria). # AN OUTLINE

<note>original: "DELINEATIO."</note>

The **Force** diffused through all these, giving life and growth to everything, is *God, inhabiting eternity*, yet at the same time permeating, arranging, and governing all temporal things. <meta>This page continues Section XXXIII, specifically addressing the logical structure of the Pansophic system.
