->### OUTLINE<-

...only to perform a preparatory and sealing function. For <term>Metaphysics</term> <note>In this context, Metaphysics refers to the study of the fundamental principles that underlie all reality, such as being, unity, and truth.</note> teaches nothing specifically; it only lays the general foundations of all things. But the contemplation of God apart from created things is nothing but the summit of that knowledge of Him which, gathered from created things, had preceded it; and yet its perfection is deferred until the heavens. So it is that only the three middle parts remain—those three <term>Courts of the Pansophic temple</term> <note>Comenius uses the architecture of the Temple of Solomon as a metaphor for his system: the Outer Court (Nature), the Inner Court (Reason), and the Sanctuary (Spirit).</note>—to embrace in their entirety all those things which we are permitted to understand in this life: namely, *the whole apparatus of our Natural, Rational, and Spiritual life.*

Indeed, this is so. But in truth, the *Universality of Things* <note>original: *Rerum Universitas*; refers to the physical and metaphysical totality of existence.</note> is one thing, and the *knowledge of the Universality of Things*—that is, <term>Pansophia</term>—is another. The former [the universe itself] needs none of our preparations, but the latter [our knowledge] needs them entirely. For since the *knowledge of Things* is a kind of transplantation of those very things into our minds, neither the manner of carrying out that transplantation nor the use of it once completed should be ignored. Therefore Solomon, when he began to learn the ways of acquiring Wisdom, believed that very process to be a part of Wisdom itself (Wisdom 8:21). <note>original: *Sap. 8. 21*; a reference to the Book of the Wisdom of Solomon in the Apocrypha.</note> Nor

<meta>This page concludes the argument regarding the integral structure of the Pansophic system. It specifically defends the inclusion of the "outer" parts—Metaphysics at the beginning and the contemplation of God at the end—as essential to the "body" of universal wisdom, ensuring that knowledge has both a foundation and a final purpose.</meta>

->### THE PANSOPHIC TEMPLE ###<-

<note>The Latin text here appears to have a missing "not" (non), as the context implies a critique of incomplete knowledge.</note> Truly wise is he who knows something, and yet does not know the use of what he knows. Therefore, those parts—the first and the last—can by no means be amputated from the body of <term>Pansophia</term> <note>Universal Wisdom; the core concept of Comenius’s project to organize all human knowledge into a single, cohesive system.</note>; much less the second and the sixth. For just as a building without a foundation, and a tree without its trunk, are unable to stand, so too the **Order of Things**, in which all particulars are contained, cannot be understood without the **basis of that Order**, which <term>First Wisdom</term> <note>original: *sapientia prima*; a common term for Metaphysics, which deals with the primary principles of being and existence.</note> reveals. Finally, one could not be said to know "all things" if they were to contemplate God in Created Things, and Created Things in God, but did not also know what a **Created Thing** is outside of God, and what **God** is outside of every created thing. Because Pansophia (guided by God and His Word) intends to piously meditate upon this in its final and deepest retreat, it must not be deprived of this final <term>inner sanctuary</term> <note>original: *penetrali*; referring to the *Adytum* or the most sacred, restricted part of a temple, symbolizing the highest stage of spiritual insight.</note>.

XXXIV. ***The second virtue of the Pansophic Method is GRADATION:*** by which things are so connected to one another that, always and everywhere, subsequent things seem to be born from prior ones, and more obscure things seem to rise up naturally from those known things already set forth, without a leap and 

<meta>catchword: gap</meta>

<summary>Comenius argues that Metaphysics and the study of God are the necessary foundation and conclusion of knowledge, respectively. He introduces 'Gradation' as the essential method for ensuring that learning progresses naturally and logically from the known to the unknown.</summary>

<keywords>Pansophia, Metaphysics, Gradation, Order of Things, Created Things, Theology, Foundation of Knowledge, Pedagogy</keywords>

--- Page 133 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of "Gradation," the second virtue of the Pansophic Method. The previous page ended mid-sentence, describing how things must be connected to one another without any gaps.</meta>

<page-num>127</page-num>
<header>OUTLINE.</header>

without any gap. For just as we see happening in a **Tree**, where the **Trunk** rises from the **Root**; the **Branches** from the Trunk; the Twigs from the Branches; and from these the **Buds, Leaves, Flowers, and Fruit**, in an unbroken order, by which later things are always produced, strengthened, and vitalized by the earlier ones. By such a gradual <term>concatenation</term> <note>A chain-like connection where each part is linked to the next.</note> of **Things**, we seek to ensure that the minds of learners are not only able to raise themselves step-by-step to all things, but also (fearing no danger of falling back or wandering astray) may do so with joy. Furthermore, for those who must be freed from corrupt opinions absorbed elsewhere and led back to correct views on Things, their minds—held by such a gradation as if between <term>railings and markers</term> <note>original: *cancellos & metas*; metaphors referring to the boundaries of a track or a porch that keep one from falling.</note>—will find that even if they wished to slip away out of love for a preconceived opinion, they are forced by fear of a precipice to move forward and be carried all the way to the summit of **clear Truth** <note>original: *Veritatis liquidæ*; literally 'liquid truth,' implying it is transparent, pure, and easy to perceive.</note>.

XXXV. The great master <note>original: *artifex*; the word is completed by the catchword below.</note> of teaching **Truth** saw and taught that this is the only legitimate way to lead minds accustomed to the darkness (of ignorance or errors) safely into clear light—

This structured approach is intended to provide joy and security to students while preventing those with mistaken beliefs from straying from the path of logic.</summary>

master. <note>original: *artifex*; this completes the word from the previous page.</note> Augustine says in these words: *Ailing eyes are struck by the very glare they vehemently desire to behold, and having barely seen it, they return with delight to the darkness. For such people, it is dangerous to wish to show them what they are not yet able to perceive—even if they have already reached a state where they might rightly be called healthy. Therefore, these students must first be exercised; and their love for the light should be usefully deferred and nurtured. For first, certain things should be shown to them which do not shine by their own power, but can be seen by means of light: such as a **Garment**, or a **Wall**, or something of this sort. Then, those things that do not shine by themselves, yet gleam more beautifully through that light: such as **Gold**, **silver**, and similar things; yet these must not be so radiant that they hurt the eyes. Then perhaps this earthly fire should be demonstrated modestly; then the **stars**; then the **Moon**; then the glow of the **Dawn**, and the brightness of the whitening sky. In these things—whether more quickly or slowly, whether through the whole sequence or by skipping certain steps—each person, becoming accustomed according to their own strength, will eventually behold the **Sun** without trembling and with great pleasure.* Something of this sort is necessary for those most eager for **Wisdom**, who do not yet see it clearly—

[seeing it], <note>original: *videntibus*; completing the sentence from the previous page.</note> as the best Masters do. For *to arrive at it by a certain order is the task of good discipline; without order, however, it is a matter of scarcely believable luck.* (Augustine, *Soliloquies*, Book 1, Chapter 13). So says that blessed and prudent Teacher of Truth <note>Referring to Saint Augustine.</note> concerning the way of teaching truth both prudently and effectively. Following his example, we have resolved to organize all things drawn from the treasures of Wisdom and spread out before mortal eyes, subordinating them to one another in such a way that reaching the heights of wisdom should not require some extraordinary stroke of luck, but rather ordinary diligence (with God mercifully helping).

XXXVI. For the very sequence of the parts of **Pansophia** <note>Universal Wisdom; a system encompassing all knowledge.</note> is graded in this manner. Indeed, what else are the **Pansophic Preliminaries** <note>original: *Praecognita Pansophica*; the introductory principles of his system.</note> than an eye-salve <term>collyrium: a medicinal eye-salve used in antiquity to clear the vision</term> aptly prepared for the dim-sighted eyes of men? By examining these together here (in the first part of **Pansophia**), they will begin to notice certain rays of that **architectonic Wisdom** <note>original: *Sapientiæ architectricis*; wisdom as the master builder or designer of the universe.</note> scattered through all things. 

Then they will be led forward to behold the more illustrious works of God in the **Atrium of Nature**: where they will see with how vivid colors the Wisdom of God has illuminated those first outlines of His within created things. 

From there, admitted into the **Atrium of Art** <note>In this context, "Art" refers to human skill, technology, and the ways humans reshape the natural world.</note>, they will consider the rays of that eternal light, reflected and refracted in a thousand ways, and variously tempered among themselves in a pure and astonishingly wonderful harmony. 

Moving deeper from there, they will first be shown the horrendous chaos and endless abyss of our own darkness, into which we have fallen when left to ourselves; but then, even more brilliant rays of eternal light sent down to swallow up this [darkness] will be revealed. 

Finally, their minds will be raised to contemplate the very **SUN** of eternity,

<header>D E L I N E A T I O N.</header>

[LIGHT], as it is in itself, and as it will be beheld in its own majesty forever.

XXXVII. By this artifice of **step-by-step progression** <note>original: *Gradationis*; Comenius’s signature pedagogical method of "gradualism," moving from the known to the unknown in small, manageable steps.</note>, we hope to achieve this: that minds may be led, gradually and imperceptibly, to any height whatsoever, leaving no opportunity either for the weak to wander into error <note>original: *hallucinandi*; literally "to hallucinate," but here meaning to fall into intellectual or spiritual delusions.</note>, or for **smatterers** <note>original: *sciolis*; people who possess only a superficial or "showy" bit of knowledge.</note> and the prejudiced to argue against the truth. For it is well known that those unaccustomed to heights, if they are suddenly carried to the very top of a tower, find their vision disturbed, so that they either tremble to look down or even suffer from **vertigo**. If, however, they become accustomed to looking out from less lofty places and raise themselves step-by-step, they can use a steady gaze at the heights just as easily as they do at the ground. This is also well known: anything that is the opposite of what one is accustomed to, if it comes unexpectedly, violently strikes and disturbs the senses. Thus, even **Truth**, when offered too suddenly to those who have grown accustomed to some error, sends their minds into such a horror that they feel they are being harmed; they set themselves in opposition, contradict Truth itself, and quarrel with it. Let this, therefore, not happen in this place, where we are preparing not a **Wrestling-ground** <note>original: *Palæstra*; a gymnasium for wrestling. Comenius uses this to describe the aggressive, competitive style of academic debate common in his time.</note> for combat, but a **Temple** for contemplation.

Such a **ladder of things** will prevent this: in

...pronounced it himself, so that when he was led back to the hypothesis, he might fall silent, having recognized his error. Thus, in this case, whoever has admitted the general **Truth** and confirmed it with their own assent, by that very act strengthens the foundations, against which their own wandering opinions will later be dashed and spontaneously shatter.

XXXVIII. **The third Virtue of the Pansophic Method will be Uniformity: namely, that this subject matter is not treated one way and another another way, but all things will be treated in the same manner.** 

Just as the structure of the **Temple of Solomon and of Ezekiel** <note>Comenius refers to the biblical descriptions of the Temple in 1 Kings 6 and Ezekiel 40-42, which he views as a model of divine, mathematical symmetry and order.</note> consisted of mere parallelograms, so that all things were either square or rectangular throughout; so in the **Temple of Wisdom**, whatever is encountered will be explained through **four cardinal questions: WHAT? THROUGH WHAT? HOW? and HOW MANY KINDS?**, with causes attached wherever the subject requires. For through these four questions, whatever is essential becomes known. Consider: 

(1) The **Thing** itself. 
(2) The **Requirements** of the Thing. 
(3) The **Modes** <note>In philosophy, a "mode" is a specific quality or state of being—how a thing exists or acts.</note> of those Requirements. 
(4) And the **Modes of the Modes**, or the differences, which they call the **Genera** and **Species** <term>Genera & Species</term> <note>These are the standard logical terms for classification: a *genus* is a general class (like "animal"), and a *species* is a specific type within that class (like "human").</note> of things. 

The first question ex-

<summary>This page introduces 'Uniformity' as a core principle of the Pansophic method, comparing the structure of universal knowledge to the mathematical symmetry of Solomon's Temple. It outlines a standardized four-question approach (What, Through What, How, and How Many) to define the essence of any subject.</summary>

<meta>This page continues from page 133, where the author introduced the third virtue of the Pansophic method: Uniformity. He established that every subject in the "Temple of Wisdom" will be analyzed using four cardinal questions. This page details how those four questions are answered and explains why this fourfold structure is the "measure" of all knowledge.</meta>

<page-num>134</page-num>
<header>OF THE TEMPLE OF PANSOPHY</header>

...is dispatched with a **Definition**, explaining what the **Thing** is in its essence. The **Second** [question is answered] by the complete structure of the Thing, or its **Idea**, resolving the thing into its essential requirements <note>original: *essentialia sua requisita*; the necessary components that make a thing what it is.</note>. The **Third** by **Axioms** <note>Self-evident principles or established truths.</note>, which state all the **Essential Truths** both about the Thing and its requirements. The **Fourth** by the **Distribution** of the Thing through new specific differences, if it has any further sub-categories under it. And then any of those sub-categories is taken up again as a new theme, to be led through those same four questions.

XXXIX. **This will be the perpetual form of our Method—a kind of Pansophic and truly ingenious *Squaring of the Circle* <note>original: *Circuli Quadratura*. Traditionally an impossible mathematical problem, Comenius uses it here as a metaphor for a perfect, harmonious solution that brings stability to "wandering" or "fluid" ideas.</note>, by which the wandering flightiness of other methods can be reduced to a firmer stability.** For what **Geometers** declare concerning the **Square**—that it is the measure of all figures—can rightly be said of this quadrangular <note>Referring to the four-part structure just described.</note> Method: that it will be the measure of all Methods. Now, so that we do not seem to speak in riddles, it is my pleasure to illustrate this with one, two, or three examples, taken from **Natural**, **Artificial** <note>original: *Artificialibus*; in this period, this refers to things made by human skill or "Art," such as tools or technology, rather than "fake" things.</note>, and **Moral** matters.

<summary>This page details a standardized four-step analytical process (Definition, Structure, Axioms, and Distribution) used to categorize all knowledge. Comenius describes this "quadrangular" method as a perfect measure—a "Squaring of the Circle"—that brings scientific stability to any subject, whether natural, man-made, or moral.</summary>

<meta>This page provides a concrete application of the "quadrangular" or four-part method introduced on the previous page. Having established the theory, the author now begins a "Delineation" (an outline or sketch) using the Sun as a primary example of a "Substantial" subject.</meta>

<page-num>135</page-num>
<header>DELINEATION</header>

XL. And indeed, it is pleasing to take a threefold natural example: **Substantial**, **Accidental**, and **Defective** or **Privative** <note>These are categories of being: a "Substance" is a thing itself (like the Sun), an "Accident" is a property of a thing (like heat), and a "Privative" is the absence of a thing (like darkness).</note>. Let the **Substance** be the **SUN**: of which a brief, vigorous, and scientific Pansophic "square" <note>Referring to the four-part methodology—Definition, Idea, Axiom, Distribution—described on page 134.</note> treatment would be as follows:

*Definition.*) The Sun is the greatest star of heaven, a destined instrument of Nature for illuminating the Earth by its circuit, and thereby for bringing growth to all things upon it and for measuring the changes of the seasons.

*Idea.*) It is therefore constituted of three things: *1. A massive ball of the most brilliant light gathered into one. 2. A certain life-giving power, flowing out with its rays. 3. A perpetual movement in a circuit.*

(Note Well: If anything here should seem doubtful, it would have to be proved by providing the reasons for each individual assertion, so that the following points may be approached without hindrance. An example of this is what is said here concerning the motion of the Sun, which is attributed by others to the Earth <note>A reference to the heliocentric theory of Copernicus. Comenius acknowledges the scientific debate of his time but utilizes the traditional geocentric description—the Sun moving around the Earth—for the sake of this introductory example.</note>. However, this dispute cannot be decided in this partial treatment of this theme, where the necessary premises have not been established. Let it be admitted for now...)

<meta>catchword: this</meta>

<summary>Comenius applies his four-part Pansophic method to the Sun, defining it as a natural instrument for light and growth while acknowledging contemporary scientific debates regarding heliocentrism.</summary>
<keywords>Sun, Definition, Idea, natural philosophy, geocentrism, heliocentrism, Pansophic method, celestial bodies</keywords>

--- Page 142 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Delineation" of the Sun as a primary "Substance," completing the parenthetical thought from page 135 regarding the necessity of circular motion to illuminate the Earth, regardless of whether the Sun or the Earth is the body moving.</meta>

<page-num>156</page-num>
<header>->TEMPLE OF PANSOPHY<-</header>

...this is certain: for the Sun to illuminate the Earth on all sides, a circular motion is necessary, whether that motion resides in the Sun or in the Earth.) <note>This concludes the thought from the previous page where Comenius acknowledges the debate between the geocentric (Earth-centered) and heliocentric (Sun-centered) models.</note>

*Axioms:*) 1. The Sun is the primary source of light: [Indeed, through the remaining stars alone, we would endure perpetual night.]
2. The essence of the Sun is light [For light cannot be taken from it without the Sun ceasing to be what it is.]
3. The light of the Sun emanates through rays [This is evident to the eye and is proved by reasoning.]
4. The light of the Sun flows out in every direction with its rays. [That is, not only toward the Earth, but also to the sides: which the illumination of the Moon, wherever it may be, demonstrates.]
5. Therefore, the body of the Sun is spherical <note>original: *globosum*</note>. [For rays could not be scattered across a circumference except from a circular shape.]
6. Wherever the Sun reaches with its rays, it provides light and heat.
7. And by both of these, it imparts vigor <note>In the 17th-century context, "vigor" refers to the life-force or vitality that allows plants and animals to grow.</note> to all things.
8. The Sun always illuminates half of the Earth; the other half remains unlit.

<summary>This page lists eight fundamental axioms regarding the Sun, defining its essence as light and its form as a sphere. These axioms serve as a practical example of the Pansophic method for organizing scientific knowledge about a physical substance.</summary>
<keywords>Sun, Axioms, Light, Spherical form, Heat, Vitality, Solar rays, Earth's illumination</keywords>

--- Page 143 ---
<meta>This page continues the numbered axioms regarding the Sun and transitions to the study of "natural accidents," specifically Light, as part of Comenius's systematic Delineation of the physical world.</meta>

<page-num>137</page-num>
<header>DELINEATION. 137</header>

9. The presence of the Sun above the Earth creates **Day**; its absence, **Night**.
10. The more vertical the Sun is above the Earth, the more it shines and burns: the more collateral <note>meaning at a slanted or side-long angle</note> it is, the less so.
11. The vertical rotations of the Sun produce **Summer**; the collateral rotations, **Winter**.
12. The return of the Sun to the same verticality <note>the same highest point in the sky relative to the observer</note> constitutes a **Year**. And so much for whatever remains to be said.
*Distribution)* There is none, because the Sun is a *monodic* <note>original: *monodica*; meaning unique or the only one of its kind</note> creature, a single individual.

XLI. *Let **LIGHT** be an example of a natural accident.* <note>In the logic of this era, an "accident" refers to a quality or property that exists within a substance—like light reflecting off an object—but is not the substance itself.</note>
*Definition)* Light <note>original: *Lumen*</note> is the reflection of rays of light from the surface of an opaque body, and their dispersal through neighboring bodies.
*Concept)* To produce Light, therefore, three things are required: 1. Some **luminous body**. 2. A **ray** shot forth from it. 3. An **opaque body**, upon which the incoming ray strikes and bounces off <note>original: *diffiliat*</note>, and illuminates the surroundings.
*Axioms:)* 1. Without light, there is no ray of light.
2. Without an opaque object, there is no reflection or dispersal of a ray.

<meta>catchword: 3. Ra-</meta>

<summary>This page concludes the scientific axioms regarding the Sun's movements—defining day, night, and the seasons—and begins a new section defining the physics of light as a reflection of rays off opaque surfaces.</summary>
<keywords>Sun, seasons, year, Light, reflection, physics, natural accident, axioms, optics</keywords>

--- Page 144 ---
<meta>This page continues the numbered axioms regarding the physics of light, moving from the definitions of rays and opaque bodies on the previous page to the observable behaviors of light within a space.</meta>

<page-num>138</page-num>
<header>->THE PANSOPHIC TEMPLE<-</header>
<meta>catchword: Plu- (short for Pluribus, referring to the "More" in the first word of the next page)</meta>

3. A ray <term>radius <note>In Comenius’s physics, a *radius* is the direct line of light, while *lumen* is the general illumination resulting from that ray.</note></term> is invisible in itself: it is seen only upon a physical object.
(This is apparent in a ray let into a dark room <note>original: *tenebricosum conclave*; a dark chamber, essentially a *camera obscura* setup.</note> through a narrow hole, where the ray is not seen except on the opposite wall. If a hole is also made in that wall where the ray strikes, the ray will not be seen at all—unless perhaps tiny particles <term>atomic corpuscles <note>original: *corpuscula atoma*; here referring to dust motes that catch the light and make the beam visible.</note></term> are flying in the air, or you place your hand in its path.)

4. Opaque bodies with a smooth or polished surface only reflect the ray and send it elsewhere; however, those with a rough surface dissipate and scatter it.
(For if you place a mirror <note>original: *speculum*</note> against a ray, the entire ray is turned back elsewhere, and it transfers the image of the light entirely to that spot. If you use a wooden board or anything less polished, the ray bounces off and scatters the image of the light into a general glow.)

5. The brighter the ray, the more brilliant the light.
(Therefore, a greater light comes from a solar ray than from the ray of a candle, even if both are let in through the same hole.)

6. More rays produce more light.

<note>The following list translates the key technical terms used in the Latin text above.</note>

<page-num>139</page-num>
<header>### SKETCH <note>original: *Delineatio*; referring to the structural outline or architectural plan of the "Pansophic Temple" of knowledge.</note></header>

(For more candles, or windows, give more light.)

*Distribution.*) Light is either **Natural**, from the sun and stars; or **Artificial**, from our fire and candles; or **Dead** <note>original: *Mortuum*; this refers to what we now call bioluminescence or phosphorescence—light that does not come from heat or combustion.</note>, coming from shining gems, the scales of certain fish, rotten wood, and similar things.

XLII. **The nature of SHADOW is explained Pansophically <note>The "Pansophic" method was the author's system for organizing all human knowledge into a single, harmonious whole, mirroring the divine order.</note> thus:**

*Definition.*) A shadow is a lessening of light on an illuminated body, resulting from the intervention of an opaque <term>opaque <note>An "opaque" body (*opacum*) is any object that does not allow light to pass through it.</note></term> body.

*Concept.*) It is therefore formed by three bodies: First, a **Luminous** body, scattering light rays; second, an **Opaque** body, intercepting a part of those rays; and third, another **Opaque** body, which displays on its surface both the scattered light and the intercepted part. Remove any one of these, and you have removed the shadow.

*Axioms.*) 1. Every luminous thing radiates.

2. Every opaque thing placed opposite a luminous one casts a shadow.

3. Every opaque thing placed opposite another opaque thing <note>Specifically, an opaque object positioned behind another that is blocking the light source.</note> is shadowed.

<vocab>Delineatio: Sketch/Outline, Lumen: Light, Umbra: Shadow, Pansophia: Universal Wisdom, Axiomata: Axioms/Principles, Opacum: Opaque, Lucidum: Luminous</vocab>

<summary>This page categorizes light into natural, artificial, and "dead" types, and provides a philosophical and physical definition of shadows as the interaction between light sources and opaque objects.</summary>
<keywords>light distribution, bioluminescence, shadow, optics, pansophy, axioms, opaque bodies, Jan Amos Comenius</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the physics of light and shadow, transitioning from the basic axioms of shadow formation to the application of these principles in man-made timekeeping instruments.</meta>

<page-num>140</page-num>
->**OF THE PANSOPHIC TEMPLE**<-

4. A shadow is the shadow of an intermediate body <note>The "intermediate body" refers to the object situated between the light source and the surface where the shadow is cast.</note>.

5. A shadow is a kind of image of the intermediate body, representing all its primary features <note>original: *omnia ejus majora repræsentans*; a shadow serves as a silhouette or simplified outline of the object's proportions.</note>.

6. The greater the light and the denser the opaque bodies, the denser the shadow; and vice versa.

7. A shadow always extends in the direction opposite to the light source.

8. If the shadowing body is equal in size to the light source, the shadow remains equal in size and is projected into infinity <note>This describes a cylindrical shadow where the width remains constant.</note>.

9. If the shadowing body is larger than the light source, the shadow grows larger into infinity <note>This describes a diverging shadow that expands as it moves away from the object.</note>.

10. If the shadowing body is smaller than the light source, the produced shadow decreases until it ends in a cone and vanishes <note>This describes a converging shadow, such as the Earth's shadow in space during a lunar eclipse.</note>.

(Note: All these points can be sketched out with illustrated diagrams for easier understanding.)

*Distribution.*) Finally, the differences between shadows, if there are any, are to be explained.

...then. (For the natural **Clock** is the Heavens itself, which measures the greater periods of the World by the unending rotation of the stars; but we have sought instruments for accurately marking out even smaller, hourly spaces: and these have been found, primarily of three kinds. First, those which indicate the hour from the position and light of the sun, moon, and stars: which we call <term>Shadow-clocks</term> <note>original: *Scioterica*; from the Greek *skia* for shadow, these are instruments like sundials.</note>. <margin>*The Concept*</margin> The foundation of a clock is motion (because **Time** itself, or the duration of things, is a certain kind of motion: moreover, the measure and the thing measured must agree in their nature <note>The author is applying a philosophical principle: to measure time—which is the "measure of motion"—one must use an instrument that also moves.</note>.) Therefore, every Clock requires three things: 1. Something that moves. 2. Something that causes the movement. 3. Spaces designated by hourly intervals.

(These requirements are found equally in shadow-clocks and sand-clocks, as well as in mechanical ones.)

<margin>*Axioms*</margin> 1. Every Clock possesses motion.

XLIV. *Finally, let us add an example from Ethics concerning the* **M A G I S T R A C Y** <note>original: *Magistratu*; the office or body of government officials.</note>. *Its <term>Universal Wisdom</term> <note>original: *Pansophica*; referring to Comenius's system of integrated, all-encompassing knowledge.</note> treatment would be as follows.*

*Definition.*) The Magistracy is the lawful pre-eminence of certain individuals within human society, established for the purpose of maintaining that society in order.

*The Idea.*) The requirements of this authority are:

1. That someone be fit for so great a duty—that is, that they have the power, the knowledge, and the will to rule others and keep them in order.

2. That they be lawfully appointed (whether by right of election, inheritance, or a just war).

3. To actually rule and administer all things with dignity, vigilance, and constancy. And this is done, in turn, (1) by prescribing the laws of Order, and ensuring that those laws are not ignored once they are prescribed; (2) by observing how the Order and the Laws are being maintained.

<meta>catchword: (3)</meta>

XLV. From these examples it is clear that all **Pansophic** <note>original: *Pansophica*; pertaining to Pansophy, the system of universal wisdom and integrated knowledge</note> matters will be brief, linked together, graded, and clear in themselves. # OUTLINE #

Let **Axioms** <note>original: *Axiomata*; self-evident truths that serve as the starting points for reasoning</note> flow forth naturally; and finally, let the **Classification** <note>original: *Distributio*; the orderly arrangement of topics</note> remind us that we must proceed in the **Analysis of Things** <term>Analysis of Things: the process of breaking down complex realities into their simplest, most fundamental parts</term>. In this way, all things will be easy to perceive and solid in their practical use. For when the **Intellect** <note>the faculty of human understanding</note> sees that everything arises from within itself and nothing is imported from elsewhere, it meets the truth of things with pleasure. Just as things determine their own nature, the mind approves them by its own testimony; it is certain that if things were not exactly what the mind sees them to be, they would not even exist. By this method, it is achieved that all things shine by their own light, and there is no need to beg for belief or seek forced agreement.

XLVI. It will not be out of place to add the rules we have established for ourselves to observe in gathering and constructing these **Definitions**, **Ideas**, **Axioms**, and **Partitions** <note>original: *Partitionibus*; the logical divisions or categories used to organize a subject</note> of things. We do this so that if we are not permitted to proceed—perhaps being prevented by fate, or hindered by the labors of our ordinary calling—those who wish to succeed us will not be ignorant of our intentions. For I hope that **Christ** will send out others to enter into our labors and lead our sowing to the harvest; so that both he who sows and he who...

XLVII. Generally, **in all of these** (Definitions, Ideas, Axioms, and Classifications), **the following must be achieved:** 

1. That all things should be plain and clear in both wording and meaning, needing no commentaries as much as possible, and illustrated only by examples where necessary. 

2. That they should be of the highest Truth, strictly according to the laws of **universal application**, **essential nature**, and **primary generality** <note>original Greek: κατὰ πάντων (*kata panton*), κατ’ αὐτὸ (*kat' auto*), καθ’ ὅλου πρῶτον (*kath' holou proton*). These are Aristotelian logical principles used to ensure a statement is always true and perfectly defined.</note>: so that they cannot be attacked except through sophistry <note>deceptive or fallacious reasoning</note>, nor do they need to be defended by any exceptions, distinctions, or limitations (whether imaginary or real). This will be achieved if care is taken that nothing is attributed to a thing except that which belongs to it **primarily and by its own nature.** 

For **Amandus Polanus** <note>A German-born Reformed theologian (1561–1610) known for his work on logic and systematic theology</note> rightly writes (in the preface to his *Logic*): *"The common rules of the Arts have so many exceptions because, contrary to the law of* **primary generality** <note>original Greek: καθ’ ὅλου πρῶτον (*kath' holou proton*)</note>, *specific things are taught as if they were general. Therefore, let it be observed,"* (he says) *"which things agree and which disagree: let those that agree be referred to one category, and those that disagree to another; in this way, there will be no exception."*

...but everything will be properly encompassed by lawful **classifications.** On the other hand, as a remedy against the necessity of making Distinctions and Limitations, it will be useful to employ nothing ambiguous or same-named <note>original: *homonymum*, referring to words that sound the same but have different meanings, which can lead to logical errors</note>. Instead, all distinct **Things** should be expressed by distinct **Words** within the very analysis of the objects themselves, so that nothing is finally left to be determined by a commentary. In this way, just as things already collected do not need collecting, and things already ordered do not need ordering; so too things already made distinct will not need further distinguishing, and things already limited will not need further limiting. 

3. Care must be taken to find and express the **middle centers** of things, so that occasions for contradiction may disappear. For most disputes arise because those who do not rightly weigh the balance <note>original: *æquilibrium*</note> of things around their own centers tend to wander too far toward the extremes on either side.

XLVIII. ...is, which for us is such: **Being** <note>original: *Ens*</note> is whatever is, is spoken of, or is thought. 

(Note: For the sake of clarification, let the following be added. Just as from "I am before" <note>original: *Praesum*</note> comes "present" <note>original: *praesens*</note>, from "I am away" <note>original: *Absum*</note> comes "absent" <note>original: *absens*</note>, and from "I am able" <note>original: *Potis sum*</note> comes "potent" <note>original: *potens*</note>; so from "I am" <note>original: *Sum*</note> comes **Being** <note>original: *Ens*</note>. Therefore, **Being** is whatever exists. However, we add "and whatever is spoken of or thought," because things that do not exist are also commonly spoken of and thought about—such as **Cerberus**, **Pegasus**, **Polyphemus**, the **Cyclops** <note>original: *Monoculus*, literally "one-eyed"</note>, etc. Although such things are nothing in themselves, because they occupy the mind, the tongue, the hand, and the paper while they are being thought, spoken, or written, they are by that very fact already something, and are not absolutely nothing. Consequently, they are numbered among "Beings.")

<margin>Idea</margin> There are therefore three properties of Being: 
1. To exist. 
2. To be able to be thought. 
3. To be able to be spoken of.

<margin>Axioms</margin> 1. Whatever is, is. (Whether truly or fictionally, whether in itself or in the imagination. In whatever way it exists, so it is.) 
2. Whatever is, something can be thought about it. 
3. Whatever is, something can be spoken about it. (For even when something is imper- <meta>catchword: scrupulously / scruitinized</meta>

<summary>This page defines "Being" (Ens) as the most fundamental category of knowledge, arguing that even fictional or mythological creatures "exist" as objects of thought and language. It establishes three core axioms: that existence is its own truth, and that anything that exists can be both conceived by the mind and expressed through speech.</summary>

*Division* <note>original: *Partitio*</note>. Accordingly, **Being** is threefold: **Real**, **Notional**, and **Verbal**; or **Thing**, **Concept**, and **Word**. XLIX. Let an example of the latter <note>referring to the use of definitions to find truth</note> be the statement of that blind man in the Gospel: *God does not hear sinners.* <note>original: *Deus peccatores non audit*; a reference to John 9:31.</note> Whether this is true must be judged most conveniently from the definitions of **GOD**, **SINNER**, and **HEARING**, provided they are true definitions. For because:

(1) **God** is the **Being of beings** <note>original: *ens entium*, a scholastic term for the Supreme Being</note>, which is to say, the greatest Being, and consequently the most unified, true, and good—that is, holy.

(2) **Sin** <note>original: *Peccatum*</note>, however, is a turning away from what is good and holy.

(3) **To hear** <note>original: *Audire*</note> (in this context) means to hear favorably—that is, to graciously admit someone and to fulfill their will. How then could the most holy and most zealous God love and promote those who turn away from Him (as long as they remain so)?

### OUTLINE ###

G 5

...that there is [great] utility <note>original: *utilitatem*, completed from the previous page</note> in these definitions, provided they are of the sort that this **Method** requires. We must strive earnestly to possess such definitions, even though constructing them is a task requiring great labor and talent. For indeed, **Vives** <note>Juan Luis Vives (1492–1540), a Spanish humanist and educator who argued for educational reform</note> (in his book *On the Explanation of Essences* <note>original: *De explanatione Essentiarum*</note>, after teaching that a good definition is one that is brief, clear, and reciprocal) writes that the duty of **Defining** belongs to a great and excellent man. Such a man must not only have explored the entire nature of the thing he is about to define, but he must also not be ignorant of everything else surrounding it (if they are to be correctly limited); otherwise, it is easy to be confused and to confuse others. He adds that an error in defining, however small it may be at the start, quickly becomes a massive error, etc.

However, so that this great and difficult task (the act of accurately defining things) might be made somewhat easier, I will add one or two small observations.

**First**, let it be firmly established that a **Definition** consists of the **nearest genus** <term>the broad category a thing belongs to, such as "animal" for a human</term> and the **specific difference** <term>the unique characteristic that distinguishes it from others in that category, such as "rational" for a human</term>.

**Second**, while I do not deny that the best **Definition** is one that expresses the genus and the difference in single words (such as that one: *Flame is burning smoke* <note>original: *Flamma est fumus ardens*</note>), nonetheless, one should not superstitiously avoid...

...that there is a plurality of words, whenever the clarity and fullness of the meaning can be better served. And for that reason, those definitions of ours, set down a little earlier, are somewhat more wordy. **Thirdly**, whenever the **Difference** <term>the specific trait that distinguishes one species from another within the same category</term> cannot be obtained from the **form** (for we admit, along with others, that these forms are sometimes unknown, especially in natural things), it must be taken from the **Purpose** <term>the "final cause" or the end for which a thing exists</term>. This is gathered from **Aristotle** himself, and **Bellarino** <note>likely referencing Francesco Bellarino, a scholastic author</note> demonstrates this with the example of a **Horse** (*Practice of the Arts*, Book 3, Distinction 2, Number 5). Since the purpose of the horse is discovered from its effects and its use—namely, to serve man in transporting him or his goods from place to place, quickly and conveniently—he discards two other possibilities to distinguish the horse from other animals that provide a similar use. (For the **Ox** and the **Donkey** also transport things, but not quickly; likewise the **Camel**, but not as conveniently.) From this, he constructs the following definition:

-> **A horse is an animal organized for transporting a human from place to place quickly and conveniently.** <-

...therefore, so that the **Essence** of a thing might be more fully expressed, and might more easily resolve itself into its own principles (by the **following Idea**). To that end, in the definition of the **Sun** and the **Magistrate**, we added the **final cause** <term>the "final cause" refers to the purpose or end for which a thing exists</term>; but in the definition of **Shadow**, we added the **efficient cause** <term>the "efficient cause" is the agent or force that brings something into being</term>. Likewise, in the definition of a **Clock**, we provided a **difference** <term>the specific trait that distinguishes a thing from others in its category</term> derived from both the purpose and the efficient cause. If this is not pleasing to some, the definitions will have to be more accurately elaborated so that they are strictly concise. But I foretell that we will become obscure if we desire to be too brief: a reef <note>The author uses the metaphor of a "reef" (*scopulum*) to describe the hidden danger of over-simplification in logic.</note> which I especially avoid, and advise others to avoid as well.

LI. *The **Ideas of Things** must be established with no less care: because their use is no less significant for achieving a true, full, and distinct knowledge of things. For although the **Definition** itself may encompass the entire essence of the thing, it does so only in an "involved" or compressed manner. The complications of things are so varied that the unskilled—not knowing how to distinguish **essentials** from **accessories** <term>non-essential or "accidental" properties that are not part of a thing's core nature</term>—often seize upon that which is trivial, while omitting the fundamentals. This is an error most fertile for breeding other errors. To hold firmly to the essentials in every matter is the most beautiful [task] of the mind...*

->*Words will follow a thing well-foreseen, not at all unwillingly.*<-

But to find the true **Ideas of Things** is a matter of no less art than defining things truly: therefore, we shall offer some advice on the method by which they are to be investigated.

LII. It is necessary that everything which is made, and which exists, is made and has been made according to **Ideas**. For unless something existed first in an **Idea** (that is, if it did not have some rational principle, or form, through which it could be made and exist), it would not exist at all. Moreover, the **Ideas of Things** are threefold: **Artificial**, **Natural**, and **Divine**. 

**Artificial Ideas** are a certain **ectype** <term>an "ectype" is a copy or a reproduction of an original model</term>, or imitations of natural things. 

**Natural Ideas** are imitations of the Divine. 

**Divine Ideas** exist of themselves; they are the true **archetype** <term>an "archetype" is the original pattern or "first mold" from which all things of the same type are copied</term> of everything that is rightly made. These, however—because they are hidden in eternity, and are not accessible to us except in as much as they appear in natural things...

<header>DELINEATION.</header>

...[eternal things, but in] natural things they are expressed and lie open. Natural things must be examined by us most of all, both for their own sake—so that we may grasp the **Artistry** <term>the Latin *Artificium* refers to the skilled craftsmanship or design found in the natural world</term> of Nature—and for the sake of the artificial things that follow, so that we may understand their foundations. Finally, we study them for the sake of the divine things that preceded them, so that we may also penetrate more safely into those hidden realms, as far as the logic of divine matters permits.

LIII. All natural things are made according to the same **Ideas**, because they come from the same **Maker** <note>original: *Opifex*; a term often used to describe God as the supreme craftsman of the universe</note>. Because He is one, He has preserved one order in all things; and because He is the best, He has preserved the best order; and because He is true, He has done so truly, and not in a deceptive or imaginary way. 

Therefore, since order is truly implanted within things, it can truly be found, if it is sought through true ways. Now, the true ways are not those which our **Intellect**—which is far too fickle and prone to errors—invents for itself and forces upon things, but those which the things themselves reveal by their own process. For the qualities of things are known from their actions and passions <note>In seventeenth-century philosophy, "passions" refers to the ways an object is acted upon or affected by outside forces</note>, and their **essence** is known from those qualities; from all of these, the essential requirements of a thing become known. If we follow these paths of things, and observe the indelible characters <note>The "characters" are the distinct marks or traits "stamped" into the nature of a thing by the Creator</note> impressed upon them...

For we shall perceive that all things are found in the lowest form of **Being** <term>Ens; a philosophical term for anything that exists</term> just as they are in the highest: but at the lowest level—as is common in the first rudiments of things—they are found in an enwrapped state. Our **Metaphysics** <term>the branch of philosophy that deals with the first principles of things, including abstract concepts such as being, knowing, and substance</term> will make this clearly evident.

Therefore, all created things, down to the smallest specks of dust, are our masters and teachers here, provided that we look upon the things that come from them and are said of them not with the eyes of brutes, but with rational and attentive consideration.

should I seek in every **Being** <term>Ens; the most general philosophical term for anything that exists</term>? Certainly, every **Being** comes from somewhere; that is, it has some origin. &nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;55. *As for **Axioms** <term>Axiomata; self-evident truths or fundamental principles that require no proof and serve as the basis for further reasoning</term>, since no learned man is ignorant that the primary strength of **Truth** consists in them, no one will deny that exquisite care must be given to correctly establishing them in all things (that is, collecting them, arranging them, and solidifying them even unto an immovable strength). For they are, in truth, what they are called: norms of reasoning, the principles of all conclusions already collected or yet to be collected through true reasoning. Of these, there is a greater number, a more elegant order, and a more powerful force for inquiring into and discovering the invincible **Truth of Things**—as well as for freeing it from error and defending it—than people commonly understand as of yet. Since, therefore, these axioms have neither been collected thus far, nor reduced to a system (shining forth only scatteredly from the thoughts, doctrines, discourses, and actions of men), and because men do not sufficiently know how to rely upon them, they allow themselves to be carried away by any wind of opinions and sophisms* <note>original: *sophismatum*; fallacious arguments, especially those used deliberately to deceive</note>...

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;56. But the greatest care must be taken so that nothing is admitted as an ***Axiom*** <term>Axiom; a fundamental principle or self-evident truth that serves as a starting point for reasoning</term> which is not actually one—namely, ***Propositions*** <term>Enunciationes; formal statements or declarations</term> that are only partially true, and not true of everything, everywhere, always, by their very nature, or reciprocally. 58. Moreover, **Axioms** are best collected from **Things themselves**, through lawful, non-erratic human **reasoning**—or more accurately, **Divine** reasoning, insofar as we have them expressed in the **Word of God**. For whatever anyone thinks, speaks, writes, or argues,

<page-num>163</page-num>
<header>D E L I N E A T I O N</header>

<note>continuing from the previous page: "...or more accurately [it...]"</note> approves or disapproves, accuses or excuses, persuades or dissuades, exhorts or entreats, promises or threatens, and so on. Always and everywhere, certain presuppositions occur, upon which the **reasoning** <term>ratiocinatio; the mental process of moving from known premises to a conclusion</term> itself leans as if on a foundation. For clearly, just as whatever is moved moves around something immobile, so whatever is inferred by reasoning is inferred by the power of some unmoved **Truth**: let this be clear from examples.

If someone threatens a servant with a club because of a loss caused, and the servant exclaims, *“I did not do it willingly”* <note>original: *Non lubens feci*</note>: behold, reasoning on both sides arises from presuppositions! For the master presupposes this **Axiom**: *He who causes damage is liable to punishment* <note>original: *Damnum dans obnoxius est pœnæ*</note>; you have caused damage; therefore [you are punished]. The servant, on the other hand, reasons: *The innocent man is not liable to punishment*; I am innocent; therefore [I should not be punished]. And beneath his major premise <note>The "major premise" is the broad general rule in a logical argument</note>, he silently lays another premise as if it were already known: *Harm is voluntary* <note>original: *Noxa est voluntaria*</note>. *My action was not voluntary. Therefore, it is not harm*, etc. 

Behold, such things occur in the words and deeds of all people, even the most dull-witted, like the remaining traces of **wisdom** <note>original: *sapientiæ*</note> created within man, and indelible (though fool- <note>The text cuts off here; the catchword "titiæ" indicates the word is *stultitiæ*, meaning "though foolishness [may obscure them]"</note>

<note>continuing from the previous page: "...notions [of wisdom], characters (variously stained by the filth [of ignorance]):"</note> so that it may be clear that what the Son of Sirach <term>Syracides; the author of the biblical Book of Sirach or Ecclesiasticus</term> said is true: *Wisdom has placed eternal foundations in men* (Ecclesiasticus 1:14). To work at this task would be more than merely picking gold out of dung.

LIX. But to collect **Axioms** <term>axiomata; fundamental principles or self-evident rules</term> from the divine Oracles <note>original: *Oraculis*; referring here to the Holy Scriptures as the direct word of God</note>—that is truly to collect gems from gold; for the most precious treasure of true **Wisdom** <note>original: *Sapientiæ*</note> lies hidden there. For example: when I read in Exodus 22:1 that God commands that a thief must restore what he took, I gather this axiom: *What has been taken away unjustly must be restored.* Likewise: *What is restored must be restored to him from whom it was taken.* Because, however, God commands five oxen to be restored for one ox, and four sheep for one sheep, I establish this axiom: *He who dares more evil must be punished more.* (For an ox is more [valuable] than a sheep; therefore, he who [inflicts] a greater loss upon his neighbor...

LX. Therefore, let the **Holy Scripture** be read in order, accurately and diligently. Once the **reason** <note>original: *ratione*; the underlying logic or rationale</note> behind all the words and deeds of God (insofar as they are such) is considered—a reason which certainly underlies everything, and is all the purer and more solid as the **Wisdom of God** <note>original: *Sapientia Dei*</note> is greater than that of men—let such norms of **Things** <note>original: *Rerum*</note> and **Concepts** <note>original: *Conceptuum*</note>, or principles of reasoning, be extracted. These should then be placed alongside those very passages of Scripture (serving as examples), so that the book of **Pansophia** <term>Pansophia; Comenius's concept of "All-Wisdom," a system designed to unify all human knowledge under God</term> may truly become a key both to Reality and to the Holy Scripture. Next, other wisely written books will provide the same use, so that whatever is observed to be spoken or done with vigor and sharpness may be reduced to an **axiom** <term>axioma; a fundamental principle or self-evident rule of truth</term>.

LXI. This power is present even in lower creatures, though to a lesser degree, and it exerts itself more obscurely. From this, therefore, I gather a supreme **Metaphysical** axiom:

*Whatever exists, delights in existing.* <note>original: *Quicquid est, esse gaudet.*</note> Or: *Whatever exists, maintains itself in its own being for as long as it can.* <note>original: *Quicquid est, in suo esse, quamdiu potest, se tuetur.*</note>

LXIV. By the "art of convincing," I mean the **APODICTIC METHOD** <term>a method of logical demonstration that results in absolute certainty, similar to a mathematical proof</term>: by which every Truth is presented to every human Intellect with such clarity that no one of sound mind can deny their assent. Or, if they should deny it, the truth is wrung from them even against their will and resistance by the very force of inescapable Truth. Just as mathematicians know how to demonstrate their principles, we desire and labor to extend this same certainty to all things. If an infallible way of doing this were found, it should rightly be numbered among God’s chief gifts and the primary fruits of Universal Wisdom <note>original: *Pansophiae*</note>. For then everyone could be taught the Truth and "un-taught" their errors with such clarity and certainty that no one could fail to understand, and no one could cast doubt on anything so demonstrated.

LXV. We are well aware that there are those who strongly doubt whether that mathematical certainty of demonstration and evidence can be transferred to other subjects. Yet there is no shortage of those who stand with us against such doubters. Alstedius <note>Johann Heinrich Alsted (1588–1638), a German Calvinist clergyman and academic known for his monumental Encyclopedia.</note> (in his *Encyclopedia*, book 1, chapter 4) speaks thus: *“Mathematicians indeed claim this praise for themselves, that they possess the most noble demonstrations. But this is not so, since the principles of Metaphysics are primary, or independent. Along with the independence of principles is joined the greatest nobility of Things, and the highest clarity arising from the very nature of the Thing.”* Likewise, in book 4, chapter 12: *“Metaphysical demonstrations are the most certain, firm, and evident of all.”* Furthermore, in book 13, chapter 1, he teaches that *“Physics deals with scientific matters.”* Bellarinus <note>Likely a reference to a scholastic author or Francisco Bellarini, who wrote on the method of the sciences.</note> truly *“subjects even Ethics to the scientific Method.”* (In his *Practice of Science*, book 1, distinction 3, number 8). Truly, however, it is not through authorities, but through invincible reasons that we must prove that the **apodictic method** <term>a method of logical demonstration that results in absolute certainty, similar to a mathematical proof</term> can and ought to be extended to all things. In which, as if—

LXVI. Long ago, the Pyrrhonian Philosophers <note>Followers of Pyrrho of Elis (c. 360–270 BCE), the founder of Greek Skepticism, who argued that human certainty is impossible.</note>, called **Skeptics**, used to argue that everything could be debated on either side—even this very question: *Whether all things could be debated on either side?* They thought the truth of their assertion could be most clearly demonstrated in this way: if they were to involve this very assertion of theirs in doubt as well. Whether they played with these ideas in jest or in earnest does not matter to us; yet for us, who are seriously urging a serious matter, and asserting that necessary **Demonstrations** <term>logical proofs that lead to certain knowledge</term> should and can be introduced instead of slippery disputes, a certain necessity is imposed upon us by their example: the necessity of demonstrating this very assertion in such a way that no one can resist it. 

Therefore, for the honor of the ever-invincible **Truth**, and of **God**, the eternal guardian of truth, we now approach this task, *intending to demonstrate these three things with mathematical certainty:*

*1. That every Truth is, by its own nature, demonstrable.*
*2. That every Pansophic <note>Pertaining to "Pansophia" or Universal Wisdom—the author’s project to organize all human knowledge into a single, cohesive system.</note> truth is demonstrable by the power of the Method.*

3. *That this mystery of Method consists in the accurate Gradation of Truth.*

LXVII. The first point is demonstrated by *this apodictic Syllogism* <note>An "apodictic syllogism" is a logical argument that provides absolute, necessary proof rather than mere probability.</note>:

->Whatever possesses true, proximate, and immediate Causes, Effects, and properties, that thing is truly demonstrable.<-
->But every Truth possesses true, proximate, and immediate Causes, Effects, and properties.<-
->Therefore, every Truth is truly demonstrable.<-

The **major proposition** <note>The first statement of a logical argument, setting out a general rule.</note> is necessarily true: because the firmest demonstrations are derived from proximate causes and effects, and from **Essential properties** <term>qualities that belong to the very nature of a thing and without which it cannot exist</term>; nor has any Logician ever required, or been able to require, anything else.

The **minor proposition** <note>The second statement of the argument, which applies the general rule to a specific case.</note> is likewise certain: because if something does not possess true causes, actions, passions, and properties, it does not even truly exist—that is, it is not "true." Therefore, it is necessary that whatever is true possesses true causes, properties, and effects (whether these are already known to us, or not yet). According to this reasoning, only **Falsehood** is indemonstrable.

LXVII. This is first demonstrated by this **apodictic syllogism** <note>An "apodictic syllogism" is a logical argument that leads to an absolutely necessary and certain conclusion.</note>:

Therefore, it is necessary that whatever is true possesses true causes, properties, and effects (whether these are already known to us or not). According to this reasoning, only **Falsehood** is indemonstrable.

<page-num>174</page-num>
<header>OF THE PANSOPHIC TEMPLE</header>

<meta>catchword: qui</meta>

**first according to the whole** <note>original: *καθ' ὅλου πρῶτον* (kath' holou proton); a logical term for a property that applies to a subject universally and essentially.</note>: namely, **Definitions**, **Hy-**
**potheses**, **Theorems**, and **Problems**.
For whatever occurs to be treated **Pansophically** <note>In the system of "Universal Wisdom" where all branches of knowledge are unified into a single, harmonious whole.</note>, it is immediately and before all else **defined**. Then an **Idea** is constructed, which is like a **Hypothesis** (not a fictional one, however, but a real one), from which, once granted, all questions concerning that subject can be satisfied. Or it is like a **Problem**, which declares that something can be done if this or that is given; namely, the essential requirements of the **Thing**. Finally, what are **Axioms** but **Theorems** that state some true and necessary attribute of the **Subject**? These are of such evident **Truth** that they are like pure principles, known by themselves.
From a good arrangement, therefore, of good **Definitions**, **Hypotheses**, **Theorems**, and **Problems**, why should it not be possible to weave together good demonstrations?
LXIX. The third point I proposed to demonstrate—**that the power of the Demonstrative Method consists in accurate Gradation** <note>A step-by-step progression or "ladder" of logic where each point follows inevitably from the last.</note>—in order to demonstrate this with mathematical certainty and clarity, I demand that three mechanical principles be granted to me.
1. ***Given a height, and given men,***

<page-num>175</page-num>
<header>OUTLINE</header>

*...who strive toward that goal with all their strength, yet do not succeed, it is certain that they surely lack a fixed medium: namely, properly constructed* **Steps** <note>original: *Gradus*; here meaning stages of a logical progression or a literal ladder.</note>.

*2. Once properly constructed steps are provided, it is possible to ascend to any height.*

*3. Given a firm middle ground—one or more—between any height and its base, steps can be built.*

I demand, I say, that these points be granted to me as being evidently true. However, if anyone is so stubborn as to refuse to grant even these for free, I shall demonstrate them myself, as they are the very foundations of my demonstration.

LXX. Suppose there is a **Tower** <note>original: *Turris*</note> fifty cubits high. There are those who greatly desire to climb to its summit; therefore, they walk around it, look about, and try everything, both inside and out, yet all in vain. What will be the cause? I say: the lack of some requirement. Now, the requirements of an **Efficient Cause** <note>original: *Efficientis*; in the philosophy of the time, this refers to the agent or "doer" that brings a result into being.</note> to produce an **Effect** <note>original: *Effectus*</note> are (as **Pansophia** <note>The author's system of "Universal Wisdom" which sought to organize all human knowledge into a single, divine order.</note> teaches and demonstrates in its proper place) **Power**, **Knowledge**, and **Will** <note>original: *Posse, Scire, Velle*; the three essential components for any action to occur.</note>. If even one of these is denied, the **Effect** does not proceed. Either, therefore, those people do not **will** to climb the Tower, or they do not **know how**, or...

<summary>Comenius argues that reaching high levels of understanding is like climbing a tower; it requires a step-by-step progression (gradation) and the alignment of three factors: the will to learn, the knowledge of the method, and the power to execute it.</summary>

...they cannot. But it is not that they are unwilling; for they do desire it, and they strive for it. Yet strength is not lacking—that is, health and vigor—which is evident from their walking and their striving. Nor are they prohibited by anyone, whether by decree or by force. Therefore, the instrument by which one climbs to high places is missing: namely, a *Ladder* <note>original: *Scala*</note> or *Steps* <note>original: *Gradus*</note>. 

For because no man’s feet (the instrument given by nature for moving oneself from place to place) exceed a length of two cubits <term>cubit: an ancient unit of measurement based on the length of the forearm, roughly 18 inches or 45 centimeters</term>, nor can they be spread further than that while walking, no one is able to move himself beyond a distance of two cubits without the help of some middle ground. Since, therefore, the height of that **Tower** <note>original: *Turris*</note> is fifty cubits, while the feet of those walking around it reach only two cubits or less, it is evident that it cannot be climbed without intermediate steps. I will demonstrate this, however, with this **Apodictic Sorites** <term>Apodictic Sorites: A logical argument consisting of a series of propositions where the predicate of each becomes the subject of the next, leading to a necessary (apodictic) conclusion.</term>:

1. *Whoever has feet in good working order, he is able to go.*
(For given the primary agent and the instrument, the effect can follow; and feet are the instrument of travel <note>original: *itionis*</note>.)

2. *Whoever goes, he moves his feet alternately.*
(For unless he moved them, he would not go, but would stand still; nor can that movement happen without the alternating transposition <note>original: *transpositu*</note> of the feet.)

3. *He who moves his feet alternately, he steps.*

4. *He who steps, he proceeds forward or backward, upward and downward.*

**LXXII.** <note>Paragraph 72</note> Moreover, it is clear that Stairs can be constructed to any height, provided a firm support <note>original: *medium*, meaning the intervening substance or structure</note> is provided that joins the base to the summit. For nothing prevents stairs from being built from the earth to the clouds, except that the intervening medium—the Air, or Smoke rising to that height—is of too weak a consistency; they cannot be carved into, nor are they capable of bearing a weight. But if a mountain, or a cliff, or a tower should rise to that sublime height, nothing prevents Steps from being built into it, or attached to it, by which a man may reach even that point. For whether one chooses to construct them of wood or stone, there is no lack of (iron) tools with which they can be carved out; provided only that one does not shrink from applying labor and wisdom <note>original: *prudentiam*; here implying the practical skill of an architect or craftsman</note>.

->D E L I N E A T I O.<-
<note>original: *Delineatio*; An Outline or Sketch.</note>

...come together: the **Leader**, the **Led**, and the **Leading** itself; and in this process there are again specific points: *From Where*, *To Where*, and *By What Way*. So it is in **Instruction**, where there is the **Teacher**, the **Student**, and the **act of teaching and learning itself**. In this act, there are again specific points: the first is *From Which* <note>original: *A quo*</note>—namely, those principles on which the teacher and student first agree; the second is *To Which* <note>original: *Ad quem*</note>—namely, the conclusions still unknown to the student, but toward which the teacher takes upon himself the labor of leading him; and finally, the *way* <note>original: *via*</note> by which the student is to be led, which the student is likewise ignorant of, but which the Teacher must not be ignorant of. 

Moreover, unless this way is directed toward its proper goal and is so clearly explained that the person being led can progress without stumbling, he will not arrive at the destination. Therefore, wherever there are people who wish to lead and be led, or to teach and be taught, and yet the desired result does not follow, it is certain that specific means are lacking—that is, a well-explained path and steps correctly fitted for raising the mind. I shall demonstrate this through a step-by-step <term>Method</term> <note>Comenius uses "Method" here not just as a technique, but as a structured "road" (from the Greek *meta-hodos*) for the mind to travel.</note>, continuing with several <term>Axioms</term> <note>An Axiom is a self-evident principle that serves as a starting point for reasoning.</note>, as follows:

**Axiom 3. *He who desires to know the truth, rejoices in the truth.***

This is a property of the human heart, and inseparable from it: that if the hope of what it earnestly desires is offered, it rejoices.

**Axiom 4. *He who rejoices in the truth, embraces the truth when it arrives.***

Nor can this be absent from the nature of desire and love: that it pours itself out toward the thing beloved and joyfully awaited, joining itself to that thing, and that thing to itself.

**Axiom 3. *He who desires to know the truth, rejoices in the truth.***

**Axiom 5. *Having embraced the truth, he holds fast to the truth.***

This also is characteristic of love: not to let go of the beloved thing. Hence it is that humans are so tenacious of their opinions—whether they be true or false, provided they are painted with the color of truth <note>original: *veritatis fuco illitæ* — literally "smeared with the rouge of truth." This suggests that even false ideas, if they appear beautiful and true to the holder, are defended with the same passion as actual facts.</note>—that they would even prefer to die rather than cast away the views they have absorbed in the name of Truth.

<page-num>184</page-num>
<header>OF THE TEMPLE OF UNIVERSAL WISDOM <note>original: *Templi Pansophici* — Pansophia is the "all-encompassing wisdom" or "universal knowledge" system proposed by the author, Jan Amos Comenius.</note></header>

It is also most natural, and inseparable from the human mind, that whatever a person believes they truly perceive, they desire others to perceive the same. One considers it an injury to their own mind if someone chooses to think otherwise, as if by that act the other person intended to silently reproach <note>original: *exprobrare* — to upbraid or charge with a fault.</note> them for being in error. From this arises that offense of spirit and silent hatred among those who differ in opinion (especially regarding matters of faith); for the soul considers itself poorly treated and is restless unless it has everyone agreeing <note>original: *adstipulantes* — a legalistic term for those who join in a formal agreement or witness to a truth.</note> in the secure possession of the Truth, with no one contradicting.

**Axiom 7. *Wishing others to hold the truth, one promotes others toward the same.***

In every discrepancy of opinion, we see it happen that people draw others into their own way of thinking by whatever means they can—especially those who believe they understand more than others or who burn with greater zeal. Hence, everything resounds with disputes, while this person labors to win over that one, and one sect labors to win over another, in both Religion and Philosophy, striving to lead others over to their own opinion.

<note>The vocabulary below represents the core conceptual pillars of the author's system.</note>

<summary>This page explains the psychological root of intellectual and religious conflict: the inherent human need for others to validate our own perception of truth. It defines the "Axiom" that people naturally seek to convert others to their viewpoints, leading to the widespread debates found in philosophy and religion.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the logical development of Comenius’s pedagogical psychology. Following the previous page’s discussion on the human desire for consensus, the author now distinguishes between the "Will" (which can be coerced) and the "Intellect" (which can only be taught), arguing that true knowledge cannot be forced by authority but must be revealed through clarity.</meta>

<page-num>185</page-num>
<header>DELINEATION <note>original: *Delineatio* — a sketch or outline of the structure of knowledge.</note></header>
<meta>catchword: equally [æquè]</meta>

**Axiom 8. *One who promotes others toward the truth, promotes them by teaching.***

For other ways of propagating knowledge <term>original: *scientias* — structured fields of knowledge or "sciences"</term>, besides teaching <note>original: *doctrina* — the systematic instruction of a student.</note>, are illegitimate <note>original: *spurii* — literally "spurious" or "bastardized" methods.</note> and useless for the intended purpose: as when someone tries to persuade, order, mandate, or compel another to believe or feel this or that in such-and-such a way. For to truly know or to believe is a matter of understanding. However, understanding is not a work of the **Will**, which is usually bent to want something through persuasion, mandates, threats, or coercion; instead, it is a work of the **Intellect**, which by its own nature is nothing but a **mirror of Things**, receiving into itself whatever you present appropriately and through a clear light. Therefore, offer the truth by teaching it—clearly and appropriately—and the intellect will grasp it.

**Axiom 9. *Many people, however, teach commonly, yet they do not promote others to the place they wish.***

For the world is full of Books and outcries, by which people labor strenuously to persuade others of their own opinions; but with what progress? With very little, certainly, and least worthy of such great contentions. For among so many ranks of those in conflict, if by chance one single person is converted, a hundred others remain fixed in their place and do not allow themselves to be moved from their opinion. And those who are moved,

<vocab>Axiom [Axioma], of the Intellect [Intellectûs], of the Will [Voluntatis], knowledge/science [scientia], teaching/instruction [doctrina], of Books [Librorum]</vocab>

<summary>Comenius argues that because the human intellect acts as a passive "mirror," it cannot be forced to accept truth through threats or authority, but only through clear instruction. He critiques the ineffective, loud, and argumentative nature of the intellectual and religious debates of his time.</summary>
<keywords>Intellect vs Will, Mirror of Things, coercion in education, pedagogical theory, nature of belief, Jan Amos Comenius</keywords>

While the previous page established that knowledge must be taught to the intellect rather than forced upon the will, Comenius now addresses why even well-intentioned teachers often fail to lead others to the truth, resulting in stubbornness and multiplying controversies rather than consensus.</meta>

<page-num>186</page-num>
<header>OF THE TEMPLE OF PANSOPHY <note>original: *Templi Pansophici* — Referring to "Pansophy," Comenius’s concept of universal wisdom or an all-encompassing system of knowledge.</note></header>

they are moved just as easily from the right path to an erroneous one, or from one error to another, as they are from Error to Truth. And what is worse, controversies are not only not resolved, but they are multiplied; and to prevent them from being resolved, they become fixed as minds harden against one another.

***Axiom 10. Therefore, something is necessarily missing from common teaching methods.*** <note>original: *Vulgo igitur docentium* — literally "the common crowd of those teaching."</note>

LXXV. For what is the cause that from such good premises, such monstrous results follow? That everyone is eager for the Truth, yet they do not find it? Or perhaps they have found it (in some lucky course of events) and hold it with certainty, yet are still unable to demonstrate it to others! It is more certain than certainty itself that we deal unskillfully with one another, and that the true way and method of propagating the truth is not yet commonly understood <term>original: *vulgò teneri* — held by the public or commonly practiced</term>. Indeed, those principles which we have already seen were implanted in each person by the good God and are present in all people, are good: To want to see the truth; To want to follow it if one sees it; Likewise to want others to be sharers of the same Truth, and to desire this, and for the sake of that cause to spare no labor—and yet no...

<meta>catchword: -thing [nihil]</meta>

<summary>Comenius argues that despite a universal human desire for truth and divine-given intellect, current teaching methods are so flawed that they only increase disagreement and stubbornness. He concludes that a fundamental "true method" for sharing knowledge is still missing from common practice.</summary>
