<meta>This page continues the argument from page 186 regarding the failure of current educational methods. Having established that the lack of success is not due to a lack of will, Comenius now identifies the missing "instrument" for learning: a logical "Ladder of the Intellect."</meta>

<page-num>187</page-num>
<header>->OUTLINE.<-</header>

...to profit nothing? Given a goal, and given those who seriously desire that goal, and yet the goal is not reached, it is an infallible sign that either there is a lack of means, or the use of the means that are available is not understood—as is the case here. Specifically, the true **Ladder of the Human Intellect** <note>original: *Scala Intellectûs* — A central metaphor in Comenius's philosophy, suggesting that knowledge should be gained through a step-by-step progression from simple to complex truths.</note>, through which leaders and those being led can certainly and safely ascend and descend, is still missing. This is what needed to be shown.

LXXVI. *For if such a Ladder were built, every human mind would ascend to the very summit of things; this must now be demonstrated by a ladder of several Axioms.* <note>original: *Axiomatum* — In this context, these are self-evident principles or starting points for a logical argument.</note>

1. *Every human has a Mind.*
Indeed, one destitute of a mind would not be a Human, but a brute beast.

2. *Whoever has a mind, thinks.*
For just as it is the nature of the Eyes to see, the Ear to hear, the Tongues to taste, and the Nerves to touch; so thinking is the proper and essential function of the Mind.

3. *Whoever thinks, passes from one thing to another.*
For all thought is a certain movement of the mind, by which it goes from one thing to another,

<meta>catchword: and</meta>

<summary>Comenius argues that the failure to reach universal truth is due to the lack of a "Ladder of the Intellect" and begins a logical proof (via axioms) that all humans possess the innate capacity for thought and progression.</summary>
<keywords>Ladder of the Intellect, Axioms, Human Mind, Thought, Educational Method, Logic</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the logical proof begun on page 187, where Comenius is establishing the "Axioms" of human thought to prove that anyone can reach the summit of knowledge if provided with the right "Ladder." <page-num>188</page-num>
<header>OF THE PANSOPHIC TEMPLE</header> <note>original: *Templi Pansophici* — Comenius's vision of a structured, universal system of knowledge, often visualized as a physical building or temple.</note>

and from that again, one crosses over into something else. From which it is also called the **Discourse of the mind** <term>Discourse of the mind: *Discursus mentis* — the logical process where the mind moves sequentially from one idea to another, rather than perceiving everything at once.</term>. For just as the Eye, when it has many things to look at, cannot see those many things all at once and at the same time, but rather one after another; so the Mind, whatever it thinks, thinks *something*, *about something*, *through something*, one thing after another.

4. *He who passes from thing to thing, passes step by step.* <note>original: *gradatim transit* — This reinforces the "Ladder" metaphor mentioned on the previous page.</note>

For thoughts are linked together like a chain, in which one link pulls the next. Even when something comes to mind unexpectedly, appearing quite remote from the previous thought, it nevertheless arises from some occasion; whether that arises from within, from some known thing, or from without, from some object perceived by the senses. Just as in the physical world there is no vacuum or gap, so there is none in the **Intellect**; and just as one cannot walk except from the nearest point (whether by degree, step, or footprint) to the next, so it is impossible to arrive at any thing through thought, except from some other thing.

5. *He who passes step by step, can go as far as the Steps go.*

<summary>Comenius explains that human thought is a sequential process ("discourse") rather than an instantaneous one. He compares the mind's progression to a chain or a physical walk, arguing that knowledge is built incrementally through a series of connected steps.</summary>

<page-num>189</page-num>
<header>A SKETCH.</header> <note>original: *DELINEATIO* — A preliminary outline or draft of the proposed system.</note>

Therefore every Human can ascend in Mind, as far as he sees the **Steps of Things** before him. Which was the thing to be demonstrated. <note>original: *Quod demonstrandum fuit* — the Latin equivalent of the mathematical Q.E.D., marking the end of a logical proof.</note>

LXXVII. *Now it must be demonstrated that a **Ladder of the human Intellect** can be constructed. However, when I say "constructed," I do not mean some arbitrary labor, as is common when ladders are prepared for physical heights, in this way or that, as it pleases the craftsman: who takes the position, structure, and measure of the Steps from himself, or from those for whose uses he prepares them. This **Mental Ladder**, because it will be only an image of the **Ladder of Things** <term>Ladder of Things: *Rerum scala* — the objective, hierarchical order of the universe created by God, often called the "Great Chain of Being."</term>, woven by divine craftsmanship, will not be subject to our whim. Things themselves here will give the measure both to themselves and to the Intellect. They err dangerously who think it sufficient for Things to be considered as they appear to us, and not as they are in themselves: establishing our Senses as the norm for Things, rather than Things for the senses. The exact opposite must be done, and Things (in which **Truth** resides as if in its own Palace) must be established as the norm, both for themselves and for us, if we ever wish to struggle out from the tumult and collisions of opinions toward harmony, so that just as that Thing*

<summary>Comenius concludes that the human mind can ascend to any height if the steps of reality are visible, and argues that a true system of knowledge must be based on the objective "Ladder of Things" created by God, rather than on subjective human perception.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument from page 189, emphasizing that the human mind must conform to the objective reality of "Things" rather than imposing its own ideas. It transitions into Section LXXVIII, which begins a formal logical proof for why an intellectual ladder can be built based on the structure of the universe.</meta>

<page-num>190</page-num>
->TEMPLE OF PANSOPHY<-
<note>original: *TEMPLI PANSOPHICI* — "Pansophy" refers to "all-wisdom," a 17th-century movement led by Jan Amos Comenius to organize all human knowledge into a single, divine system.</note>

connects within itself, so too let it connect our **Mind** regarding **Things**. Our **Senses**, **imagination** <term>imagination: *phantasia* — in historical psychology, this was the faculty that received and stored images from the five senses.</term>, the **Mind** itself, or **Reason**, have nothing that they contribute to or impose upon Things. Rather, Things—by presenting themselves to the senses, imagination, and mind—form true images of themselves when they are perceived correctly <note>original: *perceptæ legitimè* — "legitimately perceived," meaning through a proper, disciplined method of observation.</note>; but they form distorted images when perceived incorrectly. Therefore, nothing at all should be attributed to Sense or the Mind on its own; instead, they must be bound most tightly to **Things**, unless we wish to be endlessly and boundlessly deceived and toyed with.

LXXVIII. ***Therefore, it is evident that the ladder of the human Intellect can be constructed from the ladder of Things: because all things are arranged according to steps, both the Universe itself as a whole, and each individual thing separately.***
For:
1. *Whatever exists is finite.*
(For we are not dealing now with the infinite, which is one: God.)
2. *Whatever is finite has limits in its essence.*
3. *Whatever has limits in its essence has within it something first and something last, something lowest and something highest.*
4. *Where, however, there is a first and a last...*

<meta>catchword: mum, (the first syllable of the next page's first word, *postremum*)</meta>

It begins a logical proof that because the universe is finite and hierarchical, the human mind can systematically climb from the lowest to the highest truths.</summary>

*...first, lowest and highest; there also is before and after, lower and higher.*

*5. Where there is before and after, lower and higher, there are steps <note>original: *gradus* — also translated as degrees or stages in a hierarchy.</note>.*

*6. But where there are steps, there is distinction; and where there is distinction, there is clarity <note>original: *claritudo*</note>.*

Therefore, whatever exists has distinct and clear steps within its own essence; when these are correctly observed, they lead the mind to all that is first and last, lowest and highest, in every thing.

LXXIX. ***We trust that such a graduated structure, which may lead the Mind toward all things, is inherent in Pansophy,*** both in the entire complex of Things and in any individual thing separately. For as for the whole, Pansophy has a **firm and immovable base, or a threefold ray: Necessity, Possibility, and Ease** <note>original: *facilitatem* — in Comenius’s philosophy, this refers to the idea that knowledge should be easily graspable or manageable when following the order of nature.</note>; these are solidly established in the book of **Preliminaries** <term>Preliminaries: *Præcognitorum* — the introductory principles or "pre-knowledge" required before embarking on the full study of universal wisdom.</term>. It also has a splendid summit, equally firm: **Blessedness, or the rest of Desires**; which the final book of Pansophy will demonstrate in the delights of the Mind illuminated within itself, in the improvement for the better of all human affairs, and in the illustrious propagation of the glory of God.

<header>THE TEMPLE OF PANSOPHY</header>

...we [can]. It also possesses a firm middle, suited to join the summit to the base and to support solid steps: on God's part, a most true Revelation, made through **Works**, **Words**, and **internal Dictates** (which are **innate notions** <note>original: *Notiones innatæ* — fundamental ideas or principles that Comenius believed were "carved" into the human soul at birth by God.</note>, carved into the mind of every human); on our part, **Sense**, **Reason**, and the **Faith** to be applied to God's testimonies. From this triple-paired shaft <note>original: *ter bino veluti scapo* — Comenius envisions the support of his system like a dual-pillared frame: three sources from God (Nature, Scripture, Mind) meeting three faculties in Man (Senses, Intellect, Belief).</note>, as it were, erected in parallel and connected everywhere, that most solid **Ladder of Things** is built: a ladder, I say, partitioned <note>original: *fracta*</note>, *fivefold*, rising through five different stories.

For the first and lowest is the *Metaphysical Ladder*; it elevates the mind through all generalities, known through themselves, as far as it can. The second, in the second region of *Natural things*, uncovers the divine craftsmanship in Things, through all the steps of Creatures. *The third* teaches one to contemplate the works of *Man, the supreme Creature*, and shows how far the power of the Human Intellect <term>Intellect: *Ingenii* — the innate talent, wit, or creative power of the human mind.</term> may ascend regarding created things. *The fourth* explains the communication of Man with God Himself, his Creator, and through this, the mysteries of salvation and damnation.

### ->SKETCH<-

The fifth step then ascends to the contemplation of the uncreated God himself, dwelling within his own eternity. From this height, there is no further place to ascend; yet, one finds a way to descend back: toward the proper use of the Creator, of Creatures, and of oneself. This ensures that whatever remains of a person’s life is spent in wise thoughts and actions, until at last, through the gate of death, a safe, secure, and joyful passage is made into blessed eternity.

**LXXX.** *These things concern the stepped structure of all **Pansophy** <term>Pansophy: From the Greek "pan-sophia" (all-wisdom). <page-num>194</page-num>
## ->OF THE PANSOPHIC TEMPLE<-

...thrusting them toward the summit, without the truth of it having been gradually demonstrated. <note>original: *obtrudit, veritate ejus sensim non commonstratâ* — this completes the thought from the previous page about the folly of expecting a student to "leap" to the top of a tower of knowledge without using the stairs.</note>

**LXXXI.** *Therefore, the purpose of this stepped **Method** <term>Method: *Methodus* — in the 17th century, this referred to a systematic, logical arrangement of ideas designed to make learning as efficient and natural as possible.</term> will be: to easily teach the ignorant, and to powerfully unteach those in error.* The former will be unable to miss the light of truth and will rejoice in it; the latter will be unable to ignore their own mistakes, and will be turned toward a love for better and truer things. However, it must be noted that teaching the ignorant is a single and simple task, whereas a double effort is required to teach those who are in error. For the ignorant, because they know neither the true nor the false, are receptive to either; they can be taught only truths, as they have no need to be "untaught" falsehoods. Those in error, however, are already preoccupied with false opinions. They must first be untaught their error so that they might become both hungry for and capable of receiving the Truth, and only then can they finally be taught. As we have already seen, this is not achieved without a gradual progression. Just as an ignorant person cannot be moved to the peak of clear knowledge without steps—neither by being thrown nor by a single leap—so too an erring person cannot be knocked down from the height of their error, because of the tenacity of their opinions,

<meta>catchword: which [quæ]</meta>

<summary>Comenius explains that his stepped method serves two purposes: guiding the ignorant to truth and correcting those with false beliefs. He emphasizes that correcting error is a more difficult "double task" because false opinions must be dismantled before new knowledge can be acquired.</summary>
<keywords>pedagogy, educational method, correcting error, logic, Pansophy, truth, step-by-step learning</keywords>

--- Page 205 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from page 194 concerning the "double effort" required to correct those who hold false opinions. It explains that because the mind naturally loves truth, it will cling to even a false "shadow" of truth until it is shown a better path through logical "stepping down."</meta>

<page-num>195</page-num>

# ->OUTLINE<-

...heights <note>Continuing from the word "heights" on the previous page, referring to the "height of false opinions."</note> which, wherever they have once taken root, cling stubbornly, even until death, unless they are wisely removed. For the human mind (by a natural instinct) pays such reverence to **Truth** that it constantly adores even its shadow, and does not allow itself to be torn away from it unless it is taught truer things. Therefore, so that one may be taught truer things, there is first a need for a certain "stepping down"—that is, a **reduction to absurdity** <term>Reduction to absurdity: *deductio ad absurdum* — a logical method that proves a statement is false by showing that it leads to an impossible or ridiculous conclusion.</term>—so that the theorist himself may clearly recognize that his own opinion conflicts with the principles of knowledge: **Sense**, **Reason**, and **Faith**. 

For then he will be considered to have been led down from the peak of **Error** to the level ground—namely, to the common field of principles. From there, he will not unwillingly allow himself to be led, through better and firmer logical consequences, to the peaks of better and firmer opinions; and he will rejoice that he has been led to a place where he feels he is better off than before.

**LXXXII.** This must also be noted: where the gap between the **teacher** and the **learner** (or rather, the one who contradicts) can be filled by a single intermediate step, there is no need for many; and that the demonstration...

# ->OF THE PANSOPHIC TEMPLE<-
<note>original: *Templi Pansophici*. "Pansophy" was a 17th-century concept of universal wisdom that integrated all knowledge into a single, harmonious system.</note>

...can be accomplished by a single **Demonstrative** Syllogism <term>Syllogism: A logical argument where a conclusion is drawn from two premises.</term> <note>original: *Syllogismo Apodictico*</note>, as was done by us in Aphorisms 67 and 68. However, where the distance is greater, it requires more intermediate steps, and a chain must be forged from them—a **Demonstrative** Sorites <term>Sorites: A form of argument consisting of a series of logical statements where the end of one becomes the start of the next.</term> <note>original: *Soritem Apodicticum*</note>, as we did in Aphorisms 71, 74, 76, and 78. This method of teaching presents a **Ladder** <note>original: *Scalam*</note> to the eye, and the truth of the chained consequence is revealed more clearly than in disconnected heaps of **Preliminary Arguments** <term>Prosyllogism: A syllogism whose conclusion serves as a premise for a following syllogism.</term> <note>original: *Prosyllogismorum*</note>. ->(1) He who has feet in good health is able to walk.<-
->(2) He who walks moves his feet alternately.<-
->(3) He who moves his feet alternately is stepping forward.<-
->(4) He who steps forward strives upward, etc.<-
->(5) He who strives upward ascends through degrees.<-
->(6) He who ascends through degrees is able to climb as far as the degrees lead.<-

LXXXIII. *In order for this **Sorites** <term>Sorites: A logical argument consisting of a series of statements where the end of one becomes the subject of the next, forming a chain.</term> to be truly **Demonstrative** <note>original: *Apodicticus*; refers to a proof that is necessarily true and certain.</note>, and a firm chain of Truth, the following must be observed.* (1) Just as in a **Chain** <note>original: *Catena*</note> one link takes up the next; so here the **propositions** <term>Propositions: Formal statements that express a judgment or density.</term> cohere, with the **predicate** <term>Predicate: The part of a sentence or clause containing a verb and stating something about the subject.</term> of the preceding proposition always being taken up as the **subject** of the following one. (2) Just as in a Chain a link is essentially fastened to another link so that it coheres inseparably: so here let every predicate announce something essential about its subject, so that it may be true without exception. (3) Just as in a chain a link is connected to a link until the measure of length has been completed: so in a **Sorites** let step be joined to step, until one reaches that point which brings an end to the controversy. (4) Finally, as in a Chain each link is separately made solid in itself so that it cannot be loosened (otherwise, if even one link is broken, the chain is ruptured): so in the individual steps...

<page-num>198</page-num>
<header>THE PANSOPHIC TEMPLE</header>
<note>original: *Templi Pansophici*. Pansophy refers to "universal wisdom," a 17th-century ideal of organizing all human knowledge into a single coherent system.</note>

One must dwell upon the **Sorites** <term>Sorites: A logical chain where the conclusion of one statement becomes the subject of the next.</term> (whenever someone is led by this ladder) until its **Truth** is established and not a shred of doubt remains. Only then must one proceed.

LXXXIV. But enough. An end must be placed upon our deliberations and sketches, so that we may rather come to the **Work** itself. For whatever we say, that proverb can always be thrown against us: *Where there is need for deeds, words do not suffice.* <note>original: *Ubi factis opus est, verba non sufficiunt.*</note> We, however, promise to give not words, but the work—even if it is not with that perfection which we conceive in our **Idea**. <note>The author uses "Idea" in the philosophical sense of a perfect mental blueprint or archetype.</note> We protest so often that this task belongs not to one man, nor to one Age; and prudent evaluators of things understand for themselves that *Nature herself willed nothing great to be accomplished quickly, and she set difficulty before every most beautiful work; she also made this a law of birth: that larger animals are contained longer in the wombs of their parents.* <note>The author is quoting a common Stoic sentiment regarding the slow maturation of great things.</note> Why then should this great birth (a product not of our own talent, but of the century) not require both a proper time and many good, faithful midwives? The Temple of Solomon was not perfected by one...

<summary>The author argues that while logical rigor is essential, theoretical planning must eventually give way to action, even if the resulting work is imperfect. He emphasizes that the "Pansophic Temple" is a generational project requiring collective effort, comparing its slow development to the long gestation of large animals in nature.</summary>
<keywords>Sorites, Pansophy, logic vs. action, Temple of Solomon, natural law, collective intellectual labor, gestation metaphor</keywords>

<header>OUTLINE.</header>
<note>original: *Delineatio*. This refers to the outline or "sketch" of the author's plan for universal knowledge.</note>

...[constructed] by a master builder: there, the **Carpenters**, **Bronze-workers**, **Stonemasons**, **Goldsmiths**, **Jewelers**, **Sculptors**, and **Painters** <note>original: *Fabri lignarii, Ærarii, Lapicidæ, Aurifabri, Gemmarii, Sculptores, Pictores*</note> contributed their labors. In the same way, **Philosophers**, **Theologians**, **Politicians**, **Doctors**, and all **Talented** people have their own tasks to perform here. Let them, therefore, hasten to this work; let them not refuse to build up this **Common Treasure** with their shared resources, and to increase it further through collective care (for this is, indeed, a common undertaking).

LXXXV. *Not only those of the present day, but also those who will come after us, are invited to pursue this same goal with the same zeal.* For the perfection of this **Work**, although always growing through constant progress, is nevertheless never expected to reach its absolute peak in this life. Just as numbers have a beginning in **Unity** <note>original: *unitatem*. In early modern mathematics, "one" was often considered the source or "principle" of numbers rather than a number itself.</note> but find no end as they proceed toward infinity, so the **Wisdom of God** communicated to us rises from small beginnings. By diffusing itself through infinite things, it can never be entirely exhausted. As the son of Sirach <term>Syracides: Refers to Jesus ben Sirach, the author of the biblical book of Ecclesiasticus (or Sirach).</term> observed when he said: *The first man did not know her perfectly, and likewise the last shall not fully search her out* (chapter 24, verse 31). <note>The author is quoting Sirach 24:28 in modern numbering, comparing the depth of Wisdom to a great river or the sea.</note>

<sig>I 4</sig>
<meta>catchword: Ha-</meta>

<summary>This page argues that the "Pansophic Temple" of knowledge requires the collaborative effort of all specialists and future generations, noting that divine wisdom is an infinite pursuit that can never be fully exhausted.</summary>

<header>OUTLINE OF THE PANSOPHIC TEMPLE</header>
<sig>4</sig>
<meta>catchword: FROM</meta>

Industrious souls will therefore have much to continually supply, amend, perfect, and polish, to the praise of their own names among posterity, yet with no detriment to the things themselves. For **Truth** is a solid thing; the more it is weighed and shaken <note>original: *libratur & vibratur*. This suggests a process of testing or balancing like a scale.</note>, the more purely it shines. There is no room for deceptions where everything is filled with characters, numbers, measurements, scales, and touchstones <note>original: *lydiorum lapidum*. A "Lydian stone" or touchstone was a dark rock used to test the purity of gold and silver by the color of the streak they left behind.</note>; nor is anything admitted unless it has withstood a severe and complete examination in every respect.

Make us walk in Your **Truth**, and teach us: for You are the God of our salvation; for You we wait.*

# ON THE SCENOGRAPHY <note>original: *Scenographia*. In the architectural terminology of the time, this refers to a perspective drawing that shows a building in three dimensions, following the ground plan and the elevation.</note>
## OF THE PANSOPHIC WORK

-> NOTICE. <-

After we have presented the *Ichnographic <note>original: *Ichnographia*. An architectural floor plan.</note> and Orthographic <note>original: *Orthographia*. An elevation drawing showing the vertical side of a building.</note> drawing* of the Pansophic Temple, I easily foresee that someone might say what Seneca said to his friend Lucilius: *I do not demand more from you than what you were attempting. Your foundations have occupied much space. Only accomplish as much as you have attempted; and treat those things which you have carried with you in your mind* <note>original: Seneca, *Epistulae Morales*, 31. Seneca is urging his friend to complete the great spiritual and intellectual goals he has set for himself.</note> (Epistle 31.) To these people, if I respond with the words of Ludovicus Vives <note>Juan Luis Vives (1493–1540) was a Spanish humanist and educator who argued for the reform of learning.</note>: *Many things cannot be brought forth and polished at the same time: something long sought after CANNOT BE DISCOVERED AND REFINED BY THE SAME PERSON: life is not sufficient for so many things, nor is the force of the human mind, being brief and weak.* And shortly thereafter: *I forgive the fact that one man* (he is speaking of Aristotle) *did not digest and refine so many, so varied, and such difficult things as he had unearthed: one man cannot perform what a hundred could scarcely provide.* (Book 1, *On the Causes of the Corruption of the Arts*.) I fear that this might not satisfy those who, in whatever way...

<page-num>202</page-num>
<header>ON THE SCENOGRAPHY <note>original: *De Scenographia*. In the architectural language of the era, this refers to a perspective drawing that provides a three-dimensional view of a building.</note></header>

...they turn promises into the calculation of a debt. <note>The author is complaining that when a scholar mentions a great idea, the public immediately demands it as a finished product they are "owed."</note> Lord Verulam <note>original: *Verulamius*. This refers to Francis Bacon (1561–1626), the English philosopher and statesman who advocated for the reform of knowledge through inductive reasoning.</note> is certainly lashed by some (even those who seem to themselves to be judicious) because he had promised a **Great Restoration of the Arts** <note>original: *Instauratio Magna*. This was Bacon's ambitious, unfinished plan to reorganize all human knowledge and scientific method.</note> and had not fulfilled it; which, however, he did not promise from himself alone, nor did he ever presume that. For time and again he protests that he is giving an **Organon** <note>original: *Organon*. From the Greek for "instrument" or "tool." Bacon's *Novum Organum* was intended as a new tool for logic and science to replace the old Aristotelian methods.</note> for the restoration of Philosophy; [he argues] that the restored Philosophy itself cannot be produced by the talent or industry of a single man or age, but by the combined and reinforced work of many, for as long as the labor is required. 

In which manner we also protest just as many times: that something perfectly finished should not be expected from us in that which we advise must be done, but only the opportunity and certain first principles of so great a Work. This is because it is better at some point to give a beginning—of whatever kind—to the things desired, than to see something as a mere idea and never attempt it. It appears, however, that the common crowd of men are imitators, and prefer to feed on what is ready-made and rest satisfied with things already acquired, rather than to track and hunt down the still hidden and fleeting order and truth of things.

<summary>The author defends the unfinished nature of his work by comparing it to Francis Bacon's Great Restoration, arguing that massive intellectual projects require the cooperation of many people over many generations.</summary>

**Julius Pollux** <note>A Greek grammarian and sophist of the 2nd century AD.</note> once excused the slower elaboration of his work, the *Onomasticon* <term>Onomasticon: A dictionary organized by topics rather than alphabetically, providing synonyms and related terms for various subjects.</term>, also by a lack of Collaborators; though he himself did not care to employ any. For he wrote thus to the **Emperor Commodus** in the preface to the seventh Book: *I could employ no collaborator for this work; for I have no one whom I truly trust, and my own judgment must always be present here.*

I would gladly employ them, if those who were capable were willing to be employed. But they pretend that they do not wish to "disturb the lines" <note>A classical idiom meaning to interfere with or alter a plan already in progress.</note>; that the structure must be finished by us just as it was begun: they claim their own industry will not be lacking later, when it is time to fill it out. A reference to a work that seeks to find a "universal harmony" or total integration of all branches of knowledge.</note> at the same time...*

### OF SCENOGRAPHY
<note>original: *De Scenographia*. In this context, "scenography" refers to the structural outline, layout, or conceptual "stage-setting" of the author's grand intellectual project.</note>

<meta>catchword: men- [The Latin text ends with "ali-", and the catchword indicates the next page begins with "men-", completing the word "alimentum" or nourishment.]</meta>

*Among these three things, the **prudence and soundness of Counsel** deservedly holds the first place; that is, the **Showing and Delineation of the right and easy Way** for accomplishing the matter which is proposed. For a **lame man** (as the saying goes) **on the right path outstrips a runner off the path.** <note>original: *Claudus... in via, antevertit Cursorem extra viam.* This was a favorite proverb of Francis Bacon, used to argue that a good scientific method allows even an ordinary mind to achieve more than a brilliant mind working without a system.</note> <insert>By which words, certainly, the Outlines of our **Pansophic Work**, if they are sound, are approved.</insert>* <note>Pansophic: relating to *Pansophia*, a 17th-century movement led by Jan Amos Comenius to organize all human knowledge into a single, cohesive system for the improvement of the world.</note> *Yet, a little later, he <note>Francis Bacon</note> desires that **collective labors** and **generous rewards** be established by public authority for those who are occupied with the **Universal foundations of Wisdom**, and the very roots themselves; in these words:*

> *“Among so many **Colleges of Europe**, most nobly founded, I marvel that all of them are destined for certain **Professions**; none dedicated to the free and **UNIVERSAL STUDIES of the Arts and Sciences**. For if anyone judges that all **Learning** should be referred to **Use and Action**, he is rightly wise: nevertheless, it is easy in that way to fall into that error which the **ancient fable** <note>This refers to the fable of "The Belly and the Members," often attributed to Aesop, which teaches that every part of a system—even the parts that seem "idle"—is necessary for the survival of the whole.</note> touches upon: in which the other **members of the Body** brought a lawsuit against the **Stomach**, because it provided neither motion, like the **Limbs**; nor sense, like the **Head**; although meanwhile it [nourished the rest]...”*

<summary>The author uses a famous proverb and a quote from Francis Bacon to argue that a correct method is more important than speed, and that society must fund "universal" research centers rather than just vocational schools.</summary>

*...nourishment, cooked and prepared, that the **Stomach** might distribute into the rest of the **Body**. <note>original: "mentum coctum atque confectum Ventriculus ille in reliquum Corpus divideret." This completes the sentence from the previous page, where Bacon compares universal philosophy to the stomach—it may not be a "limb" that does work, but it provides the nutrients for all limbs to function.</note> In exactly the same way, anyone who considers the study devoted to **universal Contemplations** to be empty and idle does not notice that from them, the sap and strength are supplied to individual **Professions** and **Arts**. And I am certainly persuaded that this very thing has been no small cause why the happier progress of **Learning** has been delayed until now: that work has been applied to these fundamental **Sciences** only in passing, nor have fuller draughts been drunk from them. For if you desire a **Tree** to become more fruitful than usual, you will think in vain about treating the **Branches**: the **Earth** itself around the **Root** must be worked, and broader soil applied; or you will achieve nothing, etc.* <note>The author uses Bacon’s famous "root and branch" metaphor to argue that fixing specific problems in law, medicine, or theology is useless unless the "root"—the universal principles of all knowledge—is first nourished.</note> *Afterwards: It is of primary importance that such (**Investigators of Things**) <note>original: *Indagatores Rerum*. In the 17th century, this referred to what we would now call scientific researchers or empirical philosophers.</note> be chosen from the best and most well-equipped: as people whose work is employed not for transitory use, but to provide a progeny of **Science** for ages to come. This cannot be done unless such rewards and conditions are established by which every most eminent person in that art can be fully satisfied:*

<page-num>210</page-num>
# ON SCENOGRAPHY
<meta>The header "De Scenographia" (On Scenography) likely refers to the larger chapter or section this page belongs to, though the text continues a philosophical argument about the funding of science.</meta>

...be able: so that at last it may not be a burden for him to spend his life in that same duty, nor should he have to worry about practical business <note>original: "practicam cogitet". Bacon argues that a dedicated researcher should be financially supported so they don't have to seek a second job or pursue science only for profit.</note>, etc. Otherwise, if sufficiently large and splendid rewards are not established for the **Fathers of the Sciences**, that famous saying will come true: *And weak children repeat the fasts of their Fathers.* <note>original: "Et Patrum invalidi referunt jejunia Nati." This is a poetic allusion suggesting that if the "father" sciences (pure research) are starved of funding, the "children" (applied sciences) will be stunted.</note>

After inserting some other remarks, he adds: Indeed, the generosity of men has not entirely ceased; for we see not only **Books** (and **Libraries**) but **Spheres**, **Globes**, **Astrolabes**, **Maps**, and other similar things provided as aids for **Astronomy** and **Cosmography**, and offered for study. We also see some places dedicated to the study of **Medicine** that have **Gardens** for the inspection and knowledge of every kind of **Simples** <term>medicinal herbs or plants used in their natural state</term>; nor are they lacking the use of dead **Bodies** for **Anatomical** observations. 

But these things concern only a few fields. In general, let it be held for certain that great progress in unearthing the secrets of nature can hardly be made unless expenses are abundantly supplied for **experiments**, whether they be of **Vulcan** <note>In the 17th century, "Vulcan" (the god of fire) was a common metaphor for chemistry and metallurgy, which required furnaces.</note> or of **Daedalus** <note>The mythical inventor "Daedalus" represents the mechanical arts, engineering, and the creation of complex machinery.</note>. And therefore, just as it is permitted for the **Secretaries** and **Emissaries** of **Princes** to submit accounts of their expenses for their diligent efforts in exploring...

<page-num>211</page-num>

### ADMONITION ON UNIVERSAL WISDOM
<note>original: "PANSOPH. ADMONITIO." Pansophy refers to the "All-Wisdom" or the pursuit of a unified system of all knowledge.</note>

...and in bringing to light new discoveries and state secrets; likewise, the **explorers** and **observers of Nature** must be reimbursed for their expenses. Otherwise, we shall never become better informed about many things most worthy of being known. For if **Alexander** <note>Alexander the Great, who was famously a pupil of Aristotle.</note> provided a vast sum of money to **Aristotle**, with which he might hire hunters, fowlers, fishermen, and others, so that he could approach the writing of his *History of Animals* <note>original: "Historia Animalium," one of Aristotle's major texts on biology.</note> more fully equipped: surely something greater is owed to those who do not merely wander through the woods of Nature, but who open a way for themselves (and for others) through the **Labyrinths of the Arts**. Thus far he <note>The author concludes his lengthy excerpt from Francis Bacon.</note>; and I recommend that his words be weighed one by one by those whom God has commanded to excel, and who—placed as if upon a watchtower—have been ordered to keep watch for the public good.

As for me, I shall gladly do whatever God wishes to be done through my own meager abilities. As soon as I am permitted to return to these studies, I shall prepare a **Skeleton of Universal Wisdom** <term>Sceleton Pansophiæ: an outline or structural framework of all-encompassing knowledge</term> and submit it to public judgment. This is so that either this structure may remain (if nothing better is found), or it may provide an opportunity to persistently seek out even better things—until the best...

->which<-

<summary>The author finishes quoting Francis Bacon's argument that scientific researchers require significant funding—just as Alexander the Great funded Aristotle—and then pledges to publish an outline of "Pansophy" for public critique.</summary>
<keywords>Pansophy, Francis Bacon, Aristotle, Alexander the Great, scientific funding, research, public good, Labyrinth of Arts</keywords>

--- Page 222 ---
<meta>This page concludes the "Admonition on Universal Wisdom" (Pansophy). It completes the citation from Francis Bacon's "The Advancement of Learning" (De Augmentis Scientiarum) and offers the author's final reflection on the feasibility of such a monumental task, ending with a date of September 10, 1643.</meta>

<page-num>212</page-num>
<header>ON THE SCENE OF THE ADMONITION ON UNIVERSAL WISDOM</header>

...until that which is the very irrefutable power and light of Order and Truth is found. 

Certainly, so that these efforts of ours may serve as a testimony, *all things should be considered possible and achievable*—allow me to close with the words of the **Great Verulam** <note>Francis Bacon, also known as Lord Verulam (1561–1626), an English philosopher and statesman often credited with developing the scientific method.</note>, with which he concludes his aforementioned Preface—*whatever can be completed by some, though not by anyone; and what can be done by many together, though not by one alone; and what can be done in the succession of ages, though not in the same lifetime; and finally, what can be done by public care and expense, though not by the wealth and industry of individuals.*

If, however, there is anyone who prefers to take up that saying of **Solomon** <note>Reference to Proverbs 26:13: "The sluggard says, 'There is a lion in the road, a fierce lion roaming the streets!'"</note>: *The sluggard says, "There is a lion in the path"*; rather than that of **Virgil** <note>A famous line from the Aeneid (5.231), "Possunt, quia posse videntur."</note>: *They can, because they think they can*; it will be enough for me if my labors are held *only among the wishes or desires of a better sort.* For just as one must not be entirely unskilled in a matter to pose a question appropriately; so too one should not seem lacking in sense who has desired things that are by no means absurd.

Farewell, be favorable, and pray to God for us, most kind Reader. Written in my retreat, September 10, in the year 1643.

->THE END<-

<summary>The author concludes his call for a universal system of knowledge by quoting Francis Bacon on the necessity of collective, long-term, and publicly funded effort. He appeals to the reader's ambition rather than laziness, offering his work as a noble aspiration for future scholars.</summary>
